Introduction

The head and neck comprise a compact arrangement of vital structures—airway, digestive tract, neurovascular bundles, salivary glands, lymph nodes and an elaborate system of fascial planes. Knowledge of the layered anatomy and functional relationships is essential for understanding the patterns of soft tissue swelling, the routes of infection spread, mechanisms of airway compromise and dysphagia, and for planning safe endoscopic, percutaneous and surgical interventions. This chapter reviews the superficial and deep cervical fascia and the key deep compartments of the neck; the musculature that influences hyoid position and pharyngeal dynamics; the detailed anatomy of the pharynx, larynx and upper airway; the salivary glands and their ducts; neurovascular elements that supply sensation and motor control; the lymphatic drainage patterns that determine nodal metastasis and infection routes; and finally the anatomy most relevant to airway management, endoscopy and surgical access.

# Superficial and deep cervical fascia and compartments (pretracheal, retropharyngeal, parapharyngeal spaces)

The fascial layers of the neck create compartments that both constrain and guide the spread of infection, hemorrhage and neoplastic processes. The superficially visible platysma muscle lies within the superficial cervical fascia, which is contiguous with superficial fascia elsewhere and contains variable amounts of fat, cutaneous nerves, and the external jugular vein system. Deep to the superficial layer lies the investing layer of the deep cervical fascia, which encircles the neck and splits to enclose the sternocleidomastoid and trapezius muscles; this layer forms the roof over several potential spaces and is an important surgical landmark.

The deeper fascia traditionally is described as consisting of three primary layers: the investing layer, the pretracheal (visceral) fascia and the prevertebral fascia. The pretracheal fascia invests the visceral compartment—trachea, thyroid gland, larynx and esophagus—and extends into the thorax, where it blends with the fibrous pericardium. The prevertebral fascia invests the vertebral column and the deep muscles of the neck and continues laterally as the axillary sheath. Between the pretracheal and prevertebral layers lies the retropharyngeal space, which is clinically important because it provides a potential route for infections from the pharynx to descend into the mediastinum. The so-called "danger space" lies posterior to the retropharyngeal space, between the alar layer and the prevertebral fascia, and communicates inferiorly with the posterior mediastinum—allowing pathogens and inflammatory material to spread from the pharyngeal region down to the diaphragm.

Anatomic compartments of special clinical relevance include the parapharyngeal space, the retropharyngeal space and the pretracheal (or visceral) compartment. The parapharyngeal space is a potential inverted pyramid lateral to the pharynx; superiorly it reaches the skull base and inferiorly it tapers toward the hyoid bone. The parapharyngeal space is subdivided by the styloid process and associated muscles/tendons into prestyloid and poststyloid compartments; the poststyloid portion contains the carotid sheath and lower cranial nerves, so lesions here may present with neurovascular signs. Deep neck infections originating in the tonsil, teeth, or salivary glands often track into these spaces. The retropharyngeal space lies posterior to the pharynx and anterior to the alar fascia; it contains lymph nodes (the nodes of Rouvière) in children and is a common site of retropharyngeal abscess, which can cause bulging of the posterior pharyngeal wall and airway compromise. The pretracheal space surrounds the thyroid and upper trachea; swelling here presents anteriorly as midline or paramedian neck masses and may extend into the superior mediastinum.

From a pathophysiologic standpoint, the presence of these confined fascial spaces explains why infections often produce focal collections and localized signs (e.g., bulging lateral pharyngeal wall with parapharyngeal abscess), while breaches of a fascial layer or tracking into a communicating space produce rapid, sometimes dramatic spread (e.g., descending mediastinitis from a retropharyngeal infection). Clinically the recognition of which fascial space is involved guides imaging selection, needle vs open drainage, and urgency of airway control.

# Strap muscles, suprahyoid and infrahyoid muscles, and their relationships to neck soft tissue swelling

The strap (infrahyoid) and suprahyoid muscle groups are central to the mechanics of swallowing, airway protection, phonation and the positional relationships of neck soft tissues. The infrahyoid (strap) muscles—sternohyoid, sternothyroid, thyrohyoid and omohyoid—span from the sternum, scapula and thyroid cartilage to the hyoid bone; they act primarily to depress the hyoid and larynx following swallowing. The anterior compartment formed by these muscles, covered by the investing fascia, lies superficial to the pretracheal fascia and the thyroid gland. Edema, hematoma, goiter or infection in the visceral space deep to the strap muscles often first presents as anterior swelling that displaces or obscures the normal muscular contours.

Suprahyoid muscles—mylohyoid, geniohyoid, stylohyoid and digastric (anterior and posterior bellies)—elevate the hyoid and floor of mouth during the initial phase of swallowing. The mylohyoid forms a muscular sling separating the sublingual space above from the submandibular space below, and defects or dehiscence in the mylohyoid can permit spread of sublingual infections into the submandibular space and vice versa. The digastric tendon courses through a fibrous sling attached to the hyoid, and the posterior belly passes adjacent to the parotid and stylomandibular region—important considerations when mapping spread of parotid infections.

Functionally, these muscle groups control airway patency through their influence on hyoid and laryngeal position. Dysfunction—whether from nerve injury (e.g., hypoglossal nerve palsy affecting geniohyoid), mechanical displacement from space-occupying lesions, or edema—can result in dysphagia, impaired airway protection, aspiration risk or visible anterior neck swelling. In the context of rapidly expanding infections (for example, Ludwig's angina), firm cellulitis and edema of the submandibular and sublingual spaces tether and elevate the floor of mouth and tongue by fixing the suprahyoid apparatus, producing posterior displacement of the tongue base and acute airway obstruction. Understanding which muscles form the boundaries of potential spaces is therefore directly relevant to anticipating clinical collapse and selecting airway strategies.

# Pharynx, larynx and upper airway anatomy (vallecula, pyriform fossa, vocal cords, epiglottis)

The upper aerodigestive tract is anatomically and functionally subdivided into the nasopharynx, oropharynx and laryngopharynx (hypopharynx), and the larynx sits at the junction between the pharynx and the trachea. The oropharynx includes the tonsillar pillars, soft palate and the base of tongue; the vallecula is the mucosal recess between the base of tongue and the epiglottis—an important landmark during direct laryngoscopy and endotracheal intubation because the tip of a laryngoscope blade often sits in the vallecula to lift the epiglottis anteriorly. The pyriform fossa (pyriform sinus) are paired mucosal recesses lateral to the laryngeal inlet that funnel material during deglutition into the esophagus; foreign bodies or mucosal lesions in this region can produce referred otalgia due to shared innervation.

The larynx is composed of cartilages (thyroid, cricoid, arytenoids, epiglottis), intrinsic and extrinsic muscles, and mucosal coverings. The vocal cords (true vocal folds) are mucosal folds overlying the thyroarytenoid muscles and vocal ligaments; the space between the cords is the glottis. Above the true cords lie the false cords (vestibular folds) which provide some protection and resonance. The epiglottis, a leaf-shaped elastic cartilage, acts as a dynamic protective valve during swallowing, retroflexing to cover the laryngeal inlet. Clinically, inflammation of supraglottic structures (epiglottitis) causes dysphagia, drooling and inspiratory stridor, and can progress to rapid airway obstruction because edema is confined within the tight mucosal envelope.

Pitch, voice quality, airway protection and cough depend on coordinated motion of the vocal cords and arytenoids; recurrent laryngeal nerve dysfunction results in vocal cord immobility, hoarseness and risk of aspiration, whereas superior laryngeal nerve sensory deficits blunt cough and place the patient at aspiration risk. The precise anatomic relationships are crucial for both diagnosis and intervention: a lesion at the level of the aryepiglottic fold will present differently than one in the pyriform sinus or vallecula. On flexible nasoendoscopy, the normal adult arytenoids appose in phonation and are mobile on respiration—immobility indicates neuropathy, mechanical fixation, or edema.

From an airway management perspective, understanding these structures directs how you visualize the larynx, where to place retracting instruments, and how pathology alters landmarks. For example, large vallecular cysts can obscure the epiglottis; periglottic edema shifts landmarks and may necessitate awake fiberoptic intubation. Recognition of bleeding risks—tonsillar or pyriform region vascularity—also influences procedural planning.

# Salivary glands and ducts (submandibular, sublingual, parotid)

The major salivary glands—the parotid, submandibular and sublingual glands—are both a source of infection and spaces that communicate with the fascial planes of the neck. The parotid gland lies superficially, overlying the posterior mandible and extending superiorly to the zygomatic arch; the facial nerve (cranial nerve VII) divides within the parotid into its branches, making parotid surgery and infection management risky for facial paralysis. The parotid duct (Stensen duct) opens into the oral cavity opposite the second upper molar; obstruction (sialolithiasis) or ductal stenosis produces glandular swelling, pain with meals (mealtime syndrome) and may predispose to ascending infection.

The submandibular gland rests in the submandibular triangle, partly superficial and partly deep to the mylohyoid muscle, with its duct (Wharton's duct) running forward in the floor of mouth to open at the sublingual caruncle adjacent to the lingual frenulum. Because part of the gland is below the mylohyoid and part above, infections or stones can present as swelling either in the submandibular triangle or as sublingual swelling that elevates the tongue. The sublingual glands lie in the floor of mouth above the mylohyoid bone as multiple small glands that empty via the duct of Rivinus or via accessory ducts into Wharton's duct.

Clinically, sialadenitis presents with localized pain, swelling, erythema and occasionally pus expressed from the ductal orifice. Ultrasonography is the first-line imaging for sialolithiasis and acute sialadenitis; computed tomography provides excellent delineation of deep glandular extension and abscess. Because the submandibular space communicates with the parapharyngeal space and because the floor of mouth is a potential route to the airway, submandibular space infections can quickly result in tongue elevation and airway compromise (e.g., Ludwig's angina). Parotid infections and neoplasms present more laterally and posteriorly but can affect the facial nerve; metastatic disease may manifest as firm, fixed parotid masses or as intraparotid lymph nodes.

# Neurovascular structures (cranial nerves IX–XII, cervical plexus, carotid sheath contents)

A clear understanding of the cranial nerves and major cervical neurovascular bundles is necessary to interpret deficits seen in head and neck disease and to avoid iatrogenic injury during interventions. Cranial nerve IX (glossopharyngeal) provides sensory innervation to the oropharynx and posterior tongue, taste to the posterior one-third of the tongue, and motor input to the stylopharyngeus muscle; its dysfunction presents with impaired gag reflex and altered taste, and lesions can produce referred otalgia. Cranial nerve X (vagus) controls motor function of most pharyngeal and laryngeal muscles via its pharyngeal and recurrent laryngeal branches, contributes to sensory input of the larynx and carries parasympathetic fibers; vagal injury results in dysphonia, dysphagia and aspiration risk. Cranial nerve XI (spinal accessory) innervates the sternocleidomastoid and trapezius; injury causes shoulder droop and impaired head rotation. Cranial nerve XII (hypoglossal) supplies tongue muscles; unilateral palsy produces tongue deviation toward the side of the lesion and dysarthria/dysphagia.

The cervical plexus (C1–C4) provides cutaneous sensation to the anterior and lateral neck via superficial branches (e.g., lesser occipital, great auricular, transverse cervical, supraclavicular), and motor innervation to infrahyoid muscles via the ansa cervicalis (C1–C3). The ansa cervicalis runs along the carotid sheath and is sometimes identified and preserved or intentionally used during surgical nerve transfers.

The carotid sheath is a condensation of deep cervical fascia that encloses the common and internal carotid arteries, the internal jugular vein and the vagus nerve within its posterior-lateral aspect. The close relationship between artery, vein and nerve explains clinical syndromes when masses distort the sheath: pulsatile masses suggest vascular lesions; thrombosis or compression can produce cranial nerve dysfunction or cerebral ischemia. The internal jugular vein offers a route for retrograde spread of infection (septic thrombophlebitis), and the carotid bifurcation at approximately the level of C3–C4 is a critical surgical landmark. Knowledge of the course of the hypoglossal nerve as it crosses the carotid arteries is also important—injury during carotid surgery can lead to tongue weakness.

From a clinical perspective, head and neck infections that involve the poststyloid parapharyngeal space may present with cranial neuropathies (IX–XII) and Horner's syndrome due to sympathetic chain involvement. Similarly, malignancies at the skull base may produce lower cranial nerve palsies because of tight confinement of these nerves in their exit foramina. Preoperative mapping of these neurovascular structures by imaging and careful neurologic exam are essential for risk stratification.

# Lymphatic drainage of head and neck

Lymphatic drainage of the head and neck follows predictable patterns that guide the evaluation of infection and malignancy. The regional lymph nodes are classically grouped into levels I–VI for surgical and radiologic communication. Level I comprises submental (Ia) and submandibular (Ib) nodes. Level II nodes lie along the upper internal jugular chain from the skull base to the level of the hyoid; level III nodes occupy the mid-jugular region; level IV nodes are the low jugular nodes extending to the clavicle; level V comprises the posterior triangle nodes; level VI are the central compartment nodes around the thyroid and trachea.

Primary drainage sites are specific: the oral cavity largely drains to levels I–III; the oropharynx and tonsillar region commonly to level II and III; the hypopharynx and larynx drain to level II–IV; and the thyroid drains to central compartment (level VI) and paratracheal nodes. Cutaneous lesions of the scalp and lateral face often drain to level V and the posterior auricular nodes; the parotid contains intraglandular nodes that drain both to preauricular and upper cervical nodes. Knowledge of these pathways aids staging of head and neck cancer, selection of neck dissection levels, and interpretation of radiologic findings: for example, an enlarged level II node with unknown primary suggests oropharyngeal or nasopharyngeal origin, while involvement of level IV nodes raises concern for laryngeal or hypopharyngeal disease.

In infection, the same anatomy explains why dental infections produce ipsilateral submandibular and submental lymphadenopathy, whereas tonsillitis causes tender level II nodes. Normal cervical lymph node size varies by location and age, but generally nodes larger than 1 cm (except for jugulodigastric and posterior auricular nodes where slightly larger size may be acceptable) or nodes with concerning features (hard, fixed, matted, progressively enlarging) require further evaluation. When evaluating septic or potentially malignant nodes, adjuncts include ultrasound, fine needle aspiration cytology, and cross-sectional imaging.

# Anatomy relevant to endoscopy, surgical access and airway management

Successful endoscopic and surgical procedures in the head and neck rely on precise knowledge of surface and deep anatomy, predictable landmarks and relationships that affect instrument passage and safety. For endoscopy and airway management, the external landmarks—mentum, hyoid bone (at level C3), thyroid cartilage (C4–C5), cricoid cartilage (C6), and sternal notch—help estimate internal levels. The vallecula and epiglottis are used during laryngoscopy to lift and expose the glottis; alternatively, direct lift of the epiglottis can be achieved in some cases. For flexible fiberoptic intubation, the nasal cavity, nasopharynx and oropharynx should be navigated with awareness of the adenoids, tonsils and tongue base that may obscure the view.

Awake fiberoptic intubation is indicated when high risk of difficult ventilation or intubation exists—for example, substantial anterior neck swelling, limited mouth opening, or distorted anatomy from tumors or infection. Contraindications or challenges include uncontrolled bleeding or excessive secretions that obscure optics. Emergency surgical access to the airway includes cricothyrotomy and tracheotomy. The cricothyroid membrane lies between the thyroid and cricoid cartilages and is preferred for rapid front-of-neck access because it is relatively avascular and superficial. Tracheotomy, performed lower at the tracheal rings, provides more durable access but is more technically demanding and has additional anatomic considerations (thyroid isthmus, large midline vessels, aberrant innominate artery). Recognition of the midline structures, vascular anomalies and previous surgery or radiation scarring is crucial; ultrasound can identify the cricoid, tracheal rings and thyroid isthmus and help avoid vascular injury.

Surgical approaches to deep neck spaces require knowledge of the relationship of the facial planes and neurovascular bundles. For drainage of a parapharyngeal abscess, a transcervical approach may be chosen if the collection is lateral and accessible, whereas a transoral approach is favored when the collection abuts the pharyngeal lumen and is amenable to endoscopic drainage—each approach carries distinct risks to nerves (e.g., glossopharyngeal, hypoglossal, facial) and vessels. When performing cervical lymph node dissections, level-specific anatomical knowledge allows selective removal while sparing non-involved structures such as the spinal accessory nerve to preserve shoulder function.

Intraoperative and bedside decision points frequently revolve around airway security: whether to attempt endotracheal intubation, perform awake fiberoptic intubation, proceed directly to surgical airway, or secure the airway before imaging or definitive drainage. Factors that raise concern include severe stridor, rapid progression of neck swelling, inability to lie supine, trismus, and signs of impending obstruction (tachypnea, use of accessory muscles, oxygen desaturation). Laboratory and imaging adjuncts—complete blood count (normal adult white blood cell count $4.0$–$11.0 \times 10^{9}$/L), markers of systemic inflammation such as C-reactive protein (CRP reference varies by lab but typically <5 mg/L), and computed tomography with contrast—help determine the presence and extent of abscess versus cellulitis and inform the timing and type of airway intervention.

Conclusion

An integrated understanding of the fascial planes, muscle groups, salivary glands, pharyngeal and laryngeal anatomy, neurovascular bundles and lymphatic pathways of the head and neck permits anticipation of disease spread, recognition of signs that predict airway compromise, and safe performance of endoscopic and surgical interventions. Clinicians must synthesize the anatomic knowledge with dynamic clinical findings—voice change, dysphagia, trismus, tracheal deviation, stridor, and progressive hypoxia—to determine the urgency and manner of airway management and to choose the most appropriate approach for drainage or definitive surgery.

Introduction

Swallowing is a complex, highly coordinated sensorimotor function that safely transports food and liquid from the oral cavity to the stomach while protecting the airway. Disruption at any point along the swallowing process can produce dysphagia, with important consequences including aspiration, malnutrition, dehydration, and reduced quality of life. For the clinician evaluating a patient with dysphagia it is essential to understand the normal phases of swallowing, the muscles and neural circuits that generate and coordinate the act, the mechanisms by which aspiration and penetration occur, and how to distinguish oropharyngeal from esophageal disorders on clinical grounds. Instrumental studies—primarily the video fluoroscopic swallow study (VFSS or modified barium swallow, MBS) and fiberoptic endoscopic evaluation of swallowing (FEES)—provide objective information about physiology and guide therapy. Management requires an individualized combination of compensatory strategies, rehabilitative exercises, diet modifications, and, when necessary, enteral feeding. This chapter reviews these topics in depth, emphasizing pathophysiology and clinical reasoning relevant to bedside and instrumental assessment.

# Phases of swallowing (oral preparatory, oral transit, pharyngeal, esophageal)

Swallowing is traditionally divided into four overlapping phases: oral preparatory, oral transit (oral propulsive), pharyngeal, and esophageal. Each phase has distinct goals, anatomy, and timing, and failure in any phase produces characteristic clinical signs.

Oral preparatory phase
The oral preparatory phase readies the bolus for safe transport. For solids this includes mastication, salivary mixing, and bolus formation; for liquids it involves lip closure, labial containment, and controlled bolus delivery. Major muscle groups include the muscles of mastication (masseter, temporalis, medial and lateral pterygoids), buccinator and orbicularis oris for lip and cheek control, and tongue intrinsic/extrinsic muscles for bolus manipulation. Sensory feedback from the oral cavity (trigeminal V and facial VII for taste and texture) informs timing of the next phase. Failure of labial or buccal control produces anterior or lateral leakage; impaired mastication leads to inadequate bolus breakdown; poor tongue control leads to premature spillage into the pharynx.

Oral transit (oral propulsive) phase
Oral transit begins when the tongue tip elevates against the anterior palate and the tongue base squeezes the bolus posteriorly. This phase is a voluntary action and typically lasts about 1 second for a single liquid bolus in healthy adults. Key muscles are the intrinsic and extrinsic tongue muscles (genioglossus, styloglossus, hyoglossus). Deficits produce prolonged oral transit time, leftover food in the oral cavity, and risk of premature spillage into the pharynx, especially with thin liquids.

Pharyngeal phase
The pharyngeal phase is a rapid, semi-automatic sequence that propels the bolus through the pharynx while protecting the lower airway. Triggering of the pharyngeal swallow occurs when the bolus reaches the level of the faucial pillars/valleculae; sensory input from glossopharyngeal (IX) and vagal (X) branches to the nucleus tractus solitarius initiates the pattern of motor output from the nucleus ambiguus and related centers. Key events include velopharyngeal closure to prevent nasal regurgitation, elevation and anterior movement of the hyoid and larynx, epiglottic inversion to protect the airway, adduction of the vocal folds and approximation of the aryepiglottic folds, pharyngeal constriction, and opening of the upper esophageal sphincter (UES). UES opening is accomplished by cricopharyngeal relaxation and traction from hyolaryngeal excursion. The pharyngeal phase is short—typically less than 1 second—but highly vulnerable; impairment produces coughing, choking, or silent aspiration.

Esophageal phase
After the bolus passes the UES, peristalsis in the tubular esophagus transports the bolus to the stomach and the lower esophageal sphincter (LES) relaxes transiently to allow passage. This phase is involuntary, mediated by vagal efferents and the myenteric plexus. Primary peristalsis follows the pharyngeal swallow; secondary peristalsis clears residual material. Disorders here present differently than pharyngeal problems and are typically perceived as a sensation of food “sticking” in the chest or retrosternal region.

# Muscles and neural control of swallowing

Swallowing is orchestrated by a network of cranial nerves, brainstem nuclei, cortical and subcortical centers, and a central pattern generator (CPG) that produces the coordinated motor sequence. Understanding the neural control helps localize lesions and anticipate physiologic consequences.

Peripheral muscles and their innervation
- Oral and facial muscles: lip closure (orbicularis oris, facial nerve VII), cheek tension (buccinator, VII), mastication (masseter, temporalis, pterygoids, mandibular branch of trigeminal V3). Tongue muscles are innervated by hypoglossal nerve (XII) for motor function (genioglossus, styloglossus, hyoglossus, intrinsic muscles); palatoglossus receives vagal innervation via pharyngeal plexus.
- Pharyngeal muscles: superior, middle, and inferior pharyngeal constrictors contract sequentially under glossopharyngeal (IX) sensory input and vagal (X) motor control via the nucleus ambiguus. Stylopharyngeus (IX) elevates the pharynx.
- Laryngeal muscles: intrinsic muscles controlling vocal fold adduction/abduction are innervated by branches of the vagus—recurrent laryngeal nerve for most intrinsic muscles and superior laryngeal nerve for cricothyroid. Cricopharyngeus (upper esophageal sphincter muscle) is innervated by vagal branches and pharyngeal plexus and acts as a tonic sphincter that relaxes during the swallow.
- Esophageal muscles: the proximal esophagus contains striated muscle under vagal and glossopharyngeal/brainstem control; the distal esophagus relies on smooth muscle and the enteric nervous system under autonomic modulation.

Central control
Sensory inputs from the oral cavity, pharynx, larynx, and esophagus travel via cranial nerves V, VII, IX, and X to the nucleus tractus solitarius (NTS) in the medulla. The NTS integrates afferent information and interacts with the adjacent nucleus ambiguus and reticular formation to form the brainstem CPG for swallowing. This CPG coordinates the timing of laryngeal closure, pharyngeal constriction, and UES opening. Descending cortical and subcortical pathways modulate initiation and timing of voluntary swallowing; cortical lesions (e.g., stroke) often produce oropharyngeal dysphagia by disrupting initiation and modulation, whereas brainstem lesions can disrupt the CPG and lead to more severe, often bilateral deficits. The cerebellum and basal ganglia also influence timing and coordination.

Clinical implications
Examining strength and symmetry of cranial nerve functions (facial expression, tongue protrusion, palatal elevation, gag and cough reflex) gives key clues about lesion location. For example, unilateral hypoglossal palsy causes tongue deviation toward the weak side and impaired bolus propulsion; recurrent laryngeal nerve injury leads to vocal fold paresis with impaired airway protection and increased aspiration risk. Central lesions can produce delayed swallow initiation and reduced hyolaryngeal elevation; peripheral nerve injuries produce more focal deficits.

# Mechanisms of aspiration and penetration

Understanding how material enters or approaches the airway clarifies why certain patients aspirate and guides targeted intervention. Two related terms are used in instrumental assessment: penetration (entry of material into the laryngeal vestibule above the vocal folds) and aspiration (entry of material below the level of the vocal folds into the trachea). Aspiration can be overt (with coughing) or silent (without effective reflex cough or sensory awareness), and its timing relative to the swallow influences cause and risk.

Timing-related mechanisms
- Premature spillage and pre-swallow aspiration: Poor oral control or improper bolus containment allows liquids to spill into the pharynx before the pharyngeal swallow is triggered. If the airway is not protected (larynx adducted) at that time, aspiration can occur before the swallow.
- Aspiration during the swallow: Inadequate laryngeal closure, incomplete vocal fold adduction, reduced epiglottic inversion, or reduced timing of hyolaryngeal elevation can allow material to enter the airway during the pharyngeal phase. Reduced pharyngeal constriction increases residue that may be propelled into the airway during subsequent inspiration.
- Post-swallow aspiration: Residue in the valleculae or pyriform sinuses can be aspirated after the swallow during subsequent breaths or with a delayed cough. In patients with poor cough reflex or reduced sensation, this residue is especially dangerous.

Pathophysiologic factors that increase aspiration risk
- Sensory deficits: Reduced laryngeal/pharyngeal sensation (e.g., neuropathy, stroke, head and neck radiation) diminishes cough reflex and awareness, producing silent aspiration. Laryngeal anesthesia studies show increased penetration/aspiration when mucosal sensation is absent.
- Motor deficits: Weakness or discoordination of tongue, pharyngeal constrictors, or laryngeal elevators reduces bolus propulsion and airway protection. Reduced hyolaryngeal excursion prevents adequate UES opening and airway closure.
- Structural lesions: Zenker diverticulum, pharyngeal strictures, or neck masses can trap material that then spills into the airway. Poor oral closure (lip incompetence) leads to anterior leakage and drooling, but aspiratory risk is mainly related to pharyngeal residue.
- Gastroesophageal reflux and laryngopharyngeal reflux can produce chronic inflammation, edema and reduced sensation, increasing risk of penetration and chronic aspiration.

Clinical significance
Aspiration is not synonymous with pneumonia, but it increases risk of pulmonary infection and chemical pneumonitis. Silent aspiration is of particular concern because patients may lack cough or subjective symptoms, requiring instrumental studies to detect. Interventions (postural changes, diet modification, exercises, airway protective maneuvers) are chosen to address the specific mechanism identified.

# Clinical features distinguishing oropharyngeal vs esophageal dysphagia

Differentiating oropharyngeal (transfer) dysphagia from esophageal dysphagia guides diagnostic testing and treatment. History and focused exam can usually distinguish the two.

Oropharyngeal dysphagia
Oropharyngeal dysphagia refers to difficulty initiating a swallow or transferring the bolus from the mouth into the esophagus. Typical clinical features include:
- Immediate coughing, choking, or throat clearing upon attempting to swallow, often with thin liquids.
- Nasal regurgitation of liquids during swallowing if velopharyngeal closure is impaired.
- Drooling or difficulty managing oral secretions.
- Hoarseness, wet or gurgly voice quality after swallowing, or recurrent aspiration pneumonia.
- Multiple swallows per mouthful, or need to swallow repeatedly to clear bolus.
- Timing: symptoms occur at the start of a swallow and are noted with mouth-to-throat transfer.
- Common etiologies: neurologic disorders (stroke, Parkinson disease, ALS), neuromuscular junction disorders (myasthenia gravis), structural lesions in the oropharynx, head and neck surgery or radiation.

Esophageal dysphagia
Esophageal dysphagia is a sensation of food sticking or stopping after the swallow, often localized to the chest or lower sternal area. Features include:
- Sensation of obstruction or food “stuck” in the throat/chest, often occurring seconds after the swallow.
- Difficulty with solids progressing to liquids suggests mechanical obstruction (e.g., peptic stricture, rings, malignancy), whereas dysphagia to both solids and liquids from onset suggests motor disorder (e.g., achalasia).
- Regurgitation of undigested food, especially if nocturnal or independent of swallowing, points to esophageal retention or motility disorder.
- Odynophagia (painful swallowing) suggests inflammatory or infectious esophagitis (e.g., pill esophagitis, candida).
- Timing: symptoms are delayed relative to swallow initiation and occur during the esophageal transit phase.
- Common etiologies: peptic stricture, eosinophilic esophagitis, Schatzki ring, esophageal carcinoma, achalasia, scleroderma-related motility disorders.

Clinical decision points
Key historical distinctions include whether coughing/choking occurs at or immediately after swallowing (favoring oropharyngeal) versus fullness/retention feeling in the chest later after swallowing (favoring esophageal). Presence of recurrent aspiration pneumonia, drooling, or an abnormal voice strongly suggests oropharyngeal dysfunction and should prompt instrumental assessment of safety of swallowing. If esophageal dysphagia is suspected, upper endoscopy and esophageal manometry are the primary diagnostic modalities.

# Instrumental assessments (video fluoroscopic swallow study / modified barium swallow, fiberoptic endoscopic evaluation of swallowing)

Instrumental studies provide objective, physiologic evaluation of swallowing safety (aspiration risk) and efficiency (residue, bolus flow). The two most used are VFSS (MBS) and FEES; each has unique strengths and limitations.

Video fluoroscopic swallow study (VFSS / modified barium swallow)
VFSS is a dynamic radiographic study that visualizes the oral, pharyngeal, and esophageal phases using barium-contrasted boluses of varying consistencies and volumes. It is typically recorded in lateral and anterior–posterior views.

What VFSS shows:
- Oral bolus formation and oral transit time.
- Triggering and timing of the pharyngeal swallow.
- Hyolaryngeal excursion, epiglottic inversion, pharyngeal contraction, UES opening.
- Penetration/aspiration and its timing relative to the swallow.
- Pharyngeal residue in valleculae and pyriform sinuses.
- Proximal esophageal findings such as cricopharyngeal dysfunction, esophageal stasis, and gross motility abnormalities.

Advantages:
- Comprehensive view of all swallow phases.
- Allows observation of compensatory strategies (postural changes, maneuvers) and immediate assessment of their effectiveness.
- Quantitative measures can be derived (e.g., Penetration-Aspiration Scale).

Limitations:
- Exposure to ionizing radiation; study duration and number of swallows are limited.
- Requires patient transport to radiology and cooperation (sitting upright, following commands).
- Visualization is of radiopaque boluses only; secretions are not well visualized unless stained or contrasted.

Clinical indications and interpretation:
VFSS is the preferred initial instrumental test for assessing aspiration risk in patients with suspected oropharyngeal dysphagia when a comprehensive physiologic assessment is needed and when postural or dietary management decisions hinge on visualization of the pharyngeal swallow. Findings guide diet recommendations and therapeutic plans.

Fiberoptic endoscopic evaluation of swallowing (FEES)
FEES uses a transnasal flexible endoscope positioned in the hypopharynx to visualize laryngeal and pharyngeal structures before and after the swallow. It is performed without radiation and can be performed at bedside.

What FEES shows:
- Anatomic structure and mucosal integrity of the nasopharynx, pharynx, larynx.
- Presence and amount of secretions pooling in the hypopharynx.
- Penetration and aspiration of dyed or colored boluses (usually food/liquid dyed blue or green).
- Pharyngeal residue, vocal fold mobility, and response to sensory stimulation.

Advantages:
- No radiation; can be repeated frequently and used for biofeedback during therapy.
- Performed at bedside and in nontransportable patients.
- Direct visualization of secretion management and laryngeal sensation testing (e.g., touch to arytenoids).

Limitations:
- Brief “white-out” period during the actual swallow due to epiglottic inversion, so aspiration occurring exactly at swallow peak may be missed; however, pre- and post-swallow observations usually allow inference.
- Does not visualize oral phase or esophageal transit; proximal esophageal findings may only be inferred.
- Requires nasal passage and patient tolerance.

Clinical indications and choice between tests
Choice depends on clinical question and patient factors. Use VFSS when detailed analysis of bolus flow, UES opening, and esophageal phase is needed or when comparing compensatory strategies’ effects on biomechanical events. Use FEES for bedside safety assessment, to evaluate secretion management, to observe laryngeal structures, in patients who cannot be transported or tolerate radiation, and for therapy biofeedback. Often both tests provide complementary information.

Additional instrumental modalities
- High-resolution manometry assesses esophageal motility and UES/LES function and is indicated when esophageal motility disorders (e.g., achalasia, spasm) are suspected.
- Scintigraphic swallow studies quantitatively measure aspiration of radiolabeled material and can evaluate chronic aspiration but are less practical for immediate clinical decision-making.
- Clinician-administered bedside swallow assessments and standardized screening tools (e.g., water swallow test, Gugging Swallowing Screen) are used for screening but cannot replace instrumental testing when aspiration risk or physiologic detail is required.

# Management principles (diet modification, enteral feeding options, swallowing rehabilitation)

Management of dysphagia aims to maintain nutrition and hydration, prevent aspiration and pulmonary complications, and—when possible—restore safer, more efficient swallowing. Approaches are individualized based on the physiologic diagnosis.

Diet modification and texture/consistency changes
Modifying bolus texture and volume is a common compensatory strategy. Thicker liquids reduce flow rate and pharyngeal spillage and may lower aspiration rates in some patients, whereas thin liquids are more difficult to control for those with impaired oral containment or weak pharyngeal squeeze. Texture modifications include pureed solids, mechanically soft diets, and graduated liquid consistencies (nectar-, honey-thick). Tools such as the International Dysphagia Diet Standardisation Initiative (IDDSI) provide standardized levels to describe consistencies. Clinicians should consider patient preferences and hydration/nutrition trade-offs, because thickened liquids may reduce fluid intake.

Postural adjustments and behavioral/postural maneuvers
Simple postural changes can modify bolus flow and airway protection. Chin tuck (neck flexion) may reduce aspiration for some patients by narrowing the airway entrance and reducing premature entry to the airway. Head rotation toward the weak side can close the pyriform sinus on that side and direct bolus down the stronger side. The supraglottic swallow and super-supraglottic swallow are voluntary airway-protective maneuvers that enlist conscious breath-holding and cough to protect the airway during swallow. The Mendelsohn maneuver increases and prolongs laryngeal elevation and UES opening by voluntary sustained elevation of the larynx during swallow. These strategies are most useful when demonstrated effective on instrumental testing.

Swallowing rehabilitation
Rehabilitative exercises aim to improve strength, range, timing, and coordination of swallowing musculature. Common approaches include:
- Tongue and lingual resistance exercises (e.g., pushing against a depressor or using devices) to improve oral pressure generation and bolus propulsion.
- Shaker exercise and chin tuck against resistance to strengthen suprahyoid muscles and improve UES opening.
- Effortful swallow to increase pharyngeal constriction pressure and bolus clearance.
- Neuromuscular electrical stimulation (NMES) applied to anterior neck musculature: data are mixed; some patients benefit when NMES is applied as adjunctive therapy, but it is not universally accepted as standard of care.
- Task-specific, progressive swallowing practice and neurorehabilitation principles apply—repetition, intensity, and specificity drive functional gains.

Pharmacologic and surgical interventions
Pharmacologic therapy is limited but includes agents to manage excessive secretions (anticholinergic agents or botulinum toxin for sialorrhea), prokinetics for gastroparesis contributing to reflux, and acid suppression when reflux-associated laryngeal inflammation contributes to dysphagia. Surgical options include cricopharyngeal myotomy for UES dysfunction, procedures to address Zenker diverticulum, or laryngeal medialization for unilateral vocal fold paralysis. Botulinum injection into the cricopharyngeus may be diagnostic and therapeutic for some UES hypertonicity.

Enteral feeding options and decision points
When oral intake is unsafe or insufficient, enteral feeding provides nutrition. Options include nasogastric (NG) tubes, nasojejunal tubes, percutaneous endoscopic gastrostomy (PEG), and surgical gastrostomy/jejunostomy. Clinical decision points:
- Short-term feeding needs (expected duration < 2–4 weeks) often use NG tubes; longer-term feeding generally favors PEG because of patient comfort, reduced tube dislodgement, and lower likelihood of sinusitis or nasopharyngeal irritation.
- If aspiration of gastric contents or severe reflux is a concern, postpyloric feeding (nasojejunal or jejunostomy) may be preferable.
- Timing of enteral access: in critically ill patients early enteral nutrition (within 24–48 hours) is generally favored. For stroke patients, guidelines vary; if prolonged inability to swallow is anticipated (> 7–14 days), percutaneous feeding access is considered. Ethical and goals-of-care discussions about long-term feeding are essential, particularly in progressive or terminal neurologic diseases.
- Feeding route selection must consider patient prognosis, wishes, and quality-of-life implications.

Multidisciplinary care
Management often requires a team: speech-language pathologists for assessment and therapy, nutritionists for caloric and hydration needs, gastroenterologists/ENT surgeons for structural or esophageal causes, neurologists for central etiologies, and primary care/palliative care for goals-of-care and long-term planning.

# Complications of dysphagia (aspiration pneumonitis, malnutrition, airway compromise)

Dysphagia-related complications are common and can be serious. Recognizing and preventing them is a key clinical priority.

Aspiration pneumonitis vs. aspiration pneumonia
Two concepts are important:
- Aspiration pneumonitis refers to chemical injury from inhalation of sterile gastric contents (acid, digestive enzymes). It typically presents acutely after a witnessed aspiration event with inflammatory lung injury and may progress rapidly to acute lung injury or ARDS. Management is primarily supportive; antibiotics are not indicated unless there is superimposed infection.
- Aspiration pneumonia refers to infection resulting from inhalation of bacteria-laden oropharyngeal secretions and food particles. It often presents with fever, leukocytosis, focal infiltrates on chest imaging, and systemic signs of infection. Treatment requires appropriate antimicrobial therapy targeted to the clinical scenario and likely organisms.

Risk factors and prevention
Patients with impaired consciousness, poor oral hygiene, high-volume pharyngeal secretions, and reduced cough reflex are at high risk. Preventive measures include oral hygiene to reduce bacterial load, positional feeding strategies, swallowing therapy to reduce aspiration, and appropriate enteral access when safe oral intake cannot be maintained.

Malnutrition, dehydration, and metabolic consequences
Inadequate intake due to dysphagia leads to weight loss, protein-energy malnutrition, micronutrient deficiencies, and dehydration. Objective measures include unintended weight loss (e.g., >5% in 1 month or >10% over 6 months is clinically significant) and laboratory markers (e.g., low prealbumin or albumin—though these are affected by inflammation and not specific). Assessment should include weight trends, body mass index (BMI), oral intake records, and screening tools such as the Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool (MUST) or Subjective Global Assessment (SGA). Malnutrition increases morbidity, impairs wound healing, and complicates rehabilitation.

Airway compromise and respiratory failure
Bulky aspiration or severe edema of the larynx can cause acute airway obstruction. Recurrent large-volume aspirations may lead to chronic bronchiectasis or recurrent infections. Patients with progressive neuromuscular disease may have combined dysphagia and respiratory muscle weakness, increasing risk for respiratory failure and poor cough clearance.

Other complications
- Social and psychological effects include isolation, anxiety around eating, and reduced quality of life.
- Dental disease from poor oral hygiene and retained food fragments.
- For patients with feeding tubes, tube-related complications: aspiration of gastric contents despite feeding tube, tube dislodgement, local infection at PEG site, or bowel perforation in rare cases.

Monitoring and follow-up
Monitoring for complications involves regular reassessment of swallow safety via clinical and instrumental testing as indicated, nutritional surveillance (weight, intake, labs), pulmonary monitoring for signs of infection, and reassessment of goals of care. Interdisciplinary follow-up improves outcomes.

Conclusion

An understanding of the phases of swallowing, the muscles and neural systems that accomplish and coordinate the act, and the mechanisms that permit aspiration is essential for the assessment and management of dysphagia. Differentiating oropharyngeal from esophageal causes guides diagnostic strategy: instrumental studies such as VFSS and FEES provide complementary physiologic information that informs compensatory strategies, rehabilitative plans, and decisions about enteral feeding. Management is individualized, often multidisciplinary, and must balance aspiration risk, nutritional needs, patient preferences, and prognosis. Vigilance for complications—aspiration pneumonia/pneumonitis, malnutrition, and airway compromise—and proactive measures to prevent them are central to care.

Introduction

Skeletal muscle is the largest organ system by mass in most adults and serves essential roles in posture, locomotion, respiration, and metabolic homeostasis. Understanding the microanatomy, fiber-type specialization, and the tissue-level organization of muscle is critical for interpreting clinical syndromes of weakness, exercise intolerance, and myoglobinuric kidney injury. Equally important is familiarity with the mechanisms by which muscle is injured and regenerates, and with the laboratory and electrodiagnostic tools used to detect and localize muscle pathology. This chapter reviews skeletal muscle structure and organization, the cellular and molecular bases of muscle injury and regeneration, the common biochemical markers used to detect myocyte damage (their isoforms and interpretation), the significance of myoglobinuria and its contribution to acute kidney injury, the electrophysiologic distinctions between myopathic and neuropathic processes, and the typical MRI and PET imaging features of active inflammatory myositis versus chronic fibrotic or fatty replacement.

# Muscle fiber types, fascicular organization, connective tissue (endomysium, perimysium, epimysium)

Skeletal muscle is organized hierarchically from the molecular contractile apparatus to whole muscles. At the cellular level, the muscle fiber (myocyte) is a multinucleated syncytium formed by fusion of myoblasts during development. Each fiber contains parallel arrays of myofibrils composed of repeating sarcomeres—structures of interdigitating actin and myosin filaments whose regulated sliding generates force.

Muscle fiber types are commonly divided into slow-twitch (type I) and fast-twitch (type II) classes, with further subdivisions of type II into IIa, IIx (IIb in some species), and IIb in experimental contexts. These types reflect differences in contractile speed, metabolic substrate utilization, fatigue resistance, and innervation patterns.

- Type I fibers (slow oxidative): These fibers express myosin heavy chain isoforms adapted for slow, sustained contraction. They have abundant mitochondria, high capillary density, and rely primarily on oxidative phosphorylation. They are fatigue-resistant and predominate in postural and endurance muscles. Histochemically they are dark on oxidative enzyme stains (e.g., succinate dehydrogenase) and show low glycogen content relative to fast-glycolytic fibers.

- Type IIa fibers (fast oxidative-glycolytic): Intermediate in contractile speed and fatigue resistance, type IIa fibers use both oxidative and glycolytic metabolism. They are recruited for activities requiring more power than pure endurance but more endurance than pure sprinting.

- Type IIx/IIb fibers (fast glycolytic): These fibers have high contraction speed and generate large forces but fatigue rapidly. They have fewer mitochondria, lower capillary density, and rely heavily on anaerobic glycolysis and stored glycogen. They are prevalent in muscles used for brief, powerful bursts.

Fiber-type composition varies between muscles and between individuals and can adapt with training, denervation, or disease. Motor unit recruitment follows the size principle: small motor neurons innervating type I fibers are recruited first, then progressively larger motor units with type II fibers.

At the tissue level, muscle fibers are bundled within fascicles surrounded by connective tissue layers with distinct roles:

- Endomysium: A fine network of reticular connective tissue and capillary-rich extracellular matrix that envelops individual muscle fibers. The endomysium contains satellite cells (muscle stem cells), fibroblasts, microvasculature, and nerve terminals. It transmits forces between adjacent fibers and provides the extracellular niche for regeneration.

- Perimysium: Denser connective tissue that groups fibers into fascicles. It contains larger blood vessels, nerves, and lymphatics. The perimysium contributes to passive elastic properties and transmits force from fascicles to the tendinous insertion.

- Epimysium: A sheath of dense irregular connective tissue that encases the entire muscle. It integrates with the deep fascia and tendon, providing continuity of force transmission from muscle to bone and structural protection.

These connective tissue layers contain extracellular matrix components—collagens (types I and III predominating in perimysium and epimysium), proteoglycans, elastin, and glycoproteins—that influence mechanical properties and repair responses. Fibrosis following chronic injury or unresolved inflammation results from dysregulated matrix deposition, altering compliance and function.

Neuromuscular junctions are specialized sites where motor neuron terminals contact the sarcolemma at the motor endplate; their integrity is essential for voluntary contraction. Vascular supply is arranged so that capillary density correlates with oxidative capacity; reduced perfusion or microvascular injury can precipitate muscle ischemia and necrosis.

# Mechanisms of muscle injury and regeneration

Muscle injury may originate from mechanical trauma, ischemia-reperfusion, metabolic disturbances, toxins, immune-mediated inflammation, genetic defects, or denervation. Despite diverse etiologies, common final pathways of myofiber damage include sarcolemmal disruption, excitation-contraction uncoupling, mitochondrial dysfunction, intracellular calcium overload, oxidative stress, and activation of proteases.

- Sarcolemmal disruption: Mechanical strains (e.g., crush injury), exercise-induced eccentric contraction, or membrane-targeting toxins can create breaches in the sarcolemma. Loss of membrane integrity allows uncontrolled influx of extracellular calcium and sodium, efflux of cytosolic enzymes (e.g., creatine kinase), and activation of degradative pathways.

- Calcium overload: Elevated intracellular calcium activates calpains and other proteases and phospholipases, degrading structural proteins and membranes. Calcium handling defects (e.g., ryanodine receptor mutations) can produce malignant hyperthermia or exertional rhabdomyolysis.

- Mitochondrial dysfunction and oxidative stress: Impaired oxidative phosphorylation reduces ATP needed for ion pumps, exacerbating ion gradients collapse. Reactive oxygen species (ROS) from dysfunctional mitochondria further damage lipids, proteins, and DNA.

- Inflammatory responses: Immune-mediated myopathies (polymyositis, dermatomyositis, immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy) or secondary inflammation following necrosis recruit macrophages, T cells, and complement. These cells can clear necrotic debris but may also extend tissue injury through cytokine-mediated cytotoxicity.

- Ischemia-reperfusion: Vascular occlusion produces energy failure; reperfusion introduces ROS and a neutrophil-mediated inflammatory surge that magnifies injury.

- Genetic defects: Mutations in structural proteins (e.g., dystrophin in Duchenne muscular dystrophy) or in metabolic enzymes predispose fibers to mechanical stress or metabolic failure, respectively, leading to chronic degeneration-regeneration cycles.

Regeneration in skeletal muscle is robust relative to many tissues and relies on satellite cells—resident muscle stem cells located beneath the basal lamina in the endomysium. Following injury, a coordinated sequence occurs:

1. Degeneration and inflammation: Necrotic fibers are cleared by neutrophils and macrophages. Macrophage phenotype shifts from pro-inflammatory (M1) during debris clearance to anti-inflammatory/pro-repair (M2) during resolution.

2. Satellite cell activation: Quiescent satellite cells express Pax7; upon activation they upregulate MyoD and Myf5, proliferate, and generate myoblasts.

3. Differentiation and fusion: Myoblasts differentiate into myocytes expressing myogenin and fuse with one another or with surviving fibers to form multinucleated myotubes that mature into new fibers. The basal lamina provides a scaffold for correct alignment. Successful regeneration restores sarcomeric architecture.

4. Remodeling: Revascularization, reinnervation, and reconstitution of connective tissue occur. If the basal lamina is preserved and innervation reestablished, fiber size and function can be near-normal.

Poor regenerative outcomes arise when satellite cell pools are depleted, basal lamina is destroyed (e.g., extensive crush injury), chronic inflammation persists, or fibrosis/fatty infiltration dominates. Repeated cycles of necrosis and repair—as in many muscular dystrophies—lead to progressive loss of functional muscle and replacement by collagen and adipose tissue.

Clinically, timing matters: serum enzymes rise early after acute injury and fall as regeneration proceeds; biopsy features distinguish active necrosis with macrophages and regenerating fibers (rounded fibers with basophilic cytoplasm and central nuclei) from chronic changes of fiber size variation, endomysial fibrosis, and fatty replacement.

# Creatine kinase (isoforms), lactate dehydrogenase, aldolase and transaminases as muscle injury markers

Biochemical markers detect myocyte membrane disruption or increased membrane permeability, releasing cytosolic enzymes into the circulation. The most widely used marker is creatine kinase (CK). Other enzymes—lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), aldolase, and transaminases (AST and ALT)—are also informative but less muscle-specific.

Creatine kinase

CK catalyzes the reversible transfer: creatine + ATP ↔ phosphocreatine + ADP. Skeletal muscle contains high concentrations of CK to buffer ATP during contraction. CK is a dimeric enzyme with tissue-specific isoenzymes formed from combinations of M (muscle) and B (brain) subunits:

- CK-MM: Predominant in skeletal and cardiac muscle; accounts for the majority of serum CK in skeletal muscle injury.
- CK-MB: Enriched in myocardium but also present in skeletal muscle at low levels.
- CK-BB: Found in brain and smooth muscle.

Measurement:

- Reference ranges vary by laboratory; approximate normal total CK (adult male): up to ~200 U/L. Women and older adults may have lower normal values. Exercise, intramuscular injections, and recent trauma can raise CK. Many labs use units U/L with assay-specific cutoffs.

Interpretation:

- CK rises within 2–12 hours after acute muscle injury, peaks at about 24–72 hours, and declines with a half-life of roughly 36–48 hours if no ongoing injury.
- Very high CK values (>5000–10,000 U/L) raise concern for rhabdomyolysis and risk of myoglobinuria and acute kidney injury.
- CK isoenzyme analysis (CK-MB fraction) is important in distinguishing myocardial infarction from skeletal muscle injury; however, skeletal muscle injury can raise CK-MB modestly. Cardiac troponins are more specific for myocardial necrosis.

Lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)

LDH catalyzes the interconversion of lactate and pyruvate and is present in virtually all tissues. Serum LDH comprises five isoenzymes (LDH-1 to LDH-5). LDH-5 is predominant in liver and skeletal muscle. LDH rises with muscle injury but lacks specificity and is often used in conjunction with CK. LDH has a longer serum half-life than CK and may remain elevated after CK normalizes.

Aldolase

Aldolase participates in glycolysis (aldol cleavage of fructose-1,6-bisphosphate). It is abundant in skeletal muscle, especially glycolytic fibers, and serum aldolase is sometimes measured when CK is normal but clinical suspicion for myopathy remains (e.g., some inflammatory myopathies). Normal aldolase: approximately 1–7 U/L depending on lab. Like LDH, aldolase lacks tissue specificity.

Transaminases (AST and ALT)

AST (aspartate aminotransferase) is present in liver, heart, and skeletal muscle; ALT (alanine aminotransferase) is more liver-predominant but also present in muscle. Isolated elevation of AST or ALT may stem from muscle injury rather than hepatic pathology, especially when accompanied by elevated CK. In practice, a markedly elevated AST with proportionate CK elevation suggests muscle source; the AST/ALT ratio may be >1 in muscle injury. Normal ranges: AST ~10–40 U/L, ALT ~7–56 U/L (lab-dependent). Always interpret in clinical context and consider hepatic testing if needed.

Practical clinical decision points

- When CK is mildly elevated (e.g., <2–3× upper limit of normal) in an asymptomatic patient, consider exercise, intramuscular injections, or statin therapy before exhaustive workup. Repeat testing after rest can clarify trend.
- CK elevations >5× upper limit (and particularly >10×) warrant evaluation for rhabdomyolysis risk and possible renal injury. Aggressive fluid management and monitoring of electrolytes may be appropriate.
- When distinguishing myocardial vs skeletal sources of CK-MB elevation, use cardiac troponins and clinical context; CK-MB alone is insufficiently specific.
- In suspected inflammatory myositis, CK is frequently elevated but may be normal in some subtypes (e.g., inclusion body myositis or amyopathic dermatomyositis). Aldolase and LDH can help in such scenarios.

# Urine myoglobin and risk of myoglobinuria/acute kidney injury

Myoglobin is a small (17 kDa) oxygen-binding heme protein abundant in skeletal and cardiac muscle. Significant myofiber necrosis releases myoglobin into plasma; when serum myoglobin exceeds renal handling capacity, it is filtered and appears in urine—myoglobinuria—which can be directly nephrotoxic.

Pathophysiology of myoglobin-mediated kidney injury

- Tubular obstruction: Myoglobin can precipitate in the acidic distal tubule, forming casts that obstruct tubular flow. Cast formation is promoted by hypovolemia and acidic urine (pH <6).

- Direct tubular toxicity: The heme moiety of myoglobin catalyzes formation of reactive oxygen species, causing lipid peroxidation and direct cytotoxicity to tubular epithelial cells.

- Vasoconstriction and ischemia: Free heme and products of muscle breakdown promote renal vasoconstriction, worsening perfusion and exacerbating tubular injury.

Risk factors

- Massive muscle injury with large CK increases (often CK >5000–10,000 U/L) and elevated plasma myoglobin.
- Volume depletion, hypotension, sepsis, preexisting chronic kidney disease, concurrent nephrotoxins (NSAIDs, contrast agents), and acidic urine increase risk.
- Prolonged immobilization and crush injuries classically precipitate rhabdomyolysis and myoglobinuric renal failure.

Clinical recognition

- Urine may be dark brown or tea-colored; a positive urine dipstick for blood with microscopic absence or paucity of red blood cells suggests myoglobinuria (dipstick reacts to heme). Confirmatory testing for myoglobin in urine is not universally available; clinical inference is common.
- Laboratory workup: Serum CK, serum creatinine, electrolytes (especially hyperkalemia, hyperphosphatemia, hypocalcemia early then hypercalcemia in recovery), plasma myoglobin (if available), and urinalysis. Monitor urine output.

Management principles

- Early aggressive isotonic fluid resuscitation to maintain urine output (target often ≥0.5–1 mL/kg/hr, tailored to clinical context) to dilute nephrotoxins and prevent cast formation.
- Administration of bicarbonate to alkalinize urine (controversial; some guidelines suggest considering urinary alkalinization in severe cases to reduce myoglobin precipitation) — typical approach is to target urine pH >6.5, recognizing evidence is mixed.
- Diuretics such as mannitol or loop diuretics are sometimes used to promote urine flow after volume resuscitation.
- Avoid nephrotoxins and monitor/treat electrolyte abnormalities (hyperkalemia is life-threatening).
- Renal replacement therapy for severe acute kidney injury, refractory hyperkalemia, volume overload, or uremic complications.

Quantitative thresholds for risk are imprecise, but higher CK levels correlate with greater risk. Not all patients with high CK develop acute kidney injury; clinical context and supportive measures determine outcome.

# Electromyography basics for myopathy vs neuropathy

Electromyography (EMG) and nerve conduction studies (NCS) are electrodiagnostic tools used to localize neuromuscular disorders and to distinguish myopathic from neuropathic processes. EMG assesses electrical activity of muscles at rest and during voluntary contraction via needle electrodes; NCS examine conduction velocity and amplitude of peripheral nerves.

Key principles and findings

- Motor unit potential (MUP): When a motor neuron and its innervated muscle fibers fire synchronously, the summated waveform is a MUP. EMG examines MUP amplitude, duration, and morphology.

- Recruitment: Refers to how additional motor units are activated to increase force. Recruitment pattern is informative: early or reduced recruitment occurs when fewer motor units are available or functioning; increased recruitment (i.e., small MUPs but high recruitment) indicates loss of force per motor unit.

Myopathic patterns

- At rest: Usually no spontaneous activity in mild chronic myopathies. Active myositis or necrosis may show spontaneous fibrillation potentials and positive sharp waves due to irritable muscle fibers.

- During voluntary contraction: Myopathic processes characteristically produce small-amplitude, short-duration MUPs with early recruitment. Why? Because individual muscle fibers are lost or weakened within motor units, so the surviving fibers generate smaller potentials, and the nervous system recruits more motor units to achieve force.

- Complex repetitive discharges and myotonic discharges may be present in specific myopathies (e.g., myotonic dystrophy, myotonia congenita).

Neuropathic patterns

- Denervation: Focal or generalized axonal loss produces spontaneous activity at rest—fibrillation potentials and positive sharp waves—reflecting denervated single muscle fibers. Fasciculation potentials (visible twitching) are often present.

- MUPs: Chronic reinnervation yields large-amplitude, long-duration polyphasic MUPs because surviving motor neurons sprout collaterals to reinnervate denervated fibers, increasing motor unit size. Recruitment is reduced; there are fewer motor units to generate force.

- NCS: In neuropathies, conduction velocity slowing or decreased amplitudes depend on demyelinating versus axonal pathophysiology. Demyelination shows slowed conduction velocity and prolonged distal latencies. Axonal loss shows reduced compound muscle action potential (CMAP) amplitudes.

Distinguishing features and clinical reasoning

- Small, short-duration MUPs with early recruitment point to a myopathy, particularly when correlated with weak muscles and preserved reflexes (or relatively less reflex loss than would be expected for the degree of weakness). EMG may help localize active inflammation by sampling multiple muscles.

- Large, long-duration MUPs with reduced recruitment and prominent spontaneous activity suggest chronic neuropathic reinnervation.

- Acute denervation from neuropathy can produce fibrillations before reinnervation changes, which helps timing.

- EMG is a functional test and should be interpreted with clinical exam and laboratory data (CK, imaging). Muscle fiber loss without denervation can produce myopathic EMG; however, severe atrophy can also reduce MUP amplitudes. In inflammatory myopathies, EMG is sensitive but not specific; biopsy and serology are necessary for definitive diagnosis.

Technical considerations

- Sampling multiple muscles from different limbs and different spinal levels improves diagnostic yield and helps decide a biopsy site (choose muscles that are weak but not end-stage fibrotic).

- EMG is operator-dependent and can be uncomfortable. Its sensitivity varies by disease stage and subtype.

# MRI and PET imaging appearances of active myositis vs chronic changes

Imaging of muscle provides anatomical localization, assessment of disease activity, and evaluation of chronic structural changes. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is the modality of choice for muscle imaging, offering high soft-tissue contrast, assessment of edema/inflammation, and detection of fatty replacement and atrophy. Positron emission tomography (PET), typically combined with CT (PET/CT) or MRI (PET/MR) using 18F-fluorodeoxyglucose (FDG), can detect metabolically active inflammation.

MRI features

- Acute/active myositis: On MRI, active inflammatory myositis characteristically demonstrates increased signal intensity on fluid-sensitive sequences (T2-weighted with fat suppression or short tau inversion recovery—STIR). This hyperintensity reflects increased free water from edema and inflammatory cell infiltration. Post-contrast T1-weighted sequences with gadolinium often show enhancement of involved muscles, reflecting increased vascular permeability and inflammation. The pattern of involvement may be symmetric or asymmetric depending on etiology; proximal muscle groups (pelvic and shoulder girdles) are commonly involved in idiopathic inflammatory myopathies.

Acute myositis MRI findings often include fascial enhancement (in dermatomyositis), perifascicular edema, and muscle belly edema with relative sparing of fat. In some disorders (e.g., inclusion body myositis), active inflammation may be patchy and less prominent.

- Chronic changes: Chronic muscle disease demonstrates atrophy of muscle bellies, loss of bulk, and a replacement of muscle tissue by fat and fibrous tissue. On MRI, fatty infiltration is best seen on T1-weighted sequences as increased high signal intensity within muscle; fat-suppressed sequences confirm fat content. Chronic changes result in reduced or absent STIR/T2 hyperintensity (no active edema), and the distribution of fatty replacement helps differentiate myopathies (e.g., specific patterns in muscular dystrophies). Fibrosis contributes to volume loss and decreased signal heterogeneity.

- Patterns that aid diagnosis: The distribution (proximal vs distal), symmetry, selective muscle involvement within compartments, and localization to fascial planes can suggest specific diagnoses. For example, symmetric proximal thigh involvement suggests polymyositis or dermatomyositis, while selective quadriceps and deep finger flexor involvement suggests inclusion body myositis.

PET imaging (FDG-PET)

- FDG uptake increases in tissues with enhanced glucose metabolism—activated inflammatory cells (e.g., macrophages, lymphocytes) take up FDG. In active myositis, affected muscles can demonstrate increased FDG uptake, correlating with inflammatory activity. PET can therefore detect active inflammation even when MRI is equivocal and may be useful for whole-body assessment of occult inflammatory myopathies or paraneoplastic associations.

- Limitations: FDG uptake is not specific for inflammatory cells and may occur with infection, recent exercise, or neoplastic processes. Standardization of uptake quantification (SUV values) for muscle inflammation is evolving. PET is less spatially detailed than MRI for architectural changes but offers a whole-body perspective and can identify metabolically active extraskeletal sites (e.g., associated malignancies).

Clinical use and decision points

- MRI guides biopsy: Identify muscles showing edema and enhancement but not end-stage fatty replacement to increase diagnostic yield of biopsy.

- MRI monitors therapy: Reduction of T2/STIR hyperintensity and normalization of enhancement correspond to decreased inflammatory activity. Persistent fatty replacement indicates irreversible damage.

- Combined modalities: In difficult cases, combining MRI and FDG-PET may help distinguish active inflammation (FDG-avid, T2 hyperintense) from chronic changes (T1 hyperintense due to fat, low FDG uptake). PET may detect subclinical metabolic activity when MRI is normal or when muscles are diffusely involved.

- Electrophysiologic and imaging correlation: EMG may show irritability in areas of MRI-defined edema. Use imaging to localize areas for EMG or biopsy rather than random sampling.

Summary

A sound understanding of skeletal muscle architecture and fiber specialization sets the stage for interpreting pathophysiologic mechanisms in muscle disease. Injury pathways converge on sarcolemmal disruption, calcium overload, mitochondrial dysfunction, and inflammation; regeneration depends on preserved basal lamina and satellite cell function. Biochemical markers—CK and its isoforms, LDH, aldolase, and transaminases—help detect and estimate the magnitude and timing of muscle injury; very high CK values raise concern for rhabdomyolysis and myoglobin-mediated renal injury. EMG and NCS distinguish myopathic from neuropathic processes based on MUP morphology and recruitment patterns and help localize disease for biopsy. MRI (and adjunctive PET) provides powerful anatomical and functional assessment, distinguishing active inflammatory edema from chronic fatty and fibrotic replacement, guiding biopsy site selection, and tracking treatment response. Integration of clinical history, laboratory data, electrodiagnostics, and imaging yields the most accurate assessment of neuromuscular disease.

Introduction

Idiopathic inflammatory myopathies (IIMs) are a heterogeneous group of autoimmune diseases characterized primarily by skeletal muscle inflammation leading to weakness, but they frequently involve skin and multiple extramuscular organs. The major contemporary diagnostic and nosologic categories include dermatomyositis (DM), polymyositis (PM), immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy (IMNM), and inclusion body myositis (IBM). Each entity differs in pathophysiology, typical clinical phenotype, autoantibody associations, prognosis, and response to therapy. An understanding of accepted classification criteria, typical presentations, common systemic complications, and tools used to assess disease severity and activity is essential for clinical reasoning, formulation of a differential diagnosis, and selection of appropriate diagnostics and management strategies.

#

Definitions and classification criteria for dermatomyositis and other idiopathic inflammatory myopathies

Classification of IIMs has evolved from purely clinicopathological schemes toward integrated criteria that incorporate clinical features, laboratory testing (including myositis-specific and associated autoantibodies), electromyography, muscle imaging, and histopathology. Two contemporary frameworks commonly used in practice and research are the European Neuromuscular Centre (ENMC) criteria (various updates) and the EULAR/ACR 2017 classification criteria.

Dermatomyositis (DM) is defined by a characteristic combination of skin manifestations and muscle involvement. Definite DM classically requires both typical cutaneous findings (for example heliotrope rash or Gottron papules) and objective muscle weakness with supporting laboratory, electromyographic, imaging, or histopathologic evidence of myositis. The ENMC recognizes clinically amyopathic DM (CADM) as a DM subtype with characteristic cutaneous features without objective muscle weakness for at least six months. Pathologically, DM often shows a perivascular and perimysial inflammatory infiltrate with complement-mediated microangiopathy leading to capillary loss and perifascicular atrophy.

Polymyositis (PM) historically referred to a subacute, symmetric proximal myopathy with lymphocytic (endomysial) inflammation, but true PM is now considered uncommon and often reclassified after further testing. ENMC and EULAR/ACR criteria require exclusion of other causes (e.g., inclusion body myositis, muscular dystrophies, metabolic myopathies) and rely on a combination of clinical pattern and biopsy features. PM is characterized histologically by CD8+ T cells invading non-necrotic muscle fibers expressing class I MHC.

Immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy (IMNM, sometimes termed necrotizing autoimmune myopathy) is characterized by prominent myofiber necrosis with minimal or absent inflammatory cell infiltrate on muscle biopsy. IMNM is frequently associated with specific autoantibodies (e.g., anti–signal recognition particle [SRP] and anti–3-hydroxy-3-methylglutaryl-coenzyme A reductase [HMGCR]) and typically presents with severe proximal weakness, high serum creatine kinase (CK) levels, and poor spontaneous recovery without immunotherapy.

Inclusion body myositis (IBM) is a late-onset inflammatory myopathy with a distinct phenotype: slowly progressive, often asymmetric weakness involving both proximal and distal muscles (particularly quadriceps and finger flexors), and poor response to immunotherapy. Muscle biopsy shows inflammatory infiltrates plus degenerative features including rimmed vacuoles and accumulation of abnormal proteins such as TDP-43 and p62.

The 2017 EULAR/ACR classification provides a probability score based on a weighted combination of clinical features (age at onset, pattern of weakness), laboratory tests (CK elevation), systemic features (dysphagia), muscle biopsy findings, and autoantibodies. For example, a score above a defined threshold indicates high likelihood of IIM. The EULAR/ACR tool also outputs probabilities and has separate algorithms when biopsy data are available or absent. Clinically, practitioners combine these criteria with ancillary testing—serology for myositis-specific antibodies (MSAs) and myositis-associated antibodies (MAAs), muscle MRI for edema and fatty replacement, EMG for myopathic and irritable changes, and targeted biopsy when necessary—to reach a diagnosis and subtype classification.

#

Epidemiology, age distribution and typical disease courses

IIMs are rare diseases with incidence and prevalence varying by region, diagnostic criteria, and age group. Estimates for overall incidence of adult IIMs generally fall in the range of 2–10 cases per million persons per year, with prevalence roughly 10–30 per 100,000, though these numbers differ by subtype and population. Dermatomyositis and IMNM can occur at any adult age but have peak incidence in middle adulthood; juvenile dermatomyositis (JDM) is a separate entity presenting in children. IBM is the most common acquired myopathy in patients older than 50–60 years, with incidence rising with age.

Age distribution links with phenotype and prognosis. Juvenile dermatomyositis typically presents between ages 2 and 12 and has distinct features, including a high risk of calcinosis if poorly controlled. Adult dermatomyositis often presents in the 40–60 year age range, though idiopathic disease can occur at any age. IMNM typically affects adults and can present subacutely with prominent weakness and very high CK. IBM usually begins after age 50–60 and has a chronic, slowly progressive course spanning years to decades.

Typical disease courses vary by subtype. Dermatomyositis and IMNM often begin subacutely to acutely, with weeks to months of progressive weakness; both may respond to immunosuppressive therapies but can relapse or require long-term treatment. Polymyositis, where valid, also has a subacute course and tends to be steroid-responsive. Inclusion body myositis has a slowly progressive course over years and is typically refractory to standard immunotherapies; significant disability accrues gradually, with frequent falls and loss of hand and knee function. Intercurrent complications—especially interstitial lung disease (ILD), dysphagia, and cardiac involvement—largely influence morbidity and mortality across the IIM spectrum.

Epidemiologic associations with autoantibodies and malignancy are clinically important. Certain myositis-specific autoantibodies correlate with specific clinical phenotypes and risks: for example, anti-TIF1-γ and anti-NXP2 antibodies have been associated with an increased risk of malignancy in adult DM, particularly in older patients; anti–MDA5 is strongly associated with rapidly progressive interstitial lung disease in some East Asian cohorts. Recognizing these patterns guides cancer screening and pulmonary evaluation.

#

Cutaneous manifestations associated with dermatomyositis (heliotrope, Gottron papules, shawl/V-neck sign, Holster sign, periungual changes)

Cutaneous manifestations are central to dermatomyositis and may precede, coincide with, or follow muscle involvement.
