- Late pattern: Severe capillary loss with extensive avascular areas, disorganization of the capillary array and evidence of neoangiogenesis with bizarre, ramified capillaries (bushy or branching forms) occur. Hemorrhages and giant capillaries may be less prominent at this stage because the capillary bed is largely destroyed.

The presence of an SSc pattern on capillaroscopy is highly specific for systemic sclerosis and strongly predictive when combined with Raynaud phenomenon and autoantibody findings. The 2013 ACR/EULAR classification criteria for systemic sclerosis do not require capillaroscopy for diagnosis, but a capillaroscopic scleroderma pattern is recognized as a strong supportive feature in clinical assessment and can help differentiate primary from secondary Raynaud phenomenon.

Dermatomyositis and idiopathic inflammatory myopathies

In idiopathic inflammatory myopathies, particularly dermatomyositis, capillaroscopic abnormalities reflect an immune-mediated microangiopathy affecting skin and muscle. Typical findings include:

- Dilated and tortuous capillary loops; giant capillaries may be present but are generally less prominent than in scleroderma.
- Capillary dropout with reduced density, often correlating with active muscle or skin disease.
- Abnormal loops with ramification or branching in prolonged disease.
- Periungual erythema and periungual telangiectasias are commonly noted clinically and correlate with capillary loss and telangiectatic changes on capillaroscopy.

In dermatomyositis, capillary loss correlates with disease activity and may parallel complement-mediated endothelial injury seen in biopsies. Certain myositis-specific autoantibodies (for example, anti-Mi-2, anti-MDA5) have been associated with distinct capillaroscopic features and different clinical phenotypes; however, patterns are heterogeneous and not pathognomonic.

Mixed connective tissue disease, systemic lupus erythematosus and other CTDs

Capillaroscopic patterns in other CTDs are more variable and often less specific than in SSc. Examples:

- Mixed connective tissue disease (MCTD): May show scleroderma-like changes, including dilated loops and variable capillary loss, reflecting overlap features.
- Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE): Nailfold capillary abnormalities are often nonspecific—mild tortuosity, decreased density, and occasional hemorrhages. A true "scleroderma pattern" can occur in some SLE patients with overlap features, but this is uncommon.
- Primary Raynaud phenomenon: Capillary morphology is typically normal or shows only nonspecific changes (mild tortuosity). The distinction between primary and secondary Raynaud's is a central clinical application of capillaroscopy: a scleroderma-pattern or marked capillary loss indicates secondary Raynaud’s likely due to CTD.
- Vasculitides and antiphospholipid syndrome: Findings are variable, sometimes showing microhemorrhages or capillary dropout related to focal ischemia, but no pathognomonic pattern.

Key point: The pattern-based approach (recognizing scleroderma patterns vs. non-specific changes) has high clinical utility in triaging patients with Raynaud phenomenon and suspected CTD and should always be interpreted in the clinical context including history, exam, serology and, when relevant, pulmonary evaluation.

# Microhemorrhages, avascular areas, capillary dilation and dropout

Microhemorrhages

Microhemorrhages appear as dark brown to black punctate or linear deposits adjacent to capillary loops and correspond to extravasated red blood cells or hemosiderin following endothelial or perivascular damage. They are most numerous in the early and active phases of the SSc pattern, where immune-mediated endothelial injury and increased permeability permit red-cell leakage. In dermatomyositis, microhemorrhages may also occur during active skin disease.

Clinical interpretation: The presence of multiple recent hemorrhages suggests ongoing active microvascular injury. Serial observations showing decreasing hemorrhages may indicate treatment response or natural evolution, whereas new or persistent hemorrhages may alert to continuing disease activity and increased risk of ischemic complications.

Avascular areas and capillary dropout

Avascular areas denote focal regions in the distal row in which capillary loops are absent over a measurable linear distance. Capillary dropout is quantified by reduced capillary density (fewer loops per mm) and by the size and number of avascular areas. Definitions vary, but clinically significant avascular areas typically represent a loss of adjacent capillary loops over distances of several tenths of a millimeter, and when numerous, they indicate severe microangiopathy.

Pathophysiology: Progressive endothelial cell injury, obliterative microangiopathy and failure of normal angiogenic repair mechanisms result in permanent loss of capillary loops. In SSc this is an important mechanism underlying digital ischemia, ulceration and the vasculopathic complications of the disease.

Clinical interpretation: Capillary dropout and large avascular areas are strongly associated with more severe disease manifestations, including a higher risk of digital ulcers, and correlate with systemic vascular complications such as pulmonary arterial hypertension (PAH) and interstitial lung disease (ILD) in SSc. Quantitative measures of density and avascular areas are useful prognostic markers and for monitoring progression.

Capillary dilation and "giant" capillaries

Capillary dilation refers to enlargement of the capillary loop, typically most evident at the apical loop. "Giant" or megacapillaries are defined by markedly enlarged apical loops; a commonly used threshold in research settings is an apical loop diameter exceeding about 50 µm, though exact numeric cutoffs depend on magnification and measurement technique. These giant capillaries are characteristic of early and active scleroderma patterns.

Pathophysiology: Dilation reflects endothelial dysfunction, impaired perivascular support, and increased intraluminal pressure within damaged loops. In early disease, dilation may represent a compensatory change before loss; in later disease, dilated loops may be destroyed, producing avascular areas.

Clinical interpretation: The presence of giant capillaries—particularly when multiple and combined with hemorrhages—supports a diagnosis of scleroderma-spectrum microangiopathy and, in an appropriate clinical context, systemic sclerosis. In dermatomyositis, dilated loops are also seen but giant capillaries are less specific.

Neoangiogenesis and bizarre loops

Neoangiogenesis denotes the formation of new, often abnormal capillary loops that appear as tortuous, ramified, bushy or “bizarre” shapes. These are more prominent in the late scleroderma pattern and reflect attempted but abnormal vascular repair.

Pathophysiology: Chronic ischemia stimulates angiogenic signaling, but the repair process in SSc is disordered, leading to defective, fragile and morphologically aberrant capillaries. The presence of ramified capillaries indicates longstanding injury and remodeling rather than acute activity.

Clinical interpretation: Neoangiogenesis signals chronicity and microvascular remodeling. It is associated with advanced peripheral microangiopathy and, when extensive, correlates with more severe systemic disease.

# Clinical utility in diagnosis, prognostication and monitoring

Diagnostic utility

Nailfold capillaroscopy is most valuable diagnostically in patients who present with Raynaud phenomenon, unexplained digital ischemia, or clinical features suggesting CTD. The central diagnostic uses are:

- Distinguishing primary from secondary Raynaud phenomenon: A normal capillaroscopic pattern supports primary Raynaud's, whereas scleroderma-pattern changes or marked capillary loss strongly suggest secondary Raynaud’s due to CTD.
- Supporting a diagnosis of systemic sclerosis: The presence of an SSc capillaroscopic pattern in a patient with Raynaud phenomenon and appropriate clinical features increases diagnostic confidence and can be an early marker of disease even before fibrosis develops. Capillaroscopic abnormalities may precede serologic markers and other manifestations.
- Identifying microangiopathy in dermatomyositis and other inflammatory myopathies: Capillary loss and dilated loops support the concept of a microvascular pathogenesis and can help corroborate clinical suspicion.
- Differentiating overlap syndromes: Patterns that combine scleroderma-like changes with other features can suggest mixed connective tissue disease or overlap syndromes.

Prognostication

Capillaroscopy provides prognostic information in several contexts:

- Risk of digital ulcers and ischemic complications: Reduced capillary density and larger avascular areas are associated with higher incidence of digital ulcers. Patients with extensive capillary loss require closer monitoring and aggressive prevention of ischemia.
- Prediction of internal organ involvement in systemic sclerosis: Several studies have shown correlations between the extent of capillary loss/abnormality and development of pulmonary arterial hypertension and interstitial lung disease. Patients with severe late-phase capillaroscopic changes have increased likelihood of systemic vasculopathy.
- Disease activity in inflammatory myopathies: In dermatomyositis, capillary density and the presence of hemorrhages may correlate with active disease and muscle involvement; improvements in capillary morphology may accompany clinical remission.

Monitoring and measuring response to therapy

One of the advantages of capillaroscopy is that it is repeatable and noninvasive, allowing serial assessment to monitor natural disease evolution and response to therapy. Practical monitoring uses both qualitative pattern recognition (improvement from “active” to “early” or more normal-appearing loops) and quantitative metrics (changes in capillary density, number of giant capillaries, hemorrhage counts, avascular area extent).

Examples of serial use:

- In SSc, therapy that modifies vascular injury (e.g., vasodilators, immunomodulatory agents in selected situations) may be associated with stabilization or modest improvement in capillary morphology, although reversal of late-stage avascular areas is uncommon. A reduction in hemorrhages and fewer new avascular areas may indicate disease control.
- In dermatomyositis, improvement in capillary density or reduction of hemorrhages can parallel improvement in muscle enzymes, strength and cutaneous disease, and may support tapering of immunosuppression.

Clinical decision points and integration with other data

Capillaroscopy should be integrated with clinical history, physical examination, serologic testing (antinuclear antibodies including SSc-specific autoantibodies such as anti-centromere, anti-topoisomerase I, anti-RNA polymerase III), and relevant imaging (e.g., echocardiography and pulmonary function tests for SSc). Specific decision points include:

- In a patient with Raynaud phenomenon and scleroderma-pattern capillaroscopy, proceed with targeted serologic testing and periodic screening for systemic sclerosis–related organ involvement (baseline and periodic pulmonary function tests, echocardiography, assessment for digital ulcers).
- In suspected dermatomyositis with capillary dropout, correlate with serum muscle enzyme levels (CK, aldolase), electromyography, muscle MRI and autoantibodies, and use capillaroscopy as a supportive metric of microvascular involvement and disease activity.
- Use capillaroscopy findings to stratify risk for digital ischemia and to guide preventive measures (patient education, smoking cessation, digital wound care, use of vasodilator therapy such as calcium channel blockers or phosphodiesterase inhibitors, and consideration of more aggressive interventions when avascular areas are progressive).

Limitations and standardization

Capillaroscopy has limitations that clinicians must recognize. There is interobserver variability unless images are obtained and scored with standardized protocols. Technical factors—magnification, immersion medium, available reference ranges for that system—affect measurements. Some capillary abnormalities are nonspecific and occur in healthy aging, chronic smokers, diabetes and after trauma. Thus interpreting capillaroscopy requires clinical correlation.

Efforts to standardize acquisition and scoring (e.g., EULAR recommendations and standardized definitions for scleroderma patterns) improve reproducibility. Videocapillaroscopy with image storage and software-assisted measurement provides the best platform for serial monitoring and research.

Conclusion

Nailfold capillaroscopy is a low-risk, high-yield tool for evaluating microvascular structure and pathology in patients with Raynaud phenomenon, suspected systemic sclerosis, dermatomyositis and related connective tissue diseases. Mastery of technique, awareness of normal reference ranges and patterns, and understanding of the pathophysiologic significance of microhemorrhages, avascular areas, capillary dilation and neoangiogenesis allow clinicians to use capillaroscopy effectively for diagnosis, prognosis and monitoring. Integration with clinical assessment and appropriate ancillary testing maximizes its clinical value while recognizing its limitations.

Introduction

Imaging plays a central role in the evaluation and management of inflammatory myopathies and associated neck and thoracic pathology. Proper selection and interpretation of magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), computed tomography (CT), and 18F-fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography (FDG-PET) informs diagnosis, guides biopsy, stages disease, and screens for complications such as interstitial lung disease (ILD), deep neck infections or occult malignancy. This chapter reviews the imaging appearances of active and chronic myositis on MRI, the application of CT in assessing neck soft tissues and airway compromise, high-resolution CT (HRCT) patterns of myositis-associated ILD and pulmonary nodules, the utility of FDG-PET in inflammatory myopathies and cancer screening, and the limitations, artifacts, and complementary use of these modalities. Emphasis is placed on pathophysiologic correlates, practical interpretation, and clinical decision points relevant to the medical student or resident.

# MRI sequences and findings of active myositis (STIR/T2 hyperintensity, gadolinium enhancement) vs chronic atrophy/fibrosis

Magnetic resonance imaging is the most sensitive modality for detecting muscle inflammation and for distinguishing active myositis from chronic inactive changes. The pathophysiology of inflammatory myopathies—such as polymyositis, dermatomyositis, immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy, and inclusion body myositis—involves variable degrees of myofiber necrosis, inflammatory cell infiltration, edema, and later fatty replacement and fibrosis. MRI signal characteristics reflect these tissue changes: increased free water in edema and active inflammation produces increased signal on fluid-sensitive sequences, while fatty infiltration and atrophy produce high signal on fat-sensitive sequences and muscle volume loss respectively. Contrast enhancement highlights areas of vascular permeability and inflammation.

Sequence selection and their interpretive roles: T1-weighted sequences provide anatomical detail and show muscle bulk and fatty infiltration. T2-weighted sequences with fat suppression and short-tau inversion recovery (STIR) sequences are fluid-sensitive and highlight edema and active inflammation as hyperintense signal relative to normal muscle. STIR is robust across varying field strengths and less susceptible to inhomogeneous fat suppression, making it widely used for myositis imaging. Post-gadolinium T1 fat-suppressed sequences demonstrate enhancement in areas of active inflammation due to increased capillary permeability and neovascularity. Diffusion-weighted imaging (DWI) has been explored but is less standardized; it may show restricted diffusion in some inflammatory or necrotic regions. Newer quantitative methods such as T2 mapping and fat fraction (Dixon or multi-echo techniques) provide objective metrics for longitudinal monitoring and for distinguishing fat from edema.

Patterns of active myositis: Active disease typically shows muscle enlargement with hyperintense signal on STIR/T2 images, often diffuse or patchy within affected muscles. Edema tends to be peri-fascicular or more prominent in proximal muscle groups (shoulder girdle, pelvic girdle) in dermatomyositis and polymyositis. Gadolinium enhancement is usually present in areas corresponding to STIR hyperintensity; enhancement can be diffuse, patchy, or perimysial/perifascicular depending on the subtype. Fascial and subcutaneous edema may accompany dermatomyositis due to microvascular involvement and superficial inflammation. Inflammatory myopathies associated with overlap syndromes (e.g., antisynthetase syndrome) may demonstrate more subacute or patchy muscle edema.

Chronic atrophy and fibrosis: When inflammation is longstanding or severe, muscle fibers are lost and replaced by fat and scar. On T1-weighted images, fatty replacement appears as increased signal intensity within muscle, often associated with reduction in muscle bulk. On STIR/T2 images, chronic fatty infiltration shows lower or isointense signal relative to acutely inflamed muscle; chronic fibrosis is typically low signal on both T1 and T2 sequences because collagenous scar has low water content. Fat-suppressed T1 post-contrast imaging may show little enhancement in purely fibrotic tissue, although adjacent mildly inflamed areas can enhance. Clinically, the presence of fatty replacement and volume loss indicates irreversible damage and has prognostic implications.

Temporal and distributional clues: MRI can help stage disease activity. Predominant STIR/T2 hyperintensity without much fatty replacement suggests active or early disease that may respond to immunotherapy. Conversely, prominent fatty infiltration and atrophy indicate chronicity. Distribution—symmetric proximal muscle involvement is typical of classic inflammatory myopathies, yet asymmetric or distal patterns may occur (e.g., inclusion body myositis shows selective involvement of finger flexors and quadriceps). Recognizing perimysial/perifascicular patterns supports dermatomyositis; focal multifocal involvement may raise suspicion for necrotizing myopathy or focal infection/myositis. MRI also guides biopsy: targeting an area of maximal STIR hyperintensity increases diagnostic yield by sampling active inflammatory tissue rather than fibrotic or fatty tissue.

Practical interpretation and pitfalls: Interpreters must distinguish physiological variations and other causes of muscle T2 hyperintensity. Exercise or recent trauma can produce transient muscle edema. Denervation from nerve injury causes edema in the acute phase and fatty atrophy later; pattern typically follows a nerve distribution. Infectious pyomyositis tends to show focal fluid collections with rim enhancement and restricted diffusion, distinguishing it from diffuse inflammatory myositis. Patient positioning artifacts, motion, and inadequate fat suppression can mimic pathology; correlation with clinical context and laboratory markers (creatine kinase, inflammatory markers) is essential. Quantitative measures such as T2 relaxation times and muscle fat fraction thresholds are emerging to standardize assessments; normal T2 values vary by sequence and magnet strength, so institutional reference ranges are needed.

# CT neck for deep neck space infections, soft tissue edema and airway assessment

Computed tomography of the neck is the workhorse for assessing deep neck spaces, airway patency, and osseous or foreign-body-related causes of soft tissue infection or edema. CT excels at rapid evaluation in acute settings, delineating gas, calcification, abscesses, and bony involvement, and providing multiplanar reconstructions helpful for surgical planning. Understanding the fascial spaces of the neck—submandibular, parapharyngeal, retropharyngeal, prevertebral, and visceral spaces—guides interpretation and correlates with routes of infection spread and potential airway compromise.

Indications and technical considerations: Contrast-enhanced CT of the neck is indicated when deep neck infection, Ludwig angina, suspected abscess, cellulitis with potential airway compromise, or penetrating injury are clinical concerns. Intravenous iodinated contrast enhances soft tissue planes and highlights rim enhancement around collections (abscesses). Thin-slice axial imaging with coronal and sagittal reformats improves anatomic localization. Oral contrast is rarely necessary in acute soft tissue evaluation and may obscure local detail. For airway assessment, CT can be performed with the patient supine; however, because airway caliber can change with position, clinical examination and endoscopic assessment remain complementary.

Findings in deep neck infections and edema: CT differentiates phlegmonous inflammation (confluent soft tissue swelling without a discrete fluid collection) from abscess formation (fluid collection with peripheral enhancement and often internal gas). Abscesses commonly show rim enhancement after IV contrast and may have loculations. Early phlegmon may show diffuse fat-stranding and soft tissue swelling without a clear liquid center. Gas within a collection suggests anaerobic infection or gas-producing organisms and often indicates a need for surgical drainage. Cervical lymphadenopathy is often reactive but can accompany infection or malignancy. Assessment of involvement of the parapharyngeal and retropharyngeal spaces is critical because infection can descend into the mediastinum along fascial planes, leading to mediastinitis.

Airway assessment and urgent decision points: CT provides objective measurements of airway narrowing and displacement. Infections like Ludwig angina (bilateral submandibular and sublingual space involvement) can rapidly compromise the upper airway by elevating the floor of mouth and posterior displacement of the tongue. Obstruction risk is assessed by cross-sectional area reduction, subglottic edema, and associated mediastinal extension. However, decisions regarding airway control should be based on a combination of imaging, clinical signs (stridor, drooling, inability to handle secretions, oxygenation), and endoscopic findings; CT is an adjunct for planning intubation or surgical airway. Imaging findings such as a near-occlusive retropharyngeal abscess or rapidly expanding collection prompt early otolaryngology or surgical consultation.

Complications and extensions: CT can detect jugular vein thrombosis, erosion into adjacent bones (osteomyelitis), vascular involvement, and mediastinal extension. Identification of a foreign body (e.g., fish bone) or calcified sialolithiasis that predisposed to infection is often possible. In immunocompromised patients, CT may show atypical features such as more extensive necrosis or less robust rim enhancement. Radiologists must be vigilant for signs of sepsis and guide drainage procedures by identifying a safe route that avoids major vessels, nerves, and the airway.

Limitations and alternative modalities: CT exposes the neck to ionizing radiation and involves iodinated contrast, which is contraindicated in severe contrast allergy or significant renal failure. MRI can better delineate soft tissue and vascular structures without radiation, and it is superior for differentiating cellulitis from neoplasm and for characterizing vascular malformations, but it is slower and less available in emergencies. Ultrasound is valuable for superficial abscesses and guiding drainage but cannot evaluate deep or retropharyngeal spaces reliably.

# High-resolution CT chest for ILD patterns and pulmonary nodules

High-resolution computed tomography (HRCT) is the imaging standard for the evaluation of interstitial lung disease (ILD) and provides critical information in patients with inflammatory myopathies, many of whom develop myositis-associated ILD, particularly those with certain autoantibodies (e.g., anti-synthetase). HRCT characterizes patterns of lung involvement, extent of disease, and presence of pulmonary nodules or malignancy, which affect prognosis and management.

Technique and interpretation principles: HRCT is performed with thin-section (typically 1-mm to 1.5-mm) axial acquisitions at full inspiration with high spatial frequency reconstruction algorithms. Images are reviewed in lung windows for parenchymal detail and in mediastinal windows for nodules, lymphadenopathy, and pleural disease. Prone images and expiratory views may help differentiate dependent atelectasis from early fibrosis or air-trapping suggestive of small airways disease. Semi-quantitative scoring of disease extent can be useful for monitoring progression.

Common ILD patterns and their pathophysiology: HRCT patterns are described using standardized terms because they correlate with histopathology and prognosis. Key patterns include:

- Usual interstitial pneumonia (UIP): Patchy subpleural and basilar-predominant reticulation, traction bronchiectasis, and honeycombing (clustered cystic airspaces typically subpleural). UIP shows heterogeneous fibrosis with temporal and spatial variability and is associated with poorer prognosis. In the context of myositis, UIP pattern is less common than nonspecific interstitial pneumonia but may occur.

- Nonspecific interstitial pneumonia (NSIP): More uniform ground-glass opacity and reticulation with basal predominance, relative subpleural sparing early on, and less honeycombing. NSIP often reflects a more inflammatory and potentially steroid-responsive process; it is the most common pattern in connective tissue disease–associated ILD, including myositis.

- Organizing pneumonia (OP): Patchy, often peripheral or peribronchial consolidation with a tendency to migrate and resolve with therapy; may coexist with ground-glass changes. OP often improves with corticosteroids.

- Acute interstitial pneumonia/diffuse alveolar damage (AIP/DAD): Diffuse bilateral ground-glass opacities and consolidation, often rapidly progressive, associated with high morbidity and mortality.

- Mixed patterns: Patients with inflammatory myopathies can have mixed NSIP-OP or NSIP-UIP features. Recognizing mixed patterns guides therapy and prognosis.

Ground-glass opacity (GGO) represents partial airspace filling, interstitial inflammation, or fine fibrosis. Reticulation indicates fibrotic interstitial thickening. Traction bronchiectasis indicates architectural distortion from fibrosis and portends irreversible disease. Honeycombing is an indicator of established fibrosis.

Pulmonary nodules and malignancy screening: HRCT also evaluates pulmonary nodules, which may represent infection, inflammation, or malignancy. Myositis, particularly dermatomyositis and certain antibody-positive subtypes, is associated with an increased risk of malignancy. HRCT is sensitive for nodules producing guidance about size, number, attenuation, and growth on serial imaging. Management follows established nodule guidelines (e.g., Fleischner Society): solitary pulmonary nodules larger than certain thresholds (e.g., >6 mm in high-risk patients) generally require risk stratification, interval follow-up imaging, or PET/biopsy. In the myositis population, a lower threshold for investigation may be considered given the heightened malignancy risk.

Clinical decision points: Distinguishing inflammatory (potentially reversible) from fibrotic (irreversible) disease is essential. Predominant GGO and minimal reticulation suggest more inflammation and possible responsiveness to immunosuppression; prominent reticulation, traction bronchiectasis, and honeycombing indicate advanced fibrosis with limited reversibility. In rapidly progressive respiratory decline with diffuse GGO, consider acute exacerbation or DAD; urgent management and intensivist involvement are required. HRCT should be integrated with pulmonary function tests (e.g., forced vital capacity, diffusing capacity for carbon monoxide—DLCO) and serologic markers to guide therapy and monitoring. Reference ranges: DLCO normal approximately 80–120% predicted; values <60% predicted often indicate significant gas exchange impairment warranting closer attention.

Limitations: HRCT involves ionizing radiation and may not distinguish active inflammation from fibrosis in all cases. Serial imaging and adjunctive modalities (FDG-PET, MRI-based pulmonary imaging experimental techniques) can help when the distinction is unclear. Tissue diagnosis through bronchoalveolar lavage or surgical biopsy is sometimes necessary when imaging and clinical data are discordant.

# FDG-PET utility for identifying widespread muscle inflammation and occult malignancy

FDG-PET/CT measures glucose metabolism and is widely used for oncologic staging. Because activated inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages, lymphocytes) exhibit increased glucose uptake, FDG-PET can detect inflammatory foci, including inflamed muscle in inflammatory myopathies. Its dual role—identifying widespread muscle inflammation and screening for occult malignancy—makes it valuable in the workup of myositis, especially when paraneoplastic processes are a concern.

Principles and patterns in myositis: In active myositis, FDG uptake in affected muscles is increased relative to background soft tissues and may be symmetric or asymmetric. The uptake pattern may be focal, patchy, or diffuse, often involving proximal muscle groups. FDG-PET can detect subclinical muscle involvement not evident on MRI or clinical examination and can help localize sites for biopsy when MRI is unavailable or inconclusive. Standardized uptake value (SUV) measurements provide semi-quantitative assessment; however, absolute SUV thresholds distinguishing inflammation from normal muscle are not universally established and vary with scanner, timing, glycemic status, and muscle activity. Careful patient preparation—fasting, avoiding strenuous exercise prior to scanning—is necessary because physiologic muscle uptake increases after exertion.

Occult malignancy screening: Dermatomyositis and some forms of polymyositis have epidemiologic associations with occult malignancy. PET/CT has higher sensitivity than conventional staging in detecting many malignancies and can identify primary tumors or metastatic disease in the abdomen, pelvis, and chest. PET can be particularly informative when initial cancer screening (mammography, colonoscopy, CT) is negative but clinical suspicion remains high. PET/CT may find hypermetabolic lymphadenopathy or small metastatic deposits that are occult on other modalities. However, PET has limited sensitivity for certain low-metabolic tumors (e.g., some prostate cancers, indolent lymphomas) and for small lesions below spatial resolution (~5–7 mm), so a negative PET does not entirely exclude malignancy.

Interpretation caveats: FDG uptake is not specific for malignancy; infections, inflammatory processes, recent surgical sites, and physiological uptake produce increased signal. In myositis patients, diffuse muscle uptake may complicate detection of adjacent malignancy due to increased background. False positives can result from recent vaccination, infections, or inflammatory arthropathies. Conversely, hyperglycemia reduces tracer uptake by tumors and inflammatory cells; blood glucose should be controlled (preferably <160 mg/dL) for optimal imaging. The timing of PET relative to onset of therapy matters: immunosuppression reduces inflammatory uptake and may decrease sensitivity for detecting active disease. Therefore, when the goal is to detect active inflammation or occult malignancy, obtaining PET prior to initiation of high-dose immunosuppression is ideal if clinically feasible.

Clinical integration and follow-up: PET/CT findings should be correlated with clinical examination, muscle enzymes (creatine kinase: typical normal range ~25–200 U/L, though lab-specific ranges vary), autoantibody profiles, and MRI findings. Focal hypermetabolic lesions identified on PET that could represent malignancy often require directed anatomic imaging, biopsy, or further endoscopic testing. PET can also be used to monitor treatment response in myositis: decreasing FDG uptake correlates with clinical improvement in many cases, but standardized criteria for response remain under development.

# Limitations, artifacts and combining modalities for diagnosis

Each imaging modality has strengths and limitations; optimal patient care requires integrating modalities, clinical data, laboratory findings, and sometimes histopathology. Understanding common artifacts and limitations avoids misinterpretation and guides appropriate use.

MRI limitations and artifacts: MRI is sensitive but not entirely specific. Motion artifact from patient movement or respiration can mimic or obscure pathology; susceptibility artifacts near metallic implants can distort images. Inhomogeneous fat suppression and poor field homogeneity can produce false areas of high signal. Gadolinium-based contrast agents are contraindicated in advanced renal failure due to the risk of nephrogenic systemic fibrosis with certain agents, and there is theoretical concern regarding gadolinium deposition with repeated dosing—particularly linear agents—so risk-benefit assessment is necessary. Time, cost, and availability limit MRI use in acute settings. Additionally, MRI may be poorly tolerated by claustrophobic patients or those with incompatible implants (some pacemakers), though many modern devices are MRI-conditional.

CT limitations and artifacts: CT is highly available and fast but involves ionizing radiation and iodinated contrast exposure. Beam-hardening artifacts from dense structures (e.g., dental fillings) can obscure adjacent soft tissues on neck CT. Motion artifacts and swallowing can blur images. CT has limited soft-tissue contrast compared with MRI, making differentiation of cellulitis versus early neoplasm more challenging. In the chest, CT may reveal non-specific findings such as ground-glass opacity that require clinical correlation.

PET limitations and artifacts: FDG is a glucose analog and accumulates in tissues with high glucose metabolism. Physiologic muscle uptake is increased after exercise, cold exposure, or anxiety. Brown adipose tissue can take up FDG in the neck and supraclavicular regions, mimicking pathological uptake. Spatial resolution limits detection of small lesions, and some malignancies and low-grade infections/inflammations may show low FDG avidity. PET/CT uses ionizing radiation and needs careful preparation to minimize false positives (fasting, avoid exercise, measure glucose). Cost and limited availability may restrict routine use.

Combining modalities for optimal diagnosis: Multimodality imaging leverages complementary strengths. MRI is preferred to evaluate muscle inflammation and to target biopsy, while CT is superior for rapid evaluation of deep neck spaces, airway compromise, and detection of gas or calcified structures. HRCT is indispensable for detailed lung parenchymal assessment and nodule characterization. FDG-PET/CT is useful when whole-body assessment is needed—either to detect widespread muscle involvement not covered by localized MRI, to screen for occult malignancy, or to identify metastatic disease.

A pragmatic approach is to tailor imaging to the clinical question. For a patient with suspected myositis and focal muscle symptoms, MRI of the symptomatic region with STIR/T2 and post-contrast T1 fat-suppressed sequences is first-line to document active inflammation and guide biopsy. If there are symptoms or signs suggesting deep neck infection (fever, severe odynophagia, trismus, airway compromise), contrast-enhanced CT neck is urgent to assess abscess and plan drainage. For respiratory symptoms, HRCT chest is essential to characterize ILD pattern and disease extent. If paraneoplastic myositis is a concern or initial cancer screening is unrevealing, FDG-PET/CT can be performed to evaluate the whole body for occult malignancy and to survey for widespread muscle inflammation. In complex cases, combining PET and targeted MRI provides both metabolic and anatomic detail: PET identifies hypermetabolic regions and MRI characterizes tissue architecture and guides biopsy.

Clinical decision thresholds and follow-up imaging: Use imaging findings to guide therapy and interventions. Presence of STIR hyperintensity and enhancement on MRI in multiple muscles supports starting or escalating immunosuppression and selecting biopsy site. HRCT patterns influence prognosis and choice of therapy for ILD: NSIP and OP patterns are more likely to respond to immunosuppression, whereas UIP may require antifibrotic strategies and has a poorer prognosis. On PET, a newly identified hypermetabolic lesion consistent with malignancy requires tissue diagnosis; a negative PET does not definitively exclude cancer, so age-appropriate and risk-adapted screening should continue.

In summary, imaging in myositis and neck pathology relies on understanding the pathophysiologic basis of signal changes across modalities. MRI best detects muscle inflammation and chronic replacement changes; CT is essential for neck emergency assessment and detailed chest parenchymal evaluation via HRCT; FDG-PET adds whole-body metabolic assessment for inflammation and malignancy screening. Awareness of limitations and artifacts—combined with clinical judgment and targeted use of complementary modalities—optimizes diagnostic accuracy and patient care.

Introduction

Inflammatory myopathies, classically including polymyositis, dermatomyositis, and the antisynthetase syndrome, are systemic autoimmune diseases in which skeletal muscle inflammation is the dominant feature. The lungs are a common and clinically important site of involvement. Pulmonary manifestations range from interstitial lung disease (ILD) with a spectrum of radiologic and histologic patterns to complications of bulbar and respiratory muscle weakness such as aspiration and respiratory failure. For a clinician evaluating a patient with myositis, understanding the patterns of myositis-associated ILD, the distinction between aspiration pneumonitis and aspiration pneumonia, interpretation of pulmonary function testing (PFTs) including features of extrathoracic (neuromuscular) restriction, and practical approaches to monitoring respiratory muscle weakness is essential. This chapter synthesizes pathophysiology and clinical reasoning relevant to these topics and summarizes management principles for ILD in the context of connective tissue disease.

# Types and radiologic patterns of myositis-associated ILD (NSIP, OP, UIP patterns)

Myositis-associated ILD encompasses several radiologic and histopathologic patterns, the most common being nonspecific interstitial pneumonia (NSIP) and organizing pneumonia (OP), with usual interstitial pneumonia (UIP) and mixed patterns occurring less commonly. Different patterns carry implications for prognosis and response to therapy, and chest imaging—principally high-resolution computed tomography (HRCT)—provides the foundation for classification in clinical practice.

Nonspecific interstitial pneumonia (NSIP) is the pattern most frequently observed in connective tissue disease-related ILD including myositis. On HRCT NSIP is characterized by ground-glass opacities and fine reticular abnormalities that are typically symmetric and predominantly basilar and peripheral but may demonstrate relative subpleural sparing. Traction bronchiectasis may be present in more fibrotic NSIP, but honeycombing is minimal or absent compared with UIP. NSIP represents a spectrum from cellular-predominant disease—where inflammation predominates and is more steroid-responsive—to fibrotic NSIP in which architectural distortion and fibrosis are present and the disease may be less reversible. In myositis, a cellular NSIP pattern often correlates with a better prognosis and higher likelihood of response to immunosuppression.

Organizing pneumonia (OP) (sometimes called bronchiolitis obliterans organizing pneumonia, BOOP) occurs in myositis patients either alone or superimposed on NSIP. HRCT features include patchy, often peribronchovascular and peripheral consolidations, frequently with a perilobular distribution and sometimes migratory behavior. Ground-glass opacity and small nodular consolidations are common. OP generally shows more rapid clinical symptoms and often a prompt clinical and radiologic response to corticosteroids, although relapses are common when steroids are tapered.

Usual interstitial pneumonia (UIP) is less characteristic of connective tissue disease than NSIP but can occur in myositis and is associated with a worse prognosis. HRCT shows reticular opacities with traction bronchiectasis and honeycombing, with a subpleural and basal predominance. Compared with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis (IPF), CTD-associated UIP may have a greater inflammatory component, but once established fibrosis with honeycombing is present, reversibility is limited.

Beyond these canonical patterns, myositis-associated ILD may show mixed patterns or atypical features including diffuse alveolar damage in fulminant presentations, or rapidly progressive interstitial pneumonia (RP-ILD), particularly associated with some autoantibodies (e.g., anti-MDA5). Clinical and serologic context influences pretest probability for specific patterns: antisynthetase antibodies (anti-Jo-1, PL-7, PL-12, EJ, OJ) are strongly associated with ILD (often NSIP or OP or mixed), whereas certain autoantibody profiles—anti-MDA5—are linked to rapidly progressive ILD with a high risk of respiratory failure.

When to suspect each pattern clinically follows from tempo and symptomatology: subacute dyspnea and cough over weeks to months with systemic myositis signs suggest NSIP or OP; rapidly progressive respiratory failure in days to weeks, with severe hypoxemia and diffuse bilateral consolidation, raises concern for diffuse alveolar damage or fulminant OP, and may require urgent aggressive immunosuppression. HRCT combined with PFTs and, when necessary, bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) or surgical lung biopsy are used to confirm the pattern and guide therapy. Multidisciplinary discussion with radiology and pathology improves diagnostic accuracy.

# Pathophysiology of aspiration pneumonitis vs aspiration pneumonia

Aspiration into the lower respiratory tract can result in two distinct but sometimes overlapping clinical entities: aspiration pneumonitis and aspiration pneumonia. Discriminating them is important because management differs—aspiration pneumonitis is often primarily an inflammatory chemical injury for which supportive care is the mainstay, whereas aspiration pneumonia is a bacterial infection requiring antimicrobial therapy.

Aspiration pneumonitis (Mendelson’s syndrome) refers to the acute chemical injury produced by inhalation of sterile gastric contents, especially acidic material. The pathophysiology is primarily direct caustic injury to the airway and alveolar epithelium. Acid causes denaturation of surfactant, disruption of alveolar epithelial and capillary endothelial integrity, and triggers an intense local inflammatory response characterized by neutrophil influx and cytokine release. Clinically, aspiration pneumonitis typically presents shortly after a witnessed or suspected aspiration event with sudden cough, dyspnea, hypoxemia, tachypnea, and potential development of acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) in severe cases. Fever and marked leukocytosis may be absent or minimal in the immediate post-aspiration period. Chest imaging initially may show focal airspace consolidation in dependent lobes (e.g., right lower lobe when aspiration occurs in upright patients), or diffuse bilateral alveolar infiltrates in more severe injury. The mainstay of management for aspiration pneumonitis is supportive: supplemental oxygen, airway protection, bronchodilators if bronchospasm is present, and close observation. Routine empiric antibiotics are not indicated unless there is clinical or microbiologic evidence suggesting bacterial infection or if the aspiration event was accompanied by or followed by fever, purulent sputum, or progressive infiltrates beyond the first 48 hours.

Aspiration pneumonia, by contrast, refers to a lung infection resulting from inhalation of oropharyngeal secretions that are colonized with pathogenic bacteria. Risk factors include impaired consciousness, impaired cough or swallow (bulbar dysfunction), poor oral hygiene, and hospitalization with exposure to multidrug-resistant organisms. Pathogenesis involves aspiration of bacteria-laden secretions into dependent airways where host defenses are overwhelmed, allowing microbial proliferation and host inflammatory response. The clinical course is often more indolent than chemical pneumonitis: fever, leukocytosis, productive cough, and consolidation on imaging develop over 24–72 hours. Empiric antibiotic therapy is indicated and should be chosen according to aspiration risk; community-acquired anaerobes and streptococci are typical in community cases, whereas gram-negative bacilli and Staphylococcus aureus are more likely in hospitalized patients or those with recent antibiotic exposure.

There is substantial clinical overlap: an initial chemical pneumonitis can be secondarily infected and become aspiration pneumonia, and presentation may be atypical in immunocompromised patients. In myositis, bulbar weakness predisposes to both acute aspiration events and chronic microaspiration, increasing risk for both entities. Clinical decision-making should integrate timing relative to aspiration, systemic inflammatory signs, purulence of secretions, radiographic progression after 48–72 hours, and microbiology from sputum or BAL when obtainable. In short: chemical pneumonitis equals sudden injury from sterile gastric contents requiring supportive care; aspiration pneumonia equals delayed bacterial infection requiring targeted antibiotics.

# Pulmonary function testing in restrictive vs obstructive disease and extrathoracic restriction

Pulmonary function testing (PFTs) provides objective quantification of ventilatory impairment and gas exchange abnormalities and is indispensable for diagnosing and monitoring both parenchymal lung disease and respiratory muscle dysfunction. Interpretation requires integration of spirometry, lung volumes (preferably measured by body plethysmography), and diffusing capacity for carbon monoxide (DLCO). Reference values are reported as percent predicted for age, sex, height, and ethnicity.

In intrinsic restrictive lung disease (parenchymal ILD), the hallmark PFT abnormalities are a reduced total lung capacity (TLC) and reduced forced vital capacity (FVC). TLC <80% predicted indicates restriction, and severity is often graded by FVC: mild restriction FVC 65–79% predicted, moderate 50–64%, severe <50%. The forced expiratory volume in 1 second (FEV1) is reduced proportionally to FVC, so the FEV1/FVC ratio is normal or increased (≥0.70 and often >0.80). Residual volume (RV) is reduced or normal. The single-breath DLCO is frequently reduced—in ILD due to thickening of the alveolar–capillary membrane and loss of alveolar surface area—and is a sensitive early marker. Normal DLCO ranges approximately 80–120% predicted; a DLCO <80% suggests impaired gas transfer, with DLCO <40% reflecting severe impairment. DLCO corrected for hemoglobin is more precise. Arterial blood gas may show hypoxemia at rest or with exertion, and significant desaturation with exertion is prognostically important.

In obstructive disease (e.g., COPD, asthma), TLC may be normal or increased due to air trapping, FEV1 is reduced more than FVC, and the FEV1/FVC ratio is decreased (<0.70 post-bronchodilator per many guidelines). The RV and RV/TLC ratio are typically increased. DLCO may be reduced in emphysema due to loss of alveolar-capillary surface area.

Extrathoracic restrictive physiology, as seen in neuromuscular disorders and respiratory muscle weakness in myositis, shares some spirometric features with parenchymal restriction but with important distinctions. Respiratory muscle weakness typically produces a reduction in maximal inspiratory and expiratory pressures, a decline in vital capacity (VC) that is often out of proportion to any parenchymal abnormalities, and a characteristic fall in VC when measured supine. In neuromuscular weakness, TLC and FRC may be normal or reduced depending on the severity and elasticity of the chest wall; the hallmark is reduced VC and preserved flow-volume curve shapes relative to intrinsic lung disease. The FEV1/FVC ratio is normal or increased because expiratory airflow is preserved relative to reduced lung volumes. Because the lung parenchyma is otherwise normal, DLCO may be preserved or only mildly reduced; a normal DLCO in the setting of low VC suggests extrathoracic restriction rather than intrinsic ILD.

Specific PFT maneuvers and thresholds useful in neuromuscular disease include maximal inspiratory pressure (MIP, also called PImax) and maximal expiratory pressure (MEP, PEmax). Normal MIP in healthy adults is roughly -80 to -120 cmH2O for men and -60 to -80 cmH2O for women; values depend on age and effort. Normal MEP is roughly 100–140 cmH2O for men and 80–100 cmH2O for women. Values less negative than these norms signify weakness; clinically significant inspiratory muscle weakness is often considered when MIP is less negative than -30 cmH2O to -60 cmH2O, depending on context and symptomatology. Sniff nasal inspiratory pressure (SNIP) is an alternative measure of inspiratory strength—values <40 cmH2O in adults suggest clinically important weakness, though normal ranges vary. Importantly, VC that falls by more than 10–20% when the patient moves from upright to supine is a sensitive indicator of diaphragmatic weakness; a supine VC decrease >20% is strongly suggestive of diaphragmatic dysfunction. Moreover, in suspected neuromuscular respiratory failure, arterial blood gas demonstrating hypercapnia (PaCO2 >45 mmHg) or nocturnal oximetry showing desaturation are signs of ventilatory failure warranting prompt intervention.

Decision thresholds used in monitoring ILD and neuromuscular weakness: a decline in FVC ≥10% predicted over time is generally considered clinically significant and associated with worse outcomes; a decline in FVC of 5–10% plus worsening symptoms or imaging can also indicate progression. For DLCO, a decline of ≥15% is often used as significant. In neuromuscular disease, progressive decline in MIP/MEP or development of hypercapnia or nocturnal hypoventilation indicates need for escalating respiratory support.

# Clinical assessment and monitoring of respiratory muscle weakness

In myositis, respiratory compromise arises both from involvement of respiratory muscles (diaphragm, intercostals) and from bulbar dysfunction causing impaired airway protection and aspiration. Clinical assessment requires careful history, physical examination, bedside tests, and formal pulmonary and sleep studies when indicated.

History should elicit dyspnea at rest and on exertion, orthopnea, morning headache, daytime somnolence, cough, dysphagia, choking episodes, weight loss, and decreased exercise tolerance. Orthopnea and dyspnea that worsen when supine suggest diaphragmatic weakness. Nocturnal symptoms—frequent awakenings, nonrestorative sleep, morning headaches, daytime somnolence—may reflect nocturnal hypoventilation. Bulbar symptoms—dysphagia, nasal regurgitation, or choking—imply increased risk of aspiration.

Physical examination may reveal shallow rapid breathing, use of accessory muscles, paradoxical abdominal movements during inspiration (diaphragmatic weakness), reduced chest wall expansion, and hypoventilation signs such as hypercapnic facies. Auscultation may be unremarkable unless aspiration or parenchymal disease is present.

Bedside objective tests include measurement of vital capacity (sitting and supine), maximal voluntary ventilation, cough peak flow, MIP and MEP, and SNIP. A cough peak flow <160 L/min is associated with ineffective airway clearance and higher risk for secretion retention and respiratory complications; values <270 L/min indicate impaired cough for some interventions. As noted above, a supine VC drop >10–20% strongly suggests diaphragmatic weakness. Spirometry alone cannot reliably distinguish neuromuscular restriction from parenchymal disease; the pattern of preserved flows with low volumes and normal DLCO, along with muscle testing, supports extrathoracic restriction.

Nocturnal monitoring with pulse oximetry or polysomnography is useful: nocturnal hypoventilation often precedes daytime hypercapnia, manifesting as sustained or frequent oxygen desaturations during sleep and morning hypercapnia. Arterial blood gas analysis provides definitive evidence of hypercapnic respiratory failure; rising PaCO2 indicates alveolar hypoventilation and progressive ventilatory failure. When clinical suspicion is high, early collaboration with respiratory therapy and consideration of noninvasive ventilation (NIV) trials are appropriate.

Frequency of monitoring depends on severity. Patients with established weakness or progressive decline should have serial spirometry and MIP/MEP measurements at regular intervals (for example, every 3 months in active disease or sooner if symptoms change). For those with mild disease and stable function, every 6–12 months may suffice. Any new orthopnea, morning headache, daytime somnolence, or decline in supine VC should prompt urgent reassessment.

# Management principles for ILD in connective tissue disease

Treating ILD associated with inflammatory myopathies requires integrating severity assessment, pattern recognition, and balancing immunosuppression to control inflammation against risks of therapy. Management principles emphasize early recognition, risk stratification, and individualized immunomodulatory therapy, with supportive care and adjunctive measures.

Initial evaluation and risk stratification should include HRCT to define pattern and extent of disease, baseline spirometry with TLC and DLCO, oxygenation assessment at rest and with exertion (pulse oximetry or 6-minute walk test), and measurement of serologies including myositis-specific antibodies (e.g., antisynthetase antibodies, anti-MDA5) that inform prognosis and likelihood of progression. Severity thresholds that guide treatment intensity include FVC <50% predicted, DLCO <40% predicted, rapid decline in FVC (≥10% over 6–12 months), or rapid clinical deterioration such as worsening hypoxemia. Rapidly progressive ILD (RP-ILD), often associated with anti-MDA5, is a medical emergency and warrants aggressive immunosuppression and consideration of intensive care support.

Immunosuppressive therapy is the mainstay for inflammatory (cellular) ILD. High-dose systemic corticosteroids (e.g., prednisone 0.5–1 mg/kg/day, sometimes with pulsed intravenous methylprednisolone in fulminant cases) are commonly used as initial therapy, particularly when imaging suggests cellular NSIP or OP. Because prolonged steroid monotherapy carries toxicity and relapse risk, steroid-sparing agents are introduced early: mycophenolate mofetil is frequently used because of tolerability and evidence supporting benefit in CTD-ILD; azathioprine is an alternative. For more severe or refractory disease, cyclophosphamide (often IV monthly pulses) has been used; calcineurin inhibitors such as tacrolimus or cyclosporine may be particularly effective in antisynthetase syndrome–associated ILD. Rituximab, a B-cell–depleting agent, has efficacy in refractory myositis-associated ILD and is often considered for steroid-refractory disease or when an antibody-mediated mechanism is suspected. Choice among agents depends on disease severity, comorbidities, reproductive considerations, infection risk, and local practice.

Antifibrotic therapy has an emerging role in connective tissue disease–associated progressive fibrosing ILD. Nintedanib, a tyrosine kinase inhibitor, demonstrated benefit in slowing FVC decline in progressive fibrosing ILD of various etiologies, including CTD-ILD, and may be considered for patients with progressive fibrotic phenotypes despite immunosuppression. Pirfenidone has less robust data in CTD-ILD. Antifibrotics do not replace immunosuppression when active inflammation is present but are adjunctive when fibrosis advances despite control of inflammation.

Supportive measures are equally important. Supplemental oxygen for resting or exertional hypoxemia improves symptoms and may reduce pulmonary hypertension risk. Pulmonary rehabilitation improves exercise tolerance and quality of life. Vaccination against influenza and pneumococcus is recommended. For patients with significant respiratory muscle weakness and nocturnal hypoventilation, noninvasive ventilation can stabilize gas exchange and improve daytime symptoms. Early referral for lung transplantation evaluation is appropriate for patients with progressive disease despite optimal medical therapy and who meet transplant criteria.

Monitoring response to therapy relies on serial clinical assessment, PFTs (notably FVC and DLCO), 6-minute walk distance and oxygen saturation, and follow-up HRCT when clinically indicated. Clinically significant progression is commonly defined as FVC decline ≥10% predicted over 6–12 months, or an FVC decline of 5–10% combined with worsening symptoms or imaging; DLCO decline ≥15% over the same interval is also significant. Decisions to escalate therapy, add steroid-sparing agents, transition to biologic therapy, or introduce antifibrotic therapy are guided by these objective measures and the clinical course.

Infectious risk is a major consideration given immunosuppressive regimens. Baseline screening for latent infections (e.g., tuberculosis, hepatitis B/C) and prophylaxis (e.g., Pneumocystis jirovecii prophylaxis when using high-dose steroids and additional immunosuppression) should be individualized. Drug toxicities necessitate surveillance (e.g., cytopenias, hepatic dysfunction, renal toxicity), and vaccination status should be optimized before intensive immunosuppression.

Finally, management of aspiration risk and respiratory muscle weakness in myositis may overlap with ILD care. Bulbar dysfunction requires speech and swallow evaluation, dietary modification, and possibly enteral feeding in severe dysphagia. Preventing aspiration reduces pulmonary complications and supports pulmonary rehabilitation and recovery.

Conclusion

Pulmonary complications of inflammatory myopathies encompass a broad spectrum from inflammatory, potentially reversible ILD patterns (NSIP, OP) to fibrotic UIP-like disease, along with the mechanical and infectious consequences of bulbar and respiratory muscle dysfunction. Accurate radiologic characterization, careful PFT interpretation distinguishing intrinsic parenchymal restriction from extrathoracic neuromuscular restriction, and vigilant monitoring for aspiration and respiratory failure are central to clinical care. Management integrates immunosuppression targeting inflammation, antifibrotic therapy for progressive fibrosis, supportive measures including oxygen and pulmonary rehabilitation, and proactive strategies to prevent and treat aspiration. Early multidisciplinary collaboration among rheumatology, pulmonology, radiology, speech/swallow specialists, and, when needed, critical care or transplant teams optimizes outcomes for patients with myositis-associated pulmonary disease.

Introduction

Angioedema is localized, transient swelling of the deeper dermis, subcutaneous tissue, mucosa or submucosa resulting from increased vascular permeability and extravasation of fluid into the interstitial space. Although clinically recognizable, angioedema is heterogeneous in mechanism, natural history and therapeutic responsiveness. A precise mechanistic classification—histamine-mediated (mast cell–dependent), bradykinin-mediated, hereditary (C1 inhibitor [C1-INH] defects) and acquired C1-INH dysfunction—directly informs acute management, prophylaxis and diagnostic testing. This chapter reviews the pathophysiology that underlies these forms, clarifies clinical clues that separate them from each other and from inflammatory or infectious “pseudoangioedema,” outlines relevant laboratory testing (with practical interpretation), and highlights emergency airway priorities and evidence-based acute therapies.

# Mediators of angioedema: histamine vs bradykinin and implications for treatment

Two broad mediator pathways account for most clinically encountered angioedema: histamine-mediated (mast cell–dependent) and bradykinin-mediated angioedema. Their pathophysiology, clinical presentation, diagnostic tests and therapeutic responses differ substantially.

Histamine-mediated angioedema arises from activation and degranulation of mast cells and basophils with release of histamine, tryptase, prostaglandins and other mediators. Mechanisms include IgE-mediated allergic reactions to foods, drugs, insect stings and biologic agents, direct mast cell activators (e.g., radiocontrast, opiates), and nonimmune mechanisms. Histamine increases microvascular permeability by acting on endothelial H1 and H2 receptors and causes vasodilation and pruritus. Clinically, histamine-mediated angioedema tends to have: rapid onset (minutes to hours), often associated urticaria (hives), pruritus, flushing, and other signs of systemic anaphylaxis (hypotension, bronchospasm). Tryptase, a marker of mast cell degranulation, is often elevated acutely. Importantly, these episodes typically respond to epinephrine, H1/H2 antihistamines and systemic glucocorticoids.

Bradykinin-mediated angioedema results from excessive generation or impaired degradation of bradykinin, a potent vasodilator and endothelial permeability mediator that acts predominantly at bradykinin B2 receptors. Bradykinin induces increased vascular permeability without histamine-mediated symptoms such as urticaria and pruritus. Common contexts include hereditary C1-INH deficiency (see below), acquired C1-INH deficiency (consumption or autoantibodies), and drug-induced forms related to inhibition of bradykinin breakdown (notably angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors [ACEi]). Bradykinin-mediated attacks are frequently characterized by slower onset (hours), prominent submucosal edema (including laryngeal and gastrointestinal mucosa causing pain and vomiting), recurrent abdominal pain mimicking surgical abdomen, and poor or absent response to epinephrine, antihistamines and steroids. Tryptase is usually normal.

Therapeutic implications are decisive. For suspected histamine-mediated angioedema or anaphylaxis, immediate intramuscular epinephrine (0.3–0.5 mg of 1:1000 solution in adults), oxygen, airway support and H1/H2 antihistamines plus glucocorticoids are the first-line measures. For bradykinin-mediated angioedema, these measures are generally ineffective; targeted therapies that inhibit bradykinin production or block its receptor are required. Effective agents include plasma-derived or recombinant C1-INH concentrate (replacement therapy), icatibant (a selective bradykinin B2 receptor antagonist; 30 mg subcutaneously single dose), and kallikrein inhibitors such as ecallantide (30 mg subcutaneously). Fresh frozen plasma (FFP) contains C1-INH and kallikrein inhibitors and can be used when specific agents are unavailable, although theoretical and occasional paradoxical worsening may occur. Recognizing the mediator is therefore essential because empiric, time-critical airway interventions differ little, but pharmacologic therapy differs fundamentally.

# Hereditary angioedema (C1 inhibitor deficiency) and acquired C1-inhibitor dysfunction

Hereditary angioedema (HAE) is a genetic disorder characterized by recurrent episodes of bradykinin-mediated angioedema. Classic HAE results from deficiency or dysfunction of C1-INH (serpin that inhibits C1r/C1s of complement classical pathway and plasma kallikrein and factor XII in the contact system). There are three major HAE phenotypes relevant to testing and management:

- Type I HAE: Reduced quantitative C1-INH levels (approximately 85% of cases). Low antigenic C1-INH and low functional activity.
- Type II HAE: Normal or elevated antigenic C1-INH but reduced functional activity due to dysfunctional protein (approximately 15% of cases).
- "HAE with normal C1-INH" (historically Type III): Normal C1-INH antigen and function; several subtypes are described, some associated with mutations in factor XII (FXII), plasminogen, angiopoietin-1, or other genes. This phenotype is less well understood and may be estrogen-sensitive.

Pathophysiology in C1-INH deficiency: unregulated activation of plasma kallikrein leads to increased conversion of high-molecular-weight kininogen (HMWK) to bradykinin. The result is episodic bradykinin surge causing localized endothelial gap formation and edema. Laryngeal involvement is a feared complication with the potential for fatal airway obstruction. Abdominal attacks with severe colicky pain, vomiting and diarrhea are common and reflect bowel wall edema.

Acquired C1-INH deficiency is phenotypically similar but not hereditary. Mechanisms include increased consumption of C1-INH (for example, in lymphoproliferative disorders) or autoantibody-mediated neutralization of C1-INH; these are most often seen in middle-aged or older adults. Clinically, acquired C1-INH deficiency tends to be associated with underlying malignancy or autoimmune disease. Measurement of C1q protein is useful because C1q is often reduced in acquired forms (reflecting classical pathway activation), whereas it is typically normal in hereditary C1-INH deficiency.

Clinical evaluation should include family history, age of onset (HAE often presents in childhood or adolescence; acquired disease presents later), recurrent abdominal pain, lack of response to antihistamines/steroids and absence of urticaria. Management principles overlap with bradykinin-mediated therapy: on-demand treatment with C1-INH concentrate, icatibant or kallikrein inhibitors; prophylactic strategies for frequent attacks (e.g., attenuated androgens, regular C1-INH replacement, or monoclonal kallikrein inhibitors such as lanadelumab). In pregnancy, attenuated androgens are contraindicated and C1-INH concentrates are preferred for prophylaxis and acute therapy.

# Drug-induced angioedema (ACE inhibitors, DPP-4 inhibitors, NSAIDs)

Medications are a common and clinically important cause of angioedema. The three categories of medications most relevant to clinical practice are ACE inhibitors, dipeptidyl peptidase-4 (DPP-4) inhibitors and nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs). The mechanisms and clinical fingerprints differ.

ACE inhibitor–associated angioedema is a bradykinin-mediated process. ACE (kininase II) degrades bradykinin and substance P; ACE inhibition decreases bradykinin breakdown and can lead to accumulation. ACE inhibitor angioedema typically involves facial, perioral, tongue and laryngeal swelling and may present after days to years of therapy—onset may be delayed and unpredictable. There is no urticaria or pruritus, and routine antihistamine/steroid/epinephrine therapy is often ineffective. Management requires immediate cessation of the ACE inhibitor. Targeted therapies for bradykinin-mediated angioedema (icatibant, C1-INH, kallikrein inhibitors) are effective in many cases. There is no cross-reactivity between ACE inhibitors and angiotensin receptor blockers (ARBs) regarding bradykinin, so ARBs are generally safe alternatives, though caution is reasonable in individual cases.

DPP-4 inhibitors (gliptins) used in diabetes can predispose to angioedema, particularly when combined with ACE inhibitors. DPP-4 degrades several vasoactive peptides including bradykinin and substance P; inhibition can therefore prolong their activity and potentially contribute to angioedema. The phenotype overlaps with bradykinin-mediated angioedema: mucosal swelling without urticaria. Clinical vigilance is warranted when prescribing DPP-4 inhibitors to patients on ACE inhibitors.

NSAIDs provoke angioedema through a predominately non–IgE-mediated, pseudoallergic mechanism. In susceptible individuals, cyclooxygenase (COX) inhibition shunts arachidonic acid metabolism toward leukotriene production, promoting bronchospasm and increased vascular permeability. NSAID-induced angioedema frequently occurs in patients with chronic urticaria and aspirin-exacerbated respiratory disease (AERD). Presentation commonly includes urticaria, pruritus and sometimes respiratory symptoms; response to antihistamines may be partial. Avoidance of implicated NSAIDs and use of COX-2 selective agents with caution (and under specialist guidance) are typical strategies.

Recognizing the offending drug is essential: immediate discontinuation is indicated for ACE inhibitors and DPP-4 inhibitors if angioedema is suspected. For NSAID-induced reactions, discontinuation and substitution with safer analgesics are appropriate. Preventive strategies include counseling patients about recurrence risk and ensuring alternative medications are provided.

# Clinical clues to angioedema vs pseudoangioedema from inflammatory edema

Distinguishing true angioedema from other causes of localized swelling—termed pseudoangioedema—is critical. Pseudoangioedema includes infectious cellulitis, contact dermatitis, venous/lymphatic obstruction, inferior vena cava syndrome, or inflammatory edema from autoimmune disease. Misclassification can lead to inappropriate therapy and missed diagnoses (for example, failure to treat sepsis or a surgical abdomen).

Key clinical differentiators:

- Onset and speed: Histamine-mediated angioedema typically develops over minutes to hours. Bradykinin-mediated attacks often evolve over hours. Infectious cellulitis generally develops over days.
- Associated cutaneous findings: Urticaria (wheals) and pruritus strongly favor histamine-mediated angioedema. Absence of urticaria with painless swelling argues for bradykinin-mediated angioedema. Redness with warmth, tenderness, and induration are more consistent with cellulitis or localized inflammatory processes.
- Laterality and distribution: Angioedema is often asymmetric but more limited to dependent sites of mast cell activation or areas of bradykinin effect; venous/lymphatic obstruction often causes progressive dependent swelling with pitting edema. Facial angioedema frequently affects periorbital, perioral and tongue tissues; generalized nonpitting swelling is typical of angioedema. Pitting suggests fluid overload or venous/lymphatic etiologies rather than angioedema.
- Systemic signs: Fever, elevated inflammatory markers and leukocytosis support infectious or inflammatory etiologies. Rapid bronchospasm, hypotension, and mucocutaneous signs of anaphylaxis point to histamine-mediated pathology.
- Response to therapy: Rapid resolution with epinephrine, antihistamines and corticosteroids supports histamine-mediated etiology. Lack of response to these agents suggests bradykinin-mediated angioedema or non–mast cell causes.
- History: Recurrent episodic swelling with family history suggests hereditary angioedema. Medication history (ACEi, DPP-4 inhibitor, NSAID) is vital. Association with malignancy or autoimmune disease may suggest acquired C1-INH deficiency.

Clinical reasoning must integrate these observations. For example, a patient with painless abdominal pain and recurrent episodes of colicky pain with normal inflammatory markers and no dermatologic signs suggests bradykinin-mediated HAE. Conversely, a febrile, tender, erythematous, warm swollen limb that is pitting is more likely cellulitis or deep venous thrombosis.

# Laboratory tests: C3/C4, C1 esterase inhibitor level and function, tryptase, complement activation markers

Laboratory evaluation should be directed by the suspected mechanism. Timing and sample handling matter for interpretation.

Complement testing for suspected C1-INH deficiency:
- Serum complement C4: In C1-INH deficiency (both hereditary and acquired) the C4 level is typically reduced even between attacks because uncontrolled classical pathway activation consumes C4. A low C4 is a sensitive screening test. Typical adult reference range for C4 is approximately 10–40 mg/dL (0.1–0.4 g/L), though ranges vary by laboratory; many reference labs consider values below the lower limit as abnormal. In suspected HAE, a markedly low C4 is highly supportive.
- C1q: Useful to differentiate hereditary from acquired C1-INH deficiency. C1q is typically normal in hereditary disease but often decreased in acquired forms (due to immune complex–mediated classical pathway activation). No universal numeric cutoff eliminates the need to use local lab reference ranges; interpret relative to laboratory normals.
- C3: Usually normal in HAE because the classical pathway activation primarily consumes C4; C3 levels are often within normal limits (reference ~90–180 mg/dL). Decreased C3 suggests other complement-mediated diseases.
- C1-INH antigenic level and functional assay: Two key measurements are required to define HAE type. Antigenic C1-INH (quantity) is measured by immunoassay (reference ranges vary; typical antigenic C1-INH may be reported as 21–39 mg/dL or in mg/L depending on lab). Functional C1-INH activity is measured by functional assays (chromogenic or ELISA-based); normal activity is generally >50–70% (exact cutoffs depend on assay). In Type I HAE both antigenic level and function are low; in Type II antigen is normal or elevated while function is low. Testing should be performed twice on separate occasions for confirmation because acute-phase events can transiently alter levels and replacement therapy can confound results.
- Anti–C1-INH autoantibodies: In suspected acquired C1-INH deficiency, testing for anti–C1-INH antibodies is appropriate where available.

Practical testing strategy:
- First-line screening: serum C4; if low, measure C1-INH antigenic level and C1-INH functional activity.
- Include C1q when acquired deficiency is suspected (middle-age onset, associated autoimmune or lymphoproliferative disease).
- Obtain samples before giving C1-INH concentrate or FFP for diagnostic clarity; replacement may normalize levels and obscure diagnosis.

Mast cell activation testing:
- Serum tryptase: Tryptase is released from mast cells and is a useful marker of systemic mast cell activation/anaphylaxis. For suspected anaphylaxis, draw a sample as early as possible, ideally within 1–3 hours of symptom onset; obtain a baseline sample at least 24 hours later for comparison. An acute increase of tryptase by greater than $1.2\times$ baseline plus $2\ \mathrm{ng/mL}$ (i.e., acute tryptase > baseline $\times 1.2 + 2$ ng/mL) is often used to support mast cell activation. Typical normal reference upper limit is approximately 11.4 ng/mL but varies across labs. Chronic elevations in baseline tryptase (>11.4 ng/mL) suggest mastocytosis or baseline mast cell burden.
- Histamine levels are less practical because histamine is cleared rapidly.

Complement activation markers:
- In research or specialized centers, markers of complement activation such as C4a, C1r/C1s complexes, and terminal complement complex (sC5b-9) can be measured; these are not routinely necessary for initial clinical management but can inform pathophysiology in complex or research cases.

Other labs:
- When infectious or inflammatory pseudoangioedema is considered, check CBC, inflammatory markers (CRP, ESR), blood cultures and imaging as indicated to evaluate for cellulitis or deep-seated infection.
- In suspected ACEi-induced angioedema, routine complement testing is usually not required acutely unless recurrent or atypical features prompt investigation for underlying C1-INH dysfunction.

# Emergency airway considerations and acute management

Airway compromise is the most life-threatening complication of angioedema. Early recognition and a low threshold for securing the airway are essential. The approach differs little between histamine- and bradykinin-mediated angioedema with respect to airway management steps, but pharmacologic adjuncts differ.

Assessment and monitoring:
- Evaluate for stridor, voice change (dysphonia, hoarseness), difficulty swallowing (dysphagia), drooling, progressive tongue or floor-of-mouth edema, dyspnea, hypoxia, respiratory distress, and work of breathing. Laryngoscopic examination (flexible nasopharyngolaryngoscopy) when available can visualize laryngeal edema and guide timing of intervention.
- Patients with progressive oropharyngeal edema, rapid symptom evolution, or signs of impending obstruction should be managed proactively—prefer early airway control rather than waiting for complete obstruction.

Airway strategies:
- Prepare and mobilize resources: skilled airway operator (anesthesiologist/ENT/critical care), suction, difficult airway cart, video or flexible fiberoptic laryngoscope, surgical airway equipment, and operating room availability if possible.
- Intubation approach: Awake fiberoptic naso- or orotracheal intubation is often preferred when feasible and when the patient can cooperate, because it preserves spontaneous breathing and allows visualization of the airway through edematous tissues. Topical anesthesia and sedation must be used cautiously.
- If fiberoptic intubation is not feasible, prepare for rapid sequence intubation with experienced personnel; use video laryngoscopy as a first-line tool if available. However, there can be situations where visualization remains difficult because of severe edema.
- Surgical airway: Emergency cricothyrotomy (or tracheostomy if expert teams are available) must be considered when intubation fails or anatomical distortion precludes endotracheal establishment of the airway. There should be no hesitation to proceed to surgical airway in a patient with impending complete airway obstruction.

Acute pharmacologic management:
- Histamine-mediated angioedema/anaphylaxis: administer intramuscular epinephrine promptly (adult dose 0.3–0.5 mg IM of 1:1000 into lateral thigh). Supplemental oxygen, intravenous fluids for hypotension, nebulized bronchodilators for bronchospasm, H1 antihistamines (e.g., diphenhydramine 25–50 mg IV), H2 antihistamines (e.g., famotidine 20 mg IV) and systemic glucocorticoids (e.g., methylprednisolone 125 mg IV) are adjuncts. Epinephrine is life-saving for anaphylaxis; do not delay for diagnostics.
- Bradykinin-mediated angioedema: epinephrine, antihistamines and steroids are usually ineffective. When bradykinin-mediated angioedema is suspected (HAE, ACEi-related), institution of targeted therapy is indicated:
  - C1-INH concentrate (plasma-derived or recombinant): dosing varies by product; for Berinert (plasma-derived C1-INH), recommended dose for acute HAE attack is 20 U/kg IV. Ruconest (recombinant C1-INH) dosing and product specifics vary—follow local product guidance.
  - Icatibant: bradykinin B2 receptor antagonist; adult dose 30 mg subcutaneous once (some patients may require repeat dosing per protocol or clinical response).
