  - Ecallantide: kallikrein inhibitor; 30 mg subcutaneous (given as 3 subcutaneous injections of 10 mg each); requires monitoring for anaphylaxis, and is available under restricted programs in some jurisdictions.
  - Fresh frozen plasma (FFP): contains C1-INH and can be used when specific agents are unavailable. Typical dosing is 10–15 mL/kg (approx. 1–2 units adult), but institutional protocols vary. Be aware of potential for transient worsening from additional complement substrates in some cases; weigh risks and benefits.
  - For ACEi-induced angioedema, targeted therapies (icatibant and C1-INH) have been used successfully; evidence is mixed in randomized studies but case series and guidelines support their use in severe cases. Immediate cessation of the ACE inhibitor is required.
- Adjunctive measures: sedatives and airway maneuvers as indicated. For severe laryngeal edema, early otolaryngology involvement and consideration of elective surgical airway in controlled setting are advised.

Disposition and observation:
- Patients with oropharyngeal involvement, progressive symptoms, or requirement for advanced airway interventions should be admitted to an intermediate or intensive care unit for close monitoring. Observation periods depend on etiology and severity; for histamine-mediated reactions, observation of 4–24 hours may suffice if symptoms resolve. For bradykinin-mediated attacks, variable durations of observation are necessary depending on response to targeted therapy and the risk of recurrence.

Prophylaxis and longer-term management:
- For patients with recurrent HAE attacks, discuss short-term prophylaxis for procedures that might provoke attacks (dental work, intubation, surgical procedures)—administer C1-INH concentrate prior to the procedure when indicated. Long-term prophylaxis options include regular C1-INH replacement (IV or subcutaneous products), attenuated androgens (danazol, stanozolol) with consideration of adverse effects and contraindications (e.g., pregnancy, liver disease, lipid abnormalities), antifibrinolytics (e.g., tranexamic acid) with modest efficacy, and newer therapies such as lanadelumab (monoclonal antibody against kallikrein) 300 mg subcutaneously every 2–4 weeks for prophylaxis. Treatment selection should be individualized based on attack frequency, severity, comorbidities, pregnancy status and patient preference.

Summary of critical decision points:
- Rapidly determine whether airway is at risk—if yes, proceed toward securing a protected airway early with all appropriate preparations.
- Differentiate likely mediator (histamine vs bradykinin) based on history, physical exam and response to initial therapy; start epinephrine for suspected anaphylaxis without delay.
- For bradykinin-mediated suspicion (e.g., ACEi use, recurrent nonpruritic swelling, abdominal pain, family history), prioritize targeted bradykinin-directed therapy (C1-INH, icatibant, ecallantide) and avoid relying solely on antihistamines/steroids.

Concluding remarks

Angioedema encompasses distinct mechanistic categories that require different diagnostic tests and therapies. Careful clinical assessment—focusing on onset, associated symptoms (urticaria, pruritus, abdominal pain), medication history and family history—guides immediate management and appropriate laboratory evaluation. Complement testing (C4, C1-INH antigenic level and function, and C1q when needed) is pivotal in suspected hereditary or acquired C1-INH dysfunction. In acute settings, protect the airway and provide mediator-appropriate pharmacotherapy: epinephrine and antihistamines for histamine-mediated disease; C1-INH replacement, bradykinin B2 receptor antagonists and kallikrein inhibitors for bradykinin-mediated disease. Familiarity with the distinguishing features and treatment algorithms reduces morbidity and prevents fatal airway complications.

Introduction

Neck swelling is a common clinical presentation with a broad differential that ranges from minor self-limited conditions to life-threatening infections and airway emergencies. Deep neck space infections (DNSIs) and Ludwig's angina are paradigmatic examples in which rapid recognition of pathogenesis, early airway assessment, appropriate antimicrobial therapy, and timely surgical intervention can be lifesaving. This chapter reviews the etiology and pathophysiology of DNSIs, highlights the clinical features and red flags of Ludwig's angina and retropharyngeal infections, summarizes the microbiology and sepsis considerations, provides guidance on imaging and indications for surgical drainage, explains antimicrobial and airway-management principles, and outlines the clinical approach to distinguishing infectious from non-infectious causes of neck swelling.

# Etiology and pathogenesis of deep neck space infections (odontogenic, tonsillar, salivary)

Deep neck space infections arise when microbial invasion occurs within the potential spaces and fascial planes of the neck. The neck contains three layers of deep cervical fascia—superficial (investing), middle (pretracheal), and deep (prevertebral)—which compartmentalize the neck into clinically relevant spaces. Key spaces include the submandibular (further divided into sublingual and submylohyoid compartments, relevant to Ludwig's angina), parapharyngeal, retropharyngeal, peritonsillar, and the so-called "danger space" between the alar and prevertebral fascia extending to the posterior mediastinum. Infection in one anatomical compartment tends to follow predictable routes along these fascial planes and may extend contiguously to adjacent spaces, the mediastinum, or thorax.

Odontogenic sources are the most common cause of DNSIs. Dental caries and periapical abscesses—especially infections of the mandibular molars—can breach cortical bone and spread into the submandibular or sublingual spaces depending on the relationship between the mylohyoid muscle and the tooth roots. Infection below the mylohyoid muscle tends to spread into the submandibular space; above it, into the sublingual space. Bilateral extension across the midline predisposes to Ludwig's angina, a potentially fulminant cellulitis of the bilateral submandibular spaces.

Tonsillar and peritonsillar infections are the second common source of DNSIs. Peritonsillar abscesses (quinsy) can extend to the parapharyngeal space, then to the retropharyngeal space. Lymphadenitis from upper respiratory infections may also seed adjacent deep spaces. Salivary gland infections—sialadenitis from stasis (e.g., dehydration), stone obstruction, or ascending infection—can cause local cellulitis and abscess formation in the submandibular or parotid regions and may progress to the parapharyngeal space.

Pathophysiologically, DNSIs progress through stages: mucosal breach or local infection → cellulitis and phlegmon where soft tissues are inflamed and indurated → localized abscess when liquefaction and pus formation occur. Factors that increase risk for aggressive or atypical spread include immunosuppression (e.g., diabetes mellitus, malignancy, chronic corticosteroid use), delayed treatment, virulent organisms, and anatomical variants. The presence of gas-forming organisms or extension into the "danger space" can allow rapid descent into the posterior mediastinum, causing descending necrotizing mediastinitis with high mortality if not recognized early.

# Clinical signs and red flags of Ludwig's angina and retropharyngeal infections

Ludwig's angina describes a rapidly progressive, bilateral cellulitis of the submandibular, sublingual, and submental spaces that classically arises from infected mandibular molars. The hallmark clinical presentation is a firm, "wooden" swelling of the floor of the mouth and bilateral submandibular areas that is often painful. Because the sublingual and submandibular spaces are contiguous, tissue edema elevates the tongue, compromising the oropharyngeal airway. Typical findings include submandibular fullness, elevation of the tongue, drooling, difficulty handling secretions, muffled "hot potato" voice, trismus (limited mouth opening), neck stiffness, and sometimes odynophagia or dysphagia. Importantly, skin erythema may be minimal early because the infection is deep and subcutaneous tissue is tightly bound; thus the absence of marked erythema does not exclude severe disease. Key red flags for imminent airway compromise are stridor (high-pitched inspiratory sound), progressive dyspnea, inability to lie supine, progressive hypoxia, difficulty handling secretions, and rapidly progressive swelling over hours. Any of these signs warrants immediate airway evaluation by experienced personnel.

Retropharyngeal infections typically present with sore throat, fever, neck pain, dysphagia, odynophagia, and often neck stiffness. In children, torticollis is common because retropharyngeal lymph nodes are larger and can become inflamed, leading to head tilt. Odynophagia and drooling may point to oropharyngeal or posterior pharyngeal involvement. Red flags for retropharyngeal abscess include muffled voice, stridor, respiratory distress, signs of sepsis (hypotension, tachycardia, altered mental status), and signs of mediastinal involvement such as chest pain or shortness of breath. Because the retropharyngeal space communicates with the posterior mediastinum via the danger space, extension to the mediastinum with pleural effusion or empyema is a feared complication.

Both Ludwig's and retropharyngeal infections may progress rapidly and unpredictably; therefore, a high index of suspicion and frequent reassessment—especially of airway patency—are essential. Examination should include assessment of vital signs, oxygenation, oral cavity and oropharynx inspection (tongue position, floor of mouth), palpation of cervical lymph nodes, evaluation of trismus, and observation for drooling, voice changes, or respiratory compromise.

# Microbiology, sepsis considerations and indications for imaging and surgical drainage

Microbiology
Deep neck infections are typically polymicrobial, reflecting oral flora. Common aerobic organisms include viridans group streptococci (Streptococcus anginosus/milleri group), Streptococcus pyogenes, Staphylococcus aureus (including methicillin-resistant strains in selected populations), and various gram-negative bacilli. Anaerobes are important and frequently dominant contributors: Prevotella, Porphyromonas, Fusobacterium, and Bacteroides species are common. Odontogenic infections are particularly enriched for anaerobes. In severe or hospital-acquired cases, Pseudomonas and other gram-negatives may be encountered. Culture specimens obtained during drainage (aerobic and anaerobic cultures) guide targeted therapy; blood cultures should be obtained in cases with systemic signs or sepsis.

Sepsis considerations
Patients with DNSIs can develop systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS) or sepsis. Early recognition of sepsis and prompt resuscitation are critical. Clinical tools such as qSOFA (quick Sequential Organ Failure Assessment: altered mental status, systolic blood pressure ≤100 mmHg, respiratory rate ≥22/min) can help identify patients at higher risk of poor outcomes; a qSOFA score ≥2 is associated with increased mortality and mandates prompt evaluation. Serum lactate is an important marker of tissue hypoperfusion: normal lactate is typically 0.5–2.2 mmol/L; lactate >2 mmol/L indicates increased risk and >4 mmol/L suggests severe sepsis/septic shock though interpretation must include the clinical context. Basic labs include complete blood count (normal WBC 4–11 ×10^9/L), electrolytes, renal and liver function, CRP and ESR (markers of inflammation), and blood cultures prior to antibiotic administration when feasible.

Indications for imaging
Imaging is instrumental for diagnosis, delineation of the anatomic extent, and surgical planning. Contrast-enhanced computed tomography (CT) of the neck is the modality of choice in most adults because it delineates soft-tissue planes, loculated collections, gas formation, and extension to the mediastinum. CT with intravenous contrast is preferred to distinguish enhancing inflamed tissues from non-enhancing fluid collections (abscesses). In children or when radiation exposure is a concern, magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) can provide excellent soft-tissue contrast but has limited availability and requires longer acquisition times; MRI is particularly useful when evaluating for vascular complications or suspected osteomyelitis. Ultrasound is useful for superficial collections and for guiding aspiration, especially in the anterior triangle or superficial submandibular collections, but is operator-dependent and limited for deep or parapharyngeal/retropharyngeal spaces. Plain lateral neck radiographs may show prevertebral soft tissue swelling in retropharyngeal infections; as a guide, prevertebral soft tissue thickness should be small in adults (at C2 usually <7 mm, at C6 <20 mm), though radiographs are insensitive and should not replace CT when there is clinical concern.

Indications for surgical drainage
Not all DNSIs require immediate incision and drainage; early infections may be managed with antibiotics and close observation. However, specific indications for surgical intervention include: radiographic evidence of a well-formed abscess (discrete fluid collection with rim enhancement on CT), clinical fluctuance or a palpable collection amenable to drainage, airway compromise or progressive symptoms despite adequate antibiotics, loculated collections or extension to deep spaces such as parapharyngeal or retropharyngeal spaces, large size (often >2–3 cm in one dimension or causing mass effect), and presence of gas-forming organisms or descending mediastinitis. Failure to improve after 24–48 hours of appropriate antimicrobial therapy also suggests need for drainage. Surgical approaches are dictated by the space involved: transoral drainage for some peritonsillar or superior retropharyngeal collections, transcervical external approaches for parapharyngeal or lateral collections, and combined cervical and thoracic surgery when descending mediastinitis is present. Cultures obtained at drainage help tailor antibiotics.

# Antimicrobial therapy choices and airway management strategies

Antimicrobial therapy
Empiric antimicrobial regimens must cover streptococci, staphylococci, and anaerobes originating from the oral cavity. Choice of regimen is influenced by severity, local resistance patterns, patient drug allergies, and risk factors for MRSA or gram-negative pathogens.

- For many community-acquired DNSIs in non-severely ill patients, an appropriate empiric parenteral regimen is ampicillin-sulbactam (e.g., 1.5–3 g IV every 6–8 hours) because it covers common gram-positive cocci and anaerobes. An alternative is ertapenem (1 g IV daily) in selected cases when broader anaerobic coverage is desirable and Pseudomonas coverage is not needed.

- For severe infections or hospital-acquired cases where broader gram-negative coverage is required, piperacillin-tazobactam (e.g., 3.375–4.5 g IV every 6–8 hours) provides extended coverage. If Pseudomonas is a concern, anti-pseudomonal beta-lactams should be selected.

- If MRSA risk is present (recent hospitalization, IV drug use, known colonization), add vancomycin with dosing guided by troughs or AUC monitoring; alternatives for MRSA coverage include linezolid (oral/IV) depending on the clinical scenario.

- For patients with immediate severe beta-lactam allergy, clindamycin (600–900 mg IV every 8 hours) provides gram-positive and anaerobic coverage but increasing clindamycin resistance among gram-negative organisms must be considered; combination with a fluoroquinolone may sometimes be used to cover gram-negative rods, but fluoroquinolones have adverse-effect considerations.

Duration of therapy is individualized. For infections treated with drainage and clinical improvement, 10–14 days of total therapy is common, sometimes transitioning to oral agents once improvement is clear. Complicated infections (mediastinitis, osteomyelitis) often require longer courses (4–6 weeks).

Airway management strategies
Airway compromise is the most feared and immediate threat with Ludwig’s angina and retropharyngeal abscesses. Early airway planning with involvement of experts (anesthesiology, otolaryngology/head and neck surgery, and intensive care) is crucial. The key principle is to secure the airway electively under controlled conditions before complete obstruction occurs; emergency cricothyrotomy or tracheostomy performed in a fully obstructed airway carries higher complication rates.

Preferred strategies include awake fiberoptic intubation with the patient spontaneously ventilating when feasible. Awake intubation allows maintenance of airway tone and spontaneous respiration, decreasing the risk of complete obstruction during induction. Nasal fiberoptic approaches may be contraindicated if there is significant nasal or base-of-skull infection. In selected cases where oral access is limited by trismus or tongue swelling, a surgical airway (awake tracheostomy) performed by an experienced surgeon may be the safest option. In extreme, rapidly deteriorating situations where neither intubation nor tracheostomy is immediately possible, emergent cricothyrotomy may be life-saving.

Decision points include assessing the trajectory of swelling, the presence of stridor or hypoxia, inability to protect airway or handle secretions, and the feasibility of controlled airway techniques—if these favor imminent failure, early definitive airway control is indicated. Postoperative airway care is also important: many patients require admission to an intensive care unit for close monitoring, and extubation should be approached cautiously, often after edema has decreased and airway patency is confirmed.

# Distinguishing infectious swelling from non-infectious causes

Not all neck swellings are infectious, and differentiating infectious from non-infectious etiologies is essential for appropriate management. Key clinical elements that favor infection include acute onset over hours to days, systemic signs (fever, chills), localized tenderness, erythema, warmth, fluctuance, purulent drainage, and association with a plausible source (recent dental infection, upper respiratory infection, salivary gland obstruction). Laboratory markers of inflammation—leukocytosis, elevated CRP and ESR—support an inflammatory/infectious process but are nonspecific.

Non-infectious causes include congenital cysts (branchial cleft cyst, thyroglossal duct cyst), neoplasms (lymphoma, metastatic squamous cell carcinoma, salivary gland tumors), benign tumors (lipoma), autoimmune or inflammatory disorders (sarcoidosis, IgG4-related disease), endocrine swelling (goiter, thyroid nodules), vascular lesions (carotid body tumors, aneurysms), and angioedema. Neoplastic lymphadenopathy often has a more subacute to chronic course, firm and non-tender nodes, lack of overlying skin inflammation, fixation to underlying structures, constitutional symptoms such as unintentional weight loss, and risk factors such as smoking or a primary mucosal lesion. Congenital cysts typically present earlier in life or as chronic intermittent swellings that may become secondarily infected; a midline, mobile swelling that moves with swallowing or tongue protrusion suggests a thyroglossal duct cyst. Angioedema is usually rapid in onset, non-pitting, non-erythematous swelling often involving the lips, face, or oropharynx, and is usually not tender or fluctuant, often with a history of exposure to an allergen or ACE inhibitor use.

Imaging and, when needed, tissue diagnosis are essential in differentiating causes. Ultrasound can characterize cystic versus solid masses and guide fine-needle aspiration (FNA) of lymph nodes or masses. CT or MRI provide structural details, evaluate invasion, and guide biopsy or surgery. For persistent or suspicious lymphadenopathy, FNA cytology or excisional biopsy is indicated—particularly if nodes are >2 cm, persist beyond 2–4 weeks despite therapy, are progressively enlarging, or are firm and fixed.

Clinical reasoning integrates the timeline, risk factors, examination, and imaging. For example, rapidly progressive, painful, tender swelling with systemic signs and a dental source is most consistent with an odontogenic abscess or Ludwig’s angina and requires urgent airway and infection management. A slowly enlarging, firm, non-tender neck mass without systemic features prompts consideration of neoplasm and expedited imaging with tissue diagnosis rather than empiric antibiotics alone.

Conclusion

Understanding the anatomy of deep neck spaces, typical microbial etiologies, and the clinical hallmarks of severe infections such as Ludwig's angina and retropharyngeal abscess is essential for early recognition, prompt airway assessment, and initiation of appropriate medical and surgical therapy. Decisions about imaging, drainage, and airway intervention should be driven by the anatomic extent, clinical severity, and trajectory of disease, with multidisciplinary involvement. Differentiating infectious from non-infectious causes relies on careful history, examination, targeted imaging, and tissue diagnosis when needed. Early, decisive action in DNSIs can prevent catastrophic complications such as airway obstruction, descending mediastinitis, and septic shock.

Introduction

Soft tissue swelling of the face, neck and subcutaneous tissues of the anterior chest and upper shoulders is a frequent and sometimes alarming clinical presentation. While angioedema is a commonly considered cause, a broad differential must be entertained because the pathophysiology, prognosis, and management differ substantially among etiologies. A systematic approach that integrates history, focused physical examination, directed laboratory testing, and imaging allows clinicians to distinguish vascular, inflammatory/autoimmune, infectious, neoplastic, and dermatologic causes. This chapter reviews the major non-angioedema causes of subcutaneous and cervical edema with emphasis on pathophysiology, clinical clues, diagnostic criteria and decision points relevant to initial evaluation and management.

# Vascular causes

Vascular mechanisms produce soft tissue swelling by altering hydrostatic and oncotic forces, obstructing lymphatic or venous drainage, or by causing increased vascular permeability. In the neck and face, the principal vascular causes are superior vena cava (SVC) syndrome, local venous thrombosis (including jugular vein thrombosis), and systemic congestive heart failure (CHF). Each has distinct presentations and diagnostic implications.

Superior vena cava syndrome

SVC syndrome results from obstruction of venous return through the superior vena cava, which increases venous pressure in the head, neck, upper limbs and chest wall. Malignancy (bronchogenic carcinoma, lymphoma, metastatic tumor) has historically been the most common cause, but indwelling central venous catheters and thrombosis are increasingly recognized etiologies. Pathophysiologically, obstruction raises hydrostatic pressure in veins and capillaries drained by the SVC, favoring transudation of fluid into the interstitial space and causing pitting or nonpitting edema. Collateral venous pathways may develop over time, mitigating symptoms in chronic cases.

Clinically, patients report facial fullness, periorbital edema, neck and upper chest swelling, distended superficial neck/chest veins, and sometimes dyspnea, cough, hoarseness, or dysphagia. Symptoms that worsen when leaning forward or when supine are characteristic. On exam, there may be nonpitting edema of the face and neck, plethora, conjunctival injection and visible collateral veins across the chest. SVC syndrome is a clinical diagnosis supported by imaging: contrast-enhanced CT of the chest (CT angiography) is the preferred initial test because it localizes the level of obstruction and suggests cause (mass versus thrombus). If CT is contraindicated, magnetic resonance venography or duplex ultrasound (for more peripheral venous obstruction) may be used. Management prioritizes airway and cerebral perfusion if severe; treatment is directed to the cause (oncologic therapy, stenting, anticoagulation for thrombotic obstruction). Urgent intervention is indicated if there is laryngeal edema, cerebral edema, or hemodynamic compromise.

Venous thrombosis (internal jugular, facial veins)

Thrombosis of the internal jugular, facial, or retromandibular veins impairs venous drainage and produces localized swelling, pain, erythema, and sometimes systemic inflammatory signs. Causes include central venous catheters, head and neck infection (Lemierre syndrome), prothrombotic states, and direct trauma. Pathophysiologically, an obstructive thrombus elevates upstream venous pressures and can cause venous congestion of subcutaneous tissues and skin. The region of involvement tends to be focal and unilateral unless the thrombosis is extensive or bilateral.

Clinical features include a tender, indurated cord along the course of the affected vein, ipsilateral swelling of the neck or face, and sometimes fever. Doppler ultrasound is the first-line diagnostic modality for accessible veins such as the internal jugular; contrast-enhanced CT or MR venography is used for deeper or more proximal segments. Management involves systemic anticoagulation unless contraindicated, broad-spectrum antibiotics if septic thrombosis is suspected, and treatment of underlying triggers (e.g., removal of infected catheter, drainage of adjacent infection). The decision to anticoagulate requires balancing hemorrhagic risk and extent of thrombosis; consultation with hematology or vascular surgery is often appropriate for extensive or complicated cases.

Congestive heart failure

In congestive heart failure, increased systemic venous pressure from right-sided or biventricular dysfunction leads to dependent edema. While classic lower-extremity and sacral swelling are most prominent, elevated central venous pressure can cause facial and periorbital puffiness, especially in the morning or when recumbent. Edema due to CHF is typically pitting and symmetric; it fluctuates with diuretic therapy and posture.

Physical examination findings supporting CHF include elevated jugular venous pressure, peripheral edema, hepatomegaly, and pulmonary crackles if pulmonary congestion is present. Natriuretic peptide measurements (BNP or NT-proBNP) are useful laboratory adjuncts: BNP < 100 pg/mL and NT-proBNP < 300 pg/mL make heart failure unlikely, while markedly elevated values support the diagnosis (interpretation varies by age and renal function). Chest radiography and echocardiography provide structural and functional information. Management focuses on optimizing volume status with diuretics and treating the underlying cardiac dysfunction; improvement in facial/neck edema with diuresis supports a cardiac etiology.

Clinical decision points for vascular causes

When swelling is bilateral, involves the entire face and upper chest and is associated with distended chest wall veins, consider SVC syndrome and obtain emergent imaging. Localized, tender swelling along venous routes suggests thrombosis—use Doppler ultrasound and consider anticoagulation. If findings are consistent with systemic volume overload (pitting edema, elevated JVP, cardiopulmonary signs), evaluate for CHF with BNP and echocardiography and initiate diuresis as appropriate.

# Inflammatory and autoimmune causes

Autoimmune and inflammatory conditions produce cutaneous and subcutaneous swelling via immune complex deposition, complement activation, vasculitis, and inflammatory infiltration. In the neck and face, important entities include dermatomyositis, systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), and various vasculitides, especially urticarial vasculitis. Recognizing the pattern and associated systemic features guides serologic testing, biopsy decisions, and immunomodulatory therapy.

Dermatomyositis

Dermatomyositis is an autoimmune inflammatory myopathy associated with characteristic cutaneous manifestations and proximal muscle weakness. Cutaneous findings relevant to facial and cervical swelling include periorbital edema (“heliotrope” sign), facial erythema, and shawl sign (erythema and edema across the shoulders and posterior neck). The periorbital edema in dermatomyositis may be prominent and nonpitting, and it frequently coexists with violaceous heliotrope discoloration. Pathophysiology involves complement-mediated microangiopathy with capillary destruction in skin and muscle, leading to ischemia, inflammation and tissue edema. Dermatomyositis can be paraneoplastic, particularly in adults, so evaluation for occult malignancy is recommended.

Diagnosis is based on clinical features, elevated muscle enzymes (creatine kinase; normal CK varies by laboratory but roughly up to 200 U/L), electromyography, and muscle biopsy showing perivascular/perimysial inflammation with perifascicular atrophy. Myositis-specific and associated autoantibodies (e.g., anti-Mi-2, anti-TIF1-gamma, anti-MDA5) can aid classification and assess malignancy risk. Facial and cervical edema in dermatomyositis often responds to systemic immunosuppression (corticosteroids, steroid-sparing agents), but management must be individualized.

Systemic lupus erythematosus

SLE is a multisystem autoimmune disease that can cause subcutaneous edema through multiple mechanisms: nephrotic-range proteinuria causing hypoalbuminemia and decreased oncotic pressure, serositis with superior mediastinal or cervical lymphatic obstruction, and inflammation of cutaneous tissues including angioedema-like mechanisms. Lupus-specific rashes (malar rash) can be accompanied by swelling. Diagnostic criteria for SLE are based on classification systems (e.g., the 2019 EULAR/ACR criteria), which integrate clinical features and autoantibodies—anti-nuclear antibody (ANA), anti-dsDNA, anti-Smith, complement levels (C3, C4) are commonly assessed. Low complement and high anti-dsDNA titers often correlate with disease activity. Proteinuria thresholds for nephrotic syndrome are protein excretion > 3.5 g/day or urine protein-to-creatinine ratio > 3.5 g/g, associated with hypoalbuminemia and generalized edema.

When facial and cervical swelling occur in a patient with known or suspected SLE, assessment should include urinalysis, serum albumin, complement levels, and targeted imaging for lymphatic obstruction if indicated. Treatment addresses the underlying disease activity (steroids, immunosuppressants) and the mechanisms producing edema (diuretics, albumin replacement in some cases).

Vasculitis and urticarial vasculitis

Small- and medium-vessel vasculitides can cause localized or diffuse soft tissue swelling via vessel wall inflammation, increased permeability and tissue ischemia. Vasculitis involving cutaneous vessels may produce palpable purpura, urticarial lesions that persist beyond 24 hours, and subcutaneous nodules. Urticarial vasculitis is a clinical-pathologic entity characterized by urticarial lesions that show small-vessel leukocytoclastic vasculitis on biopsy. Unlike ordinary urticaria, lesions are often painful or burning, last > 24 hours, and may leave residual hyperpigmentation.

Facial or periorbital swelling in vasculitis can be accompanied by systemic signs (fever, arthralgias, renal involvement). Laboratory evaluation includes inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP), complement levels (hypocomplementemic urticarial vasculitis may show low C3/C4), urinalysis for renal involvement, and autoantibody testing (ANCA for certain vasculitides). Skin biopsy is often diagnostic; direct immunofluorescence can demonstrate immune complex deposition. Management depends on severity and systemic involvement, ranging from antihistamines and short-course corticosteroids for limited cutaneous disease to systemic immunosuppression (e.g., corticosteroids, cyclophosphamide, rituximab) for organ-threatening disease.

Clinical reasoning in inflammatory/autoimmune causes

Key clinical clues that point to autoimmune or inflammatory etiologies include chronicity, bilateral symmetric edema with accompanying dermatologic signs (heliotrope rash, shawl sign, malar rash), systemic features such as fever, arthralgia or muscle weakness, laboratory evidence of systemic inflammation or autoimmunity, and lack of response to antibiotics or diuretics. Skin and muscle biopsy may be required for definitive diagnosis; early collaboration with rheumatology and dermatology is beneficial when systemic immunosuppression may be needed.

# Infectious causes

Infections are a leading cause of localized soft tissue swelling of the face and neck. The spectrum ranges from superficial cellulitis to salivary gland infections (sialadenitis) and life-threatening deep neck space infections. Infectious processes produce edema through inflammatory vasodilation and increased vascular permeability, tissue necrosis and lymphatic obstruction. Prompt recognition and delineation of the anatomic extent are essential for appropriate antimicrobial therapy and surgical management when indicated.

Cellulitis and erysipelas

Cellulitis is acute bacterial infection of the skin and subcutaneous tissues, often caused by streptococci and staphylococci. On the face and neck, erysipelas (a superficial form of streptococcal cutaneous infection with sharply demarcated raised borders) classically affects the face. Cellulitis presents with erythema, warmth, tenderness, and edema; systemic symptoms such as fever and leukocytosis may be present. Facial cellulitis may be associated with sinusitis, dental infections, trauma, or insect bites.

Distinguishing cellulitis from other causes requires attention to skin changes (induration and warmth), progression, and systemic features. Imaging is not usually required for simple cellulitis but is indicated when deep tissue involvement is suspected. Empiric antibiotic choice depends on suspected pathogens and MRSA prevalence: for nonpurulent facial cellulitis with likely streptococcal etiology, beta-lactam antibiotics (e.g., amoxicillin-clavulanate) may be chosen; purulent infections require coverage for S. aureus (including MRSA). Clinical reassessment within 24–48 hours is important to ensure response; lack of improvement warrants imaging and consideration of deeper infection or an alternative diagnosis.

Sialadenitis

Sialadenitis is inflammation of the salivary glands—most commonly the parotid or submandibular glands—and can be infectious or obstructive. Acute bacterial sialadenitis often presents with unilateral glandular swelling, pain that worsens with meals, erythema of overlying skin, trismus or difficulty opening the mouth, and purulent discharge from the duct orifices. Risk factors include dehydration, recent surgery, immunosuppression, sialolithiasis, and poor oral hygiene. Organisms include Staphylococcus aureus, oral anaerobes and, in some settings, gram-negative bacteria.

Diagnosis is largely clinical, but ultrasound or CT imaging helps if abscess or deep extension is suspected or to detect sialolithiasis. Management includes hydration, gland massage, sialogogues (lemon drops), anti-staphylococcal antibiotics, and drainage if abscess develops. Chronic sialadenitis and autoimmune sialadenitis (e.g., Sjögren syndrome) produce recurrent painless or painful swelling and require different management.

Deep neck space infections

Deep neck space infections—peritonsillar abscess, parapharyngeal abscess, retropharyngeal abscess—are potentially life-threatening because of airway compromise, mediastinal spread, and vascular involvement (e.g., internal jugular vein thrombophlebitis). They often present with fever, severe unilateral pain, swelling of the neck, trismus, odynophagia, muffled voice, and respiratory distress in severe cases. Physical exam may reveal fluctuance, bulging of the pharyngeal wall, and neck stiffness.

CT scan of the neck with contrast is the diagnostic modality of choice to delineate the location and extent of the infection and to identify abscess formation versus cellulitis. Management necessitates prompt airway assessment, intravenous broad-spectrum antibiotics that cover oral flora (including anaerobes), and timely surgical drainage for established abscesses. Recognition of red flags—stridor, drooling, rapid progression of swelling, signs of sepsis—should lower the threshold for urgent imaging and otolaryngology consultation.

Clinical pearls for infectious etiologies

- Infectious causes commonly present with pain, erythema, systemic signs, and unilateral localization. Rapid progression and systemic toxicity suggest deep infection requiring imaging and possibly surgical intervention.
- When gland involvement is suspected, ask about changes with meals and inspect duct orifices for purulence.
- In immunocompromised patients, unusual organisms (fungi, atypical mycobacteria) and less typical presentations should be considered; broader microbiologic testing and early specialist involvement are indicated.

# Neoplastic and obstructive causes

Neoplastic processes can produce subcutaneous and cervical edema directly by mass effect—compressing veins or lymphatic channels—or indirectly via tumor-related lymphadenopathy. Salivary gland tumors, lymphomas, metastatic adenopathy, and parapharyngeal space masses can all manifest as neck swelling, often with progressive, nonfluctuant enlargement and less inflammatory change than infectious causes.

Lymphadenopathy: benign and malignant

Enlarged lymph nodes in the cervical chain are a frequent cause of neck swelling. Reactive lymphadenopathy from infections or inflammatory conditions tends to be tender, mobile, soft and may regress over weeks. Malignant nodes—due to lymphoma or metastatic carcinoma—are often painless, firm, hard, fixed, and progressively enlarging. Supraclavicular nodes, particularly on the left (Virchow node), are classically associated with intra-abdominal malignancy metastases.

When lymphadenopathy persists beyond 2–4 weeks without clear infectious cause, is progressively enlarging, or shows suspicious features (size > 2 cm, hard/fixed, systemic “B” symptoms such as fever, weight loss, night sweats), further evaluation is warranted. Core needle biopsy or excisional biopsy is preferred over fine-needle aspiration when lymphoma is suspected because tissue architecture is necessary for histopathological subtyping. Imaging—ultrasound for superficial nodes; contrast-enhanced CT or PET-CT for staging and search for occult primaries—is used according to the clinical context.

Salivary gland tumors and parapharyngeal masses

Neoplasms of the salivary glands (parotid, submandibular, sublingual) present as painless enlarging masses in the corresponding region; facial nerve weakness suggests malignant parotid disease. Parapharyngeal space masses may present as lateral pharyngeal wall bulging, dysphagia, voice changes, or external neck swelling. Tumors may be benign (pleomorphic adenoma) or malignant (mucoepidermoid carcinoma, adenoid cystic carcinoma). Imaging with contrast-enhanced CT or MRI defines the lesion’s size, extent and relationship to neurovascular structures; fine-needle aspiration cytology (FNAC) or core biopsy aids diagnosis but carries concern for tumor seeding in rare cases.

Obstructive/space-occupying lesions causing edema

Masses within the thoracic inlet, mediastinum or lower neck can obstruct venous or lymphatic outflow, leading to facial and neck edema reminiscent of SVC syndrome. Thyroid enlargement (goiter) or retrosternal masses may produce visible neck swelling and compress adjacent structures causing cough, dyspnea or dysphagia. Lymphatic obstruction secondary to tumor infiltration or prior surgery/radiation may produce lymphedema, which tends to be nonpitting and chronic.

Key diagnostic and management considerations

- Progressive, noninflammatory neck masses merit imaging (ultrasound for superficial lesions; CT/MRI for deep lesions) and tissue diagnosis.
- Consider age and risk factors: in adults >40 with new unilateral neck mass, malignancy risk is higher and prompt evaluation is needed.
- Multidisciplinary involvement (head and neck surgery, ENT, oncology) is often required for definitive management including resection, radiotherapy, or systemic therapy.

# Drug reactions, contact dermatitis and orofacial granulomatosis

Dermatologic and hypersensitivity processes of the face and neck produce soft tissue swelling through mechanisms of allergic inflammation, delayed-type hypersensitivity, or granulomatous inflammation. These entities often target mucocutaneous tissues and may be mistaken for angioedema or infectious cellulitis.

Drug reactions

Drug-induced facial swelling can result from immediate-type hypersensitivity (angioedema), delayed allergic dermatitis, or fixed drug eruptions. Distinguishing features include temporal relation to drug exposure, pruritus, rash distribution and associated systemic signs. Common culprits for facial/neck swelling include antibiotics, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, ACE inhibitors (classically causing bradykinin-mediated angioedema), and chemotherapeutic or biologic agents that induce immune dysregulation.

Immediate allergic reactions present rapidly (minutes to hours) and often involve urticaria and systemic symptoms; management includes antihistamines, corticosteroids and epinephrine for anaphylaxis. Delayed reactions may manifest as eczematous dermatitis with localized edema and require withdrawal of the offending agent and topical/systemic anti-inflammatory therapy. A thorough medication history—including new prescription drugs, over-the-counter agents, supplements and recent vaccinations—is essential.

Contact dermatitis

Contact dermatitis of the face and neck is most commonly allergic or irritant in type. Allergic contact dermatitis develops after sensitization to a contactant (nickel, fragrances, topical antibiotics, herbal products, cosmetics) and presents with pruritic erythematous papules or plaques with edema limited to areas of exposure. Irritant contact dermatitis results from direct epithelial injury and can present similarly but without an allergic mechanism. Patch testing identifies specific allergens in chronic or recurrent cases. Management involves avoidance, topical corticosteroids, and emollients; severe cases may require systemic steroids.

Orofacial granulomatosis and granulomatous reactions

Orofacial granulomatosis (OFG) is a clinical syndrome of chronic, noncaseating granulomatous inflammation affecting the lips, perioral area and sometimes the cheeks and face. Clinically it causes persistent lip swelling, firm nonpitting edema, and sometimes mucosal ulceration. OFG is heterogeneous in etiology and may be idiopathic or associated with systemic diseases such as Crohn disease, sarcoidosis, and foreign-body reactions. Histology shows noncaseating granulomas; workup includes gastrointestinal evaluation when Crohn disease is suspected, chest imaging and ACE levels if sarcoidosis is a consideration.

Management typically begins with topical or intralesional corticosteroids for localized disease; refractory or systemic disease may require immunomodulators (e.g., azathioprine, methotrexate, anti-TNF agents) after specialist input. Avoidance of potential triggers identified by patch testing (metals, food additives) can be helpful in some patients.

Clinical reasoning for dermatologic causes

Dermatologic etiologies tend to be characterized by cutaneous signs—pruritus, rash, scaling or ulceration—localized to areas of exposure or distribution consistent with specific syndromes. A careful history of exposures and medications, inspection of skin lesions, and, when needed, dermatologic tests (patch testing, skin biopsy) help confirm the diagnosis. Response to withdrawal of the suspected agent or to topical therapy can be diagnostic and therapeutic.

# Integration and approach to evaluation

A systematic approach to a patient presenting with subcutaneous or cervical edema beyond suspected angioedema includes:

1. Rapid assessment for airway compromise, respiratory distress, or hemodynamic instability. If present, secure the airway and manage emergently.
2. Focused history: onset (sudden versus progressive), laterality (unilateral versus bilateral), associated symptoms (pain, pruritus, fever, dysphagia, dyspnea, visual changes, muscle weakness), relation to posture or Valsalva, recent infections or dental/ENT procedures, medication exposures, cancer history, and systemic symptoms (weight loss, night sweats).
3. Physical examination: inspect skin (erythema, rash, induration), palpate for lymphadenopathy, tenderness, fluctuance, and venous distension; assess for JVP, chest wall collaterals, and facial plethoric changes; evaluate cranial nerve and facial nerve function if mass effect suspected.
4. Initial laboratory and bedside tests: CBC with differential (leukocytosis suggests infection), basic metabolic panel (renal function impacts edema and imaging choices), liver tests and albumin (hypoalbuminemia contributes to generalized edema), inflammatory markers (ESR/CRP), BNP if heart failure suspected, urinalysis for proteinuria, and targeted autoantibodies (ANA, ANCA, complement) based on clinical suspicion.
5. Imaging: select modality to elucidate anatomic extent—ultrasound for superficial masses and vascular thrombosis; contrast-enhanced CT for deep neck spaces, SVC syndrome evaluation, and salivary gland or parapharyngeal lesions; MRI for soft tissue characterization; chest imaging for suspected thoracic causes or malignancy. Duplex ultrasound is first-line for suspected vein thrombosis.
6. Tissue diagnosis: perform skin biopsy for vasculitis or granulomatous disease, FNAC or core/excisional lymph node biopsy for persistent lymphadenopathy or suspected malignancy, and culture/drainage of purulent collections.
7. Management decisions: tailor therapy to cause—antibiotics and drainage for infection, anticoagulation for venous thrombosis, diuretics for cardiac volume overload, immunosuppression for autoimmune disease, and oncologic or surgical intervention for neoplasms. Consult relevant specialties early (ENT/head and neck surgery, infectious disease, rheumatology, hematology/oncology, vascular surgery).

Conclusion

The differential diagnosis for subcutaneous and cervical edema is broad. Distinguishing among vascular obstruction, inflammatory/autoimmune disease, infection, neoplasm, and dermatologic reactions requires synthesizing clinical clues with directed laboratory testing and imaging. A methodical approach—prioritizing airway assessment, detailed history, targeted physical examination, appropriate imaging and timely tissue diagnosis—enables efficient triage and management. Awareness of key distinguishing features and potential red flags (rapid progression, airway compromise, signs of systemic disease or malignancy) is essential for safe and effective care.

Introduction

Dermatomyositis (DM) is an idiopathic inflammatory myopathy characterized by proximal muscle weakness and distinctive cutaneous manifestations. Beyond the morbidity attributable to muscle inflammation and skin disease, DM carries a well-established association with an increased risk of malignancy. Recognizing this association and adopting a rational, evidence-informed approach to cancer screening are essential components of the initial evaluation and ongoing management of patients with DM. This chapter reviews the epidemiologic data that link DM with specific cancers, summarizes clinical and serologic risk factors that stratify patients for malignancy, and provides practical guidance on baseline and interval screening strategies, including the role and limitations of PET-CT. Finally, it summarizes major guideline and consensus recommendations to help formulate a pragmatic, patient-centered cancer screening approach in DM.

# Epidemiologic evidence linking dermatomyositis and specific cancers

Epidemiologic studies consistently demonstrate an elevated risk of cancer in patients with DM compared with the general population. The magnitude and pattern of risk vary by study design, geographic region, and the time window considered relative to DM onset. Standardized incidence ratios (SIRs) for cancer in DM have commonly been reported in the range of approximately 2–7, with the highest risks occurring within the first 1–3 years after DM diagnosis. In contrast, polymyositis generally carries a lower cancer risk than DM, although some studies still show modestly increased rates.

The spectrum of associated malignancies includes both solid tumors and hematologic cancers. Recurrently reported solid tumors include lung, breast, colorectal, ovarian, pancreatic, stomach (gastric), and nasopharyngeal carcinomas. The relative importance of different tumor types varies by region: for example, nasopharyngeal carcinoma is overrepresented in East and Southeast Asia, whereas ovarian and lung cancers are more commonly reported in European and North American cohorts. Hematologic malignancies, particularly non-Hodgkin lymphoma, are also reported with increased frequency in some series.

Time relationship is important. A substantial proportion of cancers are detected within a narrow window around DM onset, often defined as within 3 years before to 3 years after DM diagnosis, supporting the concept of DM as a paraneoplastic phenomenon in some patients. However, an elevated long-term cancer risk persists beyond the initial years in some cohorts, and therefore long-term vigilance is reasonable, particularly in patients with ongoing risk factors or serologic markers suggestive of paraneoplastic DM.

Epidemiologic heterogeneity arises from differences in cohort selection (hospital-based vs. population-based), case ascertainment, follow-up duration, and screening intensity. These factors influence reported cancer incidence and the apparent utility of screening strategies; nevertheless, the aggregate literature supports targeted cancer screening at the time of DM diagnosis and close surveillance during the early years after diagnosis.

# Risk factors that increase likelihood of associated malignancy (age, certain autoantibodies, dysphagia, cutaneous features)

Identifying clinical and laboratory features that stratify cancer risk in DM helps tailor screening intensity. Important risk factors supported by multiple studies include older age at DM onset, certain myositis-specific autoantibodies, severe or atypical cutaneous findings, and features such as dysphagia.

Age. Advancing age at DM onset is among the most consistent predictors of associated malignancy. Most studies demonstrate a markedly higher risk of cancer in patients older than approximately 40–50 years at diagnosis, with risk climbing further with increasing age. In practical terms, age is often used to determine whether to pursue routine age-appropriate cancer screening alone or to adopt a more aggressive, targeted search for occult malignancy.

Myositis-specific autoantibodies. Autoantibody profiling has proved particularly valuable for cancer risk stratification. Anti-transcriptional intermediary factor 1-gamma (anti–TIF1-γ, also called anti–TIF1 or anti-p155/p140) antibodies are strongly associated with an elevated risk of malignancy in adults with DM. Multiple studies report large effect sizes: presence of anti–TIF1-γ may increase the odds of an associated cancer several-fold relative to antibody-negative patients. Another antibody, anti–nuclear matrix protein 2 (anti–NXP-2, formerly anti-MJ), has also been linked to increased cancer risk in some series. Anti–small ubiquitin-like modifier activating enzyme (anti–SAE) has been associated with malignancy in smaller studies. Conversely, antibodies directed against aminoacyl tRNA synthetases (e.g., anti–Jo-1) and antibodies typically associated with classic, non-paraneoplastic DM (e.g., anti–Mi-2) are generally associated with lower cancer risk. Thus, autoantibody profile is a key determinant of whether a patient should undergo intensified cancer evaluation.

Clinical features: dysphagia and cutaneous findings. Dysphagia is both common in DM and independently associated with an increased risk of cancer in some cohorts. Severe or rapidly progressive dysphagia—suggesting more extensive disease—warrants particular attention because it may coexist with occult malignancy.

Certain cutaneous phenotypes have also been tied to higher cancer risk. Classic DM skin findings include Gottron papules, heliotrope rash, V-sign and shawl sign photodistribution, and mechanic’s hands. Some studies have suggested that cutaneous necrosis, vasculopathic lesions, and rapidly progressive skin disease may correlate with a greater likelihood of an associated malignancy, possibly reflecting paraneoplastic immune mechanisms. However, the association is less robust than that for age and specific autoantibodies, and cutaneous features should be integrated with other clinical and serologic data.

Other factors. Male sex has sometimes been associated with higher cancer risk in DM, though sex effects vary by study and tumor type. Systemic inflammatory markers have no consistent predictive value for cancer. A history of smoking increases risk for lung cancer and may modify the overall probability of malignancy in individual patients.

Integrating risk factors. In clinical practice, a patient with DM who is older (commonly >40–50 years), anti–TIF1-γ or possibly anti–NXP-2 positive, and/or has dysphagia or atypical skin features would be considered high risk and merits an intensive malignancy search. Younger patients, those with anti–Jo-1 or anti–Mi-2 antibodies, and those without other risk features may be managed with standard age-appropriate cancer screening plus targeted testing guided by symptoms and physical examination.

# Baseline and interval screening strategies (imaging, tumor markers, endoscopic evaluations)

Screening aims to detect occult malignancy early enough to affect outcomes while minimizing unnecessary tests, false positives, and harms. Screening should be individualized based on risk stratification described above, resources, and patient preferences. A practical approach combines a careful history and physical examination with baseline targeted investigations, and repeated assessments during the period of greatest risk.

Baseline evaluation. All patients with newly diagnosed DM should receive an organized baseline assessment focused on identifying features suggestive of malignancy and ensuring they are up to date with routine, age-/sex-appropriate cancer screening. Key components include a thorough review of systems (weight loss, fevers, anorexia, focal pain, change in bowel or bladder habits, vaginal bleeding), comprehensive skin and lymph node exam, chest auscultation, abdominal palpation, pelvic and breast exams in appropriate patients, and ENT/oral cavity inspection when indicated (especially in regions with endemic nasopharyngeal carcinoma).

Age-appropriate screening. All patients should receive standard population screening per local guidelines: mammography for women (commonly biennial or annual depending on regional recommendations; start ages typically 40–50), Pap smear and HPV testing as indicated, colonoscopy or fecal immunochemical testing for colorectal cancer per recommended schedules (colonoscopy typically every 10 years if normal starting at age 45–50 in many regions), and PSA testing in men according to shared decision-making. These baseline preventive measures are essential because certain cancers associated with DM (e.g., breast, colorectal) are amenable to detection by routine screening.

Targeted imaging. For high-risk patients, baseline cross-sectional imaging of chest, abdomen, and pelvis is commonly recommended. Contrast-enhanced computed tomography (CT) of chest/abdomen/pelvis (CT CAP) is frequently used because it surveys the common sites for occult solid tumors. In women, pelvic imaging with transvaginal ultrasound (TVUS) or pelvic MRI may be added, particularly to evaluate for ovarian or uterine malignancy. For suspected head and neck cancers (e.g., nasopharyngeal carcinoma), contrast-enhanced MRI of the nasopharynx or dedicated ENT evaluation with flexible nasopharyngoscopy is appropriate.

Tumor markers. Serum tumor markers are imperfect as primary screening tests due to limited sensitivity and specificity, but they can play a role as adjuncts in a targeted evaluation. Commonly used markers include CA-125 for ovarian cancer (reference range commonly ≤35 U/mL), carcinoembryonic antigen (CEA) for colorectal cancer (reference usually <3–5 ng/mL depending on lab), CA 19-9 for pancreaticobiliary malignancy (reference often <37 U/mL), and alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) and beta-hCG in specific clinical contexts. PSA (prostate-specific antigen) in men is used for prostate cancer screening (commonly considered abnormal >4.0 ng/mL, though age-adjusted thresholds and shared decision-making apply). Elevations should prompt directed imaging and specialist referral but must be interpreted with caution given false positives from benign conditions.

Endoscopic evaluations. Endoscopic studies are indicated when symptoms or other screening tests suggest gastrointestinal pathology. Because gastric and colorectal cancers have been reported in DM cohorts, many clinicians recommend colonoscopy as part of baseline evaluation for patients who are not up to date with screening, particularly in older adults. Upper endoscopy (esophagogastroduodenoscopy) is considered when symptoms such as dysphagia, odynophagia, persistent upper GI pain, or unexplained iron deficiency anemia are present, or in geographic regions with high gastric cancer prevalence. For patients with dysphagia, a swallow study and ENT/esophageal evaluation are important both to assess the muscular cause and to exclude a structural or malignant obstruction.

Interval screening. The cancer risk in DM is highest in the early years after diagnosis, particularly within the first 1–3 years. Most experts advocate repeat cancer assessment at regular intervals during this window. A practical schedule used by many centers is evaluation at diagnosis with targeted imaging, then repeat assessments at 6–12 months and annually for up to 3 years in high-risk patients. For low-risk patients, maintaining routine age-appropriate screening and symptom-based reassessment may suffice. Any new systemic symptoms, unexplained weight loss, refractory DM features, or lab abnormalities (e.g., new cytopenias) should prompt expedited cancer re-evaluation irrespective of timing.

Decision points. The clinician must weigh pretest probability against test performance: in high pretest probability (older age, high-risk antibodies), the threshold for comprehensive imaging (CT CAP, pelvic imaging, or PET-CT) is lower. In low-risk patients, the harms and costs of broad imaging may outweigh benefits. Shared decision-making and documentation of the rationale for the chosen screening intensity are recommended.

# Role of PET-CT in occult cancer detection and limitations

Fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography combined with computed tomography (FDG PET-CT) is increasingly used as a “one-stop” modality to search for occult malignancy in DM. PET-CT offers whole-body metabolic imaging and can detect metabolically active lesions that are occult on anatomical imaging. Several studies report high sensitivity of PET-CT for detecting malignancy in DM, and some series suggest similar or better detection rates compared with conventional CT CAP combined with focused investigations.

Advantages. PET-CT can detect nodal and extranodal disease, provide metabolic information that helps distinguish benign from malignant lesions, and identify occult primaries that would otherwise require multiple imaging modalities to find. PET-CT is particularly useful where the differential includes lymphoma or cancers that are FDG-avid, and it can simultaneously evaluate the extent of disease for staging purposes.

Limitations and pitfalls. Despite its strengths, PET-CT has important limitations:

- False positives: Infectious or inflammatory processes—including active myositis, cutaneous inflammation, and reactive lymphadenopathy—can show increased FDG uptake, leading to false-positive findings requiring further invasive evaluation such as biopsy. In DM patients, active inflammatory muscle and skin disease can confound PET-CT interpretation.

- False negatives: Some tumor types are poorly FDG-avid (e.g., certain low-grade tumors, some prostate cancers, mucinous adenocarcinomas), or lesions may be below the spatial resolution of PET-CT (typically lesions <5–7 mm), leading to false-negative results. Small mucosal lesions in the nasopharynx or gastrointestinal tract may be missed.

- Cost, accessibility, and radiation: PET-CT is expensive and may not be readily available in some settings. It imparts a radiation dose comparable to or greater than CT, which is a consideration for younger patients or repeated studies.

- Lack of standardized protocols: There is no universally accepted algorithm for the use of PET-CT in DM; practices vary between centers. Differences in timing (baseline vs. after initial negative CT) and patient selection affect yield and cost-effectiveness.

Clinical positioning. PET-CT is most useful in patients at elevated risk for malignancy—those older at onset, anti–TIF1-γ or anti–NXP-2 positive, with systemic features suggestive of paraneoplastic disease, or in whom conventional directed testing is unrevealing despite high clinical suspicion. In centers with resources, a baseline PET-CT may be reasonable for high-risk patients as part of an initial comprehensive evaluation. If PET-CT is performed, clinicians should recognize potential false positives from inflammatory DM activity and interpret findings in the clinical context, often requiring histologic confirmation of suspected lesions. Where PET-CT is unavailable or contraindicated, contrast-enhanced CT CAP plus targeted imaging and endoscopy remains an appropriate strategy.

# Guidelines and consensus statements for myositis-associated cancer screening

Multiple national and international organizations and expert groups have issued guidance on cancer screening in idiopathic inflammatory myopathies, including DM. While recommendations vary in specifics, common themes include: perform a careful baseline cancer assessment; prioritize age-appropriate routine screening; more intensive targeted screening for older patients and those with high-risk features; and heightened surveillance during the first 1–3 years after DM onset when cancer risk is highest. Below are the key principles distilled from major guidelines and consensus statements.

General recommendations. Organizations such as the European League Against Rheumatism/American College of Rheumatology (EULAR/ACR) myositis working groups, the International Myositis Assessment and Clinical Studies Group (IMACS), and various national rheumatology and dermatology societies advocate a structured approach to cancer screening in DM:

- Baseline: At diagnosis, complete a thorough history and physical exam; ensure adherence to routine age-/sex-appropriate cancer screening; and consider targeted investigations directed by risk factors and symptoms.

- High-risk patients: For patients deemed high risk (age >40–50 years, anti–TIF1-γ or anti–NXP-2 positivity, dysphagia, rapid-onset or atypical skin findings), many experts recommend more intensive baseline evaluation, including cross-sectional imaging of chest/abdomen/pelvis (CT CAP or PET-CT), pelvic imaging in women (TVUS or MRI), and appropriate tumor markers. ENT evaluation and nasopharyngoscopy are recommended in regions with high nasopharyngeal carcinoma prevalence.

- Timing and frequency: Repeat focused assessments at 6–12 months and then annually for up to 3 years for high-risk patients is a commonly endorsed strategy. Continued vigilance beyond 3 years is individualized based on ongoing risk factors and clinical course.

Role of PET-CT. Consensus statements acknowledge the utility of PET-CT for whole-body assessment in detecting occult malignancy but stop short of a blanket recommendation for universal use, citing resource constraints, potential false positives, and variability in availability. PET-CT is often recommended as an option or as the preferred modality in centers where available for high-risk patients.

Tumor markers and endoscopy. Guidelines typically advise against indiscriminate use of serum tumor markers in low-risk patients but regard them as adjunctive tests in targeted evaluations. Endoscopic procedures are reserved for symptom-guided investigation or when regional cancer prevalence or risk factors indicate higher yield.

Shared decision-making and local adaptation. Given variation in resources, cancer epidemiology, and healthcare systems, guidelines emphasize individualized care and shared decision-making. Strategies should be adapted to local cancer prevalence (e.g., routine nasopharyngeal surveillance in endemic regions), availability of PET-CT, and patient comorbidities and preferences.

Evidence gaps and need for further research. Guidelines note the limited high-quality trial data on optimal screening algorithms and intervals, and the heterogeneity of existing studies. Consequently, many recommendations are based on expert consensus and observational data, and clinicians should apply them pragmatically while awaiting stronger evidence.

Practical algorithm (synthesis). A commonly used practical approach derived from guideline principles is:

- All patients: Detailed history and physical exam; ensure up-to-date routine, age-appropriate cancer screening (mammography, colonoscopy, Pap/HPV, PSA as applicable).

- High-risk features present: Add contrast-enhanced CT chest/abdomen/pelvis and pelvic imaging in women ± PET-CT where available; consider tumor markers (e.g., CA-125) and ENT evaluation for regional risks; repeat at 6–12 months and annually for 2–3 years.

- Low-risk patients: If no high-risk features and up to date with routine screening, maintain standard screening intervals and reassess for new symptoms; reserve broad imaging for clinical suspicion.

Conclusion

Dermatomyositis carries a clearly increased risk of malignancy, most pronounced in the early years after diagnosis. Clinical risk stratification—most prominently age and myositis-specific autoantibodies such as anti–TIF1-γ—permits targeted screening strategies that balance detection yield against costs and harms. Baseline assessment should combine careful history and examination, age-appropriate routine screening, and targeted imaging and endoscopic evaluation for high-risk features. PET-CT is a powerful tool for whole-body assessment but has limitations including false positives from inflammatory disease and variable availability. Major guidelines converge on intensified screening for older patients and those with high-risk serologic or clinical features, with close surveillance during the first 1–3 years after diagnosis. Clinicians should individualize screening plans, involve patients in decision-making, and maintain a low threshold to re-evaluate if new symptoms or refractory disease suggest an occult malignancy.

Introduction

Inflammatory myopathies are a heterogeneous group of autoimmune diseases characterized by immune-mediated muscle inflammation, weakness, and extramuscular manifestations. Dermatomyositis (DM), polymyositis (PM), immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy (IMNM), and inclusion body myositis (IBM) differ in pathophysiology, natural history, and treatment responsiveness. This chapter focuses on evidence-based pharmacologic therapies used to treat dermatomyositis and other inflammatory myopathies, emphasizing mechanisms of action, dosing strategies, monitoring, adverse effects, and how to select and sequence therapies according to disease severity, organ involvement, and patient comorbidities. Supportive care and rehabilitation measures are equally important to maximize functional recovery and reduce complications. The chapter is intended to provide the practical pharmacologic foundation necessary for clinical decision-making.

# Glucocorticoids: mechanisms, pulse dosing, tapering strategies, adverse effects

Glucocorticoids remain the first-line therapy for most inflammatory myopathies because of their rapid and broad immunosuppressive effects. Mechanistically, glucocorticoids bind to the cytosolic glucocorticoid receptor, translocate to the nucleus, and alter gene transcription to suppress proinflammatory cytokines (e.g., IL-1, IL-6, TNF-α), reduce expression of adhesion molecules, impair antigen presentation, and induce apoptosis of certain immune cells. They also have non-genomic effects, including modulation of cell membrane function and rapid suppression of inflammatory mediator release. These combined effects reduce muscle inflammation and improve strength and function.

Induction dosing depends on severity and organ threat. For most patients with active DM or PM without severe weakness or organ involvement, oral prednisone is started at 0.5–1.0 mg/kg/day (commonly 40–60 mg/day in adults). In patients with severe weakness, rapidly progressive disease, dysphagia with risk of aspiration, respiratory muscle involvement, or life-threatening extramuscular disease (e.g., myocarditis, severe interstitial lung disease), high-dose “pulse” intravenous methylprednisolone is often employed. Typical pulse regimens are methylprednisolone 500–1000 mg IV daily for 3 consecutive days; in very severe cases repeated pulses and/or adjunct high-dose oral prednisone may be used. Pulse steroids may produce a faster clinical response and allow earlier initiation of steroid-sparing agents.

Tapering strategies must balance disease control with minimizing glucocorticoid toxicity. Once clinical improvement is achieved (strength gains, improved CK or other biomarkers, symptom relief), many clinicians begin to taper prednisone after 2–4 weeks on the initial dose. A common approach is to reduce prednisone by 10%–20% of the initial dose every 1–2 weeks until a moderate dose (e.g., 20 mg/day) is reached, then slower taper by 2.5–5 mg every 2–4 weeks based on disease activity. The ultimate goal is to reach the lowest effective dose or discontinuation, often using steroid-sparing agents to permit taper. Some patients require long-term low-dose prednisone (e.g., ≤5–10 mg/day) to maintain remission.

Adverse effects of glucocorticoids are dose- and duration-dependent and include metabolic, musculoskeletal, infectious, cardiovascular, neuropsychiatric, and endocrine complications. Short-term adverse effects include hyperglycemia, mood changes, insomnia, and fluid retention. Long-term effects include osteoporosis and increased fracture risk, avascular necrosis, weight gain, central adiposity, dyslipidemia, hypertension, diabetes mellitus, cataracts, glaucoma, skin thinning and bruising, muscle wasting (steroid myopathy), impaired wound healing, and increased susceptibility to infections. Prophylactic measures are recommended: bone protection (calcium 1000–1200 mg/day, vitamin D 800–2000 IU/day, and bisphosphonates for patients at significant risk), screening and treatment for hyperglycemia and hypertension, and infection risk mitigation (vaccination, PJP prophylaxis in some cases — see below). Because chronic glucocorticoid therapy can suppress the hypothalamic–pituitary–adrenal (HPA) axis, tapering should be cautious if long-term high-dose therapy has been used to avoid adrenal insufficiency.

Monitoring during glucocorticoid therapy includes periodic assessment of blood pressure, weight, glucose/HbA1c, lipids, bone density (DEXA), ophthalmologic exams for cataracts and glaucoma, and screening for infections. Serial clinical measures of muscle strength and functional status, and serum muscle enzymes (creatine kinase; normal adult upper limit varies by lab but commonly ~40–200 U/L) help assess response, though CK may not correlate with disease activity in all subtypes (e.g., anti-synthetase patients with ILD).

# Conventional steroid-sparing agents: methotrexate, azathioprine, mycophenolate mofetil, tacrolimus (mechanisms, dosing, toxicities)

Rationale and general principles
Because many patients require prolonged therapy, steroid-sparing immunosuppressants are routinely used to reduce glucocorticoid exposure and manage refractory disease. Choice among agents depends on disease phenotype, comorbidities, childbearing potential, organ involvement (e.g., lung), safety profile, and clinician experience. Onset of action for these agents is slower than glucocorticoids; clinical improvement may take weeks to months. Baseline screening (infectious disease testing, pregnancy testing, CBC, renal and liver function, and vaccine status) should precede initiation. Drug-specific details follow.

Methotrexate (MTX)
Mechanism: Methotrexate is a folate antagonist that inhibits dihydrofolate reductase and other enzymes in purine and pyrimidine synthesis; at the low doses used for autoimmune disease, it also increases extracellular adenosine with anti-inflammatory effects and modulates immune cell function.

Dosing: Typical dosing in inflammatory myopathies is methotrexate 15–25 mg once weekly, given orally or subcutaneously. Many clinicians start at 15 mg weekly and increase to 20–25 mg weekly depending on tolerability and response. Folic acid 1 mg daily or 5–10 mg weekly (excluding the dosing day) is given to reduce mucosal and hematologic side effects.

Monitoring and toxicities: Baseline labs include CBC with differential, liver function tests (LFTs), serum creatinine, hepatitis B/C serologies, and chest imaging if indicated. Monitor CBC and LFTs every 2–4 weeks during early therapy, then every 1–3 months. Methotrexate can cause hepatotoxicity (transaminase elevations, fibrosis), cytopenias, pulmonary toxicity (rare but potentially severe interstitial pneumonitis), mucositis, and alopecia. Avoid in pregnancy — methotrexate is teratogenic and requires contraception; men should also be counseled regarding paternal exposure. Dose adjustments are needed for renal impairment.

Azathioprine (AZA)
Mechanism: Azathioprine is converted to 6-mercaptopurine and metabolites that interfere with purine synthesis, leading to reduced lymphocyte proliferation. It exerts an immunosuppressive effect on both T and B cells.

Dosing: Azathioprine is typically started at 1–2 mg/kg/day and titrated up to 2–3 mg/kg/day as tolerated; once-daily dosing is common. Therapeutic effects may take 3–6 months.

Monitoring and toxicities: TPMT (thiopurine methyltransferase) enzyme activity or genotype should be checked before initiation because low TPMT activity predisposes to myelosuppression. Baseline CBC, LFTs, and viral serologies are recommended; monitor CBC and LFTs frequently (e.g., every 1–2 weeks during dose escalation then every 1–3 months). Toxicities include bone marrow suppression (leukopenia, anemia, thrombocytopenia), hepatotoxicity, pancreatitis, and increased infection risk. Azathioprine is teratogenic risk category; discuss pregnancy planning — some clinicians use azathioprine in pregnancy with caution (it is relatively safer than methotrexate which is contraindicated).

Mycophenolate mofetil (MMF)
Mechanism: MMF is converted to mycophenolic acid, which inhibits inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase (IMPDH), a key enzyme in de novo guanosine nucleotide synthesis; lymphocytes are particularly dependent on this pathway, leading to selective inhibition of T- and B-cell proliferation.

Dosing: Typical dosing in inflammatory myopathies ranges from 2 g/day (1000 mg twice daily) up to 3 g/day (1500 mg twice daily), depending on tolerance. Start at lower doses and escalate as tolerated. Clinical response may take several months.

Monitoring and toxicities: Baseline CBC, LFTs, and pregnancy testing are essential. MMF commonly causes gastrointestinal side effects (diarrhea, nausea), leukopenia, and increased infection risk; less hepatotoxicity than azathioprine or methotrexate. MMF is teratogenic and contraindicated in pregnancy; effective contraception is required.

Tacrolimus
Mechanism: Tacrolimus is a calcineurin inhibitor that binds FK506-binding protein (FKBP) and inhibits calcineurin phosphatase, preventing dephosphorylation of NFAT and downstream transcription of IL-2 and other cytokines, thus suppressing T-cell activation.

Dosing: Tacrolimus has been used off-label for refractory dermatomyositis and in patients with significant interstitial lung disease associated with myositis. Oral tacrolimus dosing often begins at 0.075 mg/kg/day to 0.15 mg/kg/day in two divided doses, typically adjusted to achieve trough levels in the range of 5–15 ng/mL (target depends on disease and center practice). Some regimens use a fixed dose of 1–3 mg twice daily with trough monitoring. Therapeutic adjustments are common.

Monitoring and toxicities: Monitor tacrolimus trough levels, renal function, blood pressure, electrolytes (especially potassium and magnesium), fasting glucose, and hepatic function. Adverse effects include nephrotoxicity (dose- and duration-dependent), hypertension, neurotoxicity (tremor, headache), hyperglycemia, dyslipidemia, and increased infection risk. Drug interactions (CYP3A4 inhibitors/inducers) are common and important to manage.

Comparative notes and sequencing
No high-quality randomized trials dictate an absolute hierarchy among agents; selection is individualized. Methotrexate and azathioprine are widely used first-line steroid-sparing agents due to clinician familiarity, evidence from observational cohorts and trials, and cost. MMF is favored in patients with concomitant interstitial lung disease and in those intolerant to MTX or AZA. Tacrolimus and cyclosporine are reserved for refractory disease, especially with significant pulmonary involvement or where T-cell-directed therapy is desired. When choosing agents, consider reproductive plans, comorbid liver or renal disease, infection history, and monitoring capacity.

# Biologic therapies: rituximab (anti-CD20), tofacitinib/JAK inhibitors and emerging agents

Rituximab (anti-CD20)
Mechanism: Rituximab is a chimeric monoclonal antibody targeting CD20 on B lymphocytes, leading to B-cell depletion through complement-dependent cytotoxicity, antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity, and induction of apoptosis. B cells contribute to myositis pathogenesis via autoantibody production, antigen presentation, and cytokine secretion; therefore, rituximab can be effective in autoantibody-associated myositis and refractory cases.

Evidence and indications: Rituximab has been studied in dermatomyositis and polymyositis, including the RIM trial (rituximab in myositis), which showed mixed primary endpoint results but suggested benefit in subgroups and in open-label and registry data; it is commonly used for refractory disease or in cases with prominent autoantibody-mediated pathology (e.g., anti-synthetase, anti-Mi-2, anti-SRP disease depending on context). Rituximab is also preferred for certain extramuscular manifestations such as severe cutaneous disease refractory to conventional agents and for some patients with concurrent systemic autoimmune diseases.

Dosing: Two common regimens are 375 mg/m^2 weekly for 4 weeks (oncology regimen) or 1000 mg IV given on days 1 and 15 (rheumatologic regimen).
