Repeat courses are guided by clinical response and B-cell repopulation (CD19+/CD20+ counts), typically every 6–12 months or sooner if relapse. Pre-medication with acetaminophen, antihistamine, and corticosteroids reduces infusion reactions.

Monitoring and adverse effects: Baseline screening for hepatitis B and C, tuberculosis, and evaluation of vaccination status is required. Reactivation of hepatitis B is a serious risk; appropriate prophylaxis for HBV carriers is mandatory. Infusion reactions are common during the first infusion and are manageable. Rituximab increases infection risk, including rare progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML) due to JC virus. Measure immunoglobulin levels (IgG) before and periodically during therapy because hypogammaglobulinemia can develop and predispose to infections; IVIG replacement may be required in selected patients.

Tofacitinib and other JAK inhibitors
Mechanism: Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitors (e.g., tofacitinib, baricitinib, ruxolitinib) block intracellular signaling downstream of cytokine receptors by inhibiting JAK family kinases (JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, TYK2 depending on agent). This interrupts signaling pathways for many proinflammatory cytokines (e.g., type I interferons, IL-6, IL-2 family) implicated in myositis, especially in dermatomyositis where a type I interferon signature is prevalent.

Evidence and emerging use: Case reports, small case series, and early-phase studies suggest that JAK inhibitors may be effective in refractory dermatomyositis, particularly for cutaneous disease and some systemic manifestations. Tofacitinib (typical dose 5 mg twice daily) has been used off-label, and baricitinib (2–4 mg/day) has shown promise in limited data. Controlled trials are emerging. JAK inhibitors may be particularly attractive for patients with interferon-driven disease or refractory skin disease.

Dosing and monitoring: Tofacitinib is typically dosed at 5 mg twice daily or extended-release 11 mg once daily; dose adjustments are required for renal impairment. Baseline screening similar to other biologics: TB, hepatitis B/C, CBC, LFTs, lipid panel, and vaccination status. Monitor CBC, LFTs, and lipids after initiation. Adverse effects include increased risk of infection (including herpes zoster), laboratory abnormalities (cytopenias, transaminase and lipid elevations), and, in some populations, increased risk of major adverse cardiovascular events, venous thromboembolism, and malignancy as suggested by safety signals in rheumatoid arthritis populations with certain JAK inhibitors. Risk–benefit assessment is essential.

Emerging agents
Other biologic therapies under investigation target different pathways: anti-TNF agents (limited efficacy and risk profile), abatacept (CTLA4-Ig) to modulate T-cell co-stimulation, anti–type I interferon therapies, complement inhibitors, and agents targeting the neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn) to reduce pathogenic autoantibody levels. Clinical trial data are evolving; patients with refractory disease may be candidates for enrollment in trials.

Clinical considerations when using biologics
Use of biologic agents requires careful baseline infectious disease screening, consideration of concomitant immunosuppressive drugs, and vaccination planning (ideally complete inactivated vaccines before initiation; live vaccines generally contraindicated during therapy). Cost and access considerations are substantial. Sequential or combination use (e.g., rituximab + conventional agents) is common, but combination therapy increases infection risk and requires close monitoring.

# Intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG): mechanisms, indications, dosing regimens and adverse effects

Mechanisms of action
Intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) is pooled human IgG from donors and exerts pleiotropic immunomodulatory effects. Proposed mechanisms relevant to inflammatory myopathies include neutralization of autoantibodies, modulation of Fc receptor expression on innate immune cells, inhibition of complement activation, modulation of cytokine networks, interference with antigen presentation, and expansion of regulatory T-cell populations. IVIG can reduce muscle inflammation and improve strength, particularly in dermatomyositis and some cases of immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy.

Indications and evidence
IVIG is an approved therapy for refractory dermatomyositis (parenteral immunoglobulin [IVIG] has randomized controlled trial evidence showing benefit) and is widely used for severe or refractory cutaneous manifestations, dysphagia, or rapidly progressive disease. It is particularly useful when rapid immunomodulation is desired, when other immunosuppressants are contraindicated (e.g., pregnancy in some contexts), or when infections / comorbidities preclude stronger immunosuppression. IVIG may be less effective in inclusion body myositis. In IMNM with anti–HMGCR antibodies, IVIG has shown effectiveness in some series, particularly in statin-related cases.

Dosing regimens
The most commonly used induction regimen is IVIG 2 g/kg total dose given over 2–5 days (e.g., 0.4 g/kg/day for 5 days, or 1 g/kg/day for 2 days). For maintenance, regimens vary: 1 g/kg every 4 weeks, or 2 g/kg every 4–8 weeks depending on clinical response and tolerability. Individualization is required: some patients maintain remission with infusions every 4–8 weeks; others require more frequent dosing initially. When dosing for obese patients, ideal body weight adjustment or capped doses are sometimes used to avoid excessive dosing; local protocols vary.

Monitoring and adverse effects
Before infusion, obtain baseline renal function, CBC, and consider serum IgA measurement (to assess risk of anaphylactic reactions in IgA-deficient patients with anti-IgA antibodies). Monitor vital signs during infusion for infusion reactions. Common adverse effects are infusion-related: headache (including aseptic meningitis), flushing, fever, chills, myalgias, and nausea. Serious but uncommon adverse effects include thromboembolic events (venous and arterial thrombosis), acute kidney injury (more frequent with sucrose-containing IVIG preparations and in patients with pre-existing renal insufficiency, advanced age, diabetes, volume depletion), hemolytic anemia (due to blood group antibodies in the product), and anaphylaxis (rare, especially in IgA-deficient patients). Risk mitigation includes using slower infusion rates in high-risk patients, adequate hydration, choosing products without sucrose in renal-risk patients, and prophylactic measures for headaches (e.g., pre-infusion acetaminophen, antihistamine, and hydration).

Practical considerations
IVIG is expensive and resource-intensive. It is typically used when rapid effect is needed, when other agents have failed, or in specific clinical scenarios such as severe dysphagia, cutaneous DM not responsive to topical/systemic agents, or IMNM with severe weakness. Documenting objective clinical improvement (strength testing, functional status) guides continuation and intervals. Concomitant therapies (corticosteroids, steroid-sparing agents) are usually continued during IVIG induction and may be adjusted as response allows.

# Treatment selection principles based on disease severity, organ involvement and comorbidities

Principles of individualized therapy
Therapeutic decisions in inflammatory myopathies hinge on disease severity, organ system involvement, myositis subtype, autoantibody profile, comorbidities, pregnancy status, infection risk, and patient preferences. A stepwise and often combination approach is used: induction with glucocorticoids for rapid control, early addition of a steroid-sparing agent to permit taper, and escalation to biologics or IVIG for refractory or severe disease.

Severity categories and corresponding strategies
- Mild to moderate muscle-predominant disease without major extramuscular manifestations: Begin oral prednisone (0.5–1.0 mg/kg/day) and start a steroid-sparing agent (commonly methotrexate 15–25 mg weekly or azathioprine 2 mg/kg/day). Monitor response and taper steroids over weeks–months as improvement permits.

- Severe muscle weakness, rapidly progressive disease, dysphagia, respiratory muscle involvement, or significant extramuscular disease (e.g., myocarditis, severe ILD): Use aggressive induction with IV methylprednisolone pulses (e.g., 0.5–1 g/day for 3 days) followed by high-dose oral prednisone, and simultaneously initiate a steroid-sparing agent. Early consideration of IVIG and/or rituximab is appropriate, particularly for dysphagia, cutaneous DM refractory to other agents, or certain autoantibody-associated phenotypes (e.g., anti-SRP, anti-HMGCR, or anti-MDA5 with rapidly progressive ILD). For severe ILD, combine immunosuppressants (e.g., high-dose steroids + tacrolimus or cyclosporine ± cyclophosphamide) based on pulmonary and rheumatologic expertise.

- Refractory disease: For patients not improving on conventional DMARDs and steroids, consider IVIG, rituximab, or JAK inhibitors depending on phenotype, prior therapy, and safety considerations. For refractory cutaneous disease, JAK inhibitors have increasing evidence. For IMNM and anti-HMGCR–positive disease, IVIG is frequently effective.

Organ-specific considerations
- Interstitial lung disease (ILD): Some myositis subtypes (anti-synthetase, anti-MDA5) have prominent ILD. Agents with anti-T-cell effects (calcineurin inhibitors such as tacrolimus, cyclosporine), MMF, and cyclophosphamide are commonly used; early aggressive therapy is warranted for rapidly progressive ILD. Rituximab is also used in refractory cases. Pulmonary specialists should be involved early.

- Cutaneous disease in dermatomyositis: Skin disease may be refractory to systemic immunosuppression; antimalarials (hydroxychloroquine 200–400 mg/day) are often used for cutaneous DM, although photosensitivity and limited efficacy in some patients limit benefit. IVIG and JAK inhibitors have documented benefit for refractory dermatomyositis skin disease. Sun protection and topical therapies are adjuncts.

- Dysphagia and aspiration risk: Dysphagia is an indication for early IVIG and more aggressive therapy because of aspiration risk and nutritional compromise.

- Cardiac involvement: Myocarditis requires prompt, aggressive immunosuppression (high-dose steroids ± cyclophosphamide or other agents) and cardiology input.

Comorbidities and safety considerations
- Pregnancy: Avoid methotrexate, MMF, and mycophenolate in pregnancy. Azathioprine and IVIG are considered relatively safer options; rituximab has been used but requires nuanced counseling regarding timing and neonatal B-cell effects. Preconception counseling and coordination with obstetrics are essential.

- Infection or chronic viral infection: Screen for TB, HBV, HCV, and consider prophylaxis or alternative agents if infections are present. Rituximab requires HBV screening and prophylaxis for carriers; high immunosuppression may require PJP prophylaxis (TMP-SMX) when patients are on high-dose steroids plus another immunosuppressant.

- Malignancy risk: Dermatomyositis has an association with malignancy; initial cancer screening (age-appropriate plus attention to DM-associated malignancy patterns) is important. Presence of malignancy influences immunosuppressive choices, and some paraneoplastic myopathies respond better to cancer-directed therapy.

Decision points and monitoring of response
Clinical decision points include assessing response at 4–12 weeks for conventional agents; inadequate improvement after an appropriate trial (often 3–6 months) typically prompts escalation. Objective measures include manual muscle testing, quantitative muscle testing, functional scales (e.g., MMT-8, patient-reported outcomes), pulmonary function testing for ILD, repeat CK (not always reflective), and skin activity assessment. Tapering of immunosuppression should be considered after sustained clinical remission (often at least 6–12 months) but individualized due to relapse risk.

# Supportive care and rehabilitation (physiotherapy, nutritional support, speech/swallow therapy)

Role of multidisciplinary supportive care
Supportive measures are fundamental components of management, often as important as immunosuppression for restoring function and preventing complications. A multidisciplinary team — physical and occupational therapists, speech-language pathologists, dietitians, pulmonologists, cardiologists, dermatologists, and mental health professionals — should be engaged early.

Physiotherapy and exercise
Once significant inflammatory activity is controlled and there is medical stability, graded exercise and physiotherapy are safe and beneficial. Historically, concerns about exercise worsening inflammation have been allayed by trials showing that tailored, progressive aerobic and resistance training improves muscle strength, endurance, physical function, and quality of life. Programs should start with low-intensity isometric and concentric exercises, gradually increasing to resistance and aerobic conditioning as tolerated. Avoid excessive eccentric and maximal-load activities during active severe inflammation to reduce risk of muscle damage. Physical therapists experienced with inflammatory myopathies can design individualized home and supervised programs focusing on core strength, posture, and activities of daily living. Occupational therapy can assist with adaptive equipment and energy conservation strategies.

Nutritional support
Malnutrition, weight loss, or obesity may complicate inflammatory myopathies. Dysphagia increases aspiration risk and may necessitate texture modification or enteral nutrition. Dietitian involvement helps optimize caloric and protein intake to support muscle repair and immune competence, manage steroid-induced weight gain and metabolic complications, and address micronutrient needs (e.g., calcium and vitamin D for bone health). Monitor for and manage dysphagia-related dehydration or aspiration pneumonia.

Speech and swallow therapy
Dysphagia affects a subset of patients with dermatomyositis and polymyositis and is associated with morbidity. Early evaluation by a speech-language pathologist, bedside swallow assessments, and instrumental studies (videofluoroscopic swallow study or fiberoptic endoscopic evaluation of swallowing) guide therapy. Interventions include swallowing maneuvers, exercises to strengthen oropharyngeal muscles, diet modification, and when necessary, temporary enteral feeding (nasogastric or percutaneous endoscopic gastrostomy) to maintain nutrition and reduce aspiration risk. Improvement in dysphagia often parallels immunosuppressive therapy but rehabilitation measures are critical adjuncts.

Prevention and management of complications
Osteoporosis prevention should be instituted for patients expected to receive prolonged glucocorticoids: baseline DEXA scan and calcium/vitamin D supplementation, with bisphosphonates for patients at high fracture risk. Vaccinations (inactivated) should be updated before initiating immunosuppression when possible (influenza, pneumococcal, HPV, hepatitis B). Live vaccines are contraindicated during significant immunosuppression. Prophylaxis against Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP) should be considered for patients receiving high-dose glucocorticoids (e.g., prednisone ≥20 mg/day for several weeks) combined with other immunosuppressants; trimethoprim–sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX) is typically used (e.g., TMP-SMX DS 3 times weekly or daily depending on protocol). Monitor for steroid-related metabolic complications and treat accordingly.

Psychosocial support, vocational rehabilitation and patient education
Chronic disease management requires attention to psychological impact: anxiety, depression, and reduced quality of life are common. Referral to mental health professionals, support groups, patient education about disease, medication side effects, and strategies for self-monitoring empower patients. Vocational rehabilitation and social work can assist with disability evaluation, workplace modifications, and financial resources.

Follow-up and long-term monitoring
Regular follow-up assesses disease control and medication toxicity. Schedule visits to track muscle strength, function, CK levels, pulmonary function for ILD patients, and organ-specific monitoring as indicated. Periodic cancer surveillance (age-appropriate and DM-directed) is recommended, particularly in the initial years after diagnosis when risk is higher.

Conclusion

Effective management of dermatomyositis and other inflammatory myopathies integrates rapid and appropriate immunosuppression with long-term steroid-sparing strategies, biologic use for refractory disease, or IVIG for specific indications. Treatment selection must be individualized based on severity, organ involvement, comorbidities, reproductive plans, and safety considerations. Concurrent supportive care — physiotherapy, nutritional optimization, swallowing rehabilitation, and preventive measures — is essential for functional recovery and minimizing complications. Close monitoring for therapeutic efficacy and adverse effects, plus coordination across specialties, underpins successful outcomes.

Introduction

Immunosuppressive therapies—whether prescribed for autoimmune disease, organ transplantation, hematologic malignancy, or other indications—substantially increase the risk of infection from reactivation of latent pathogens and from new exposures. Safe initiation and maintenance of these therapies therefore requires systematic pre-treatment screening for latent and chronic infections, targeted prophylactic strategies against opportunistic pathogens, and ongoing monitoring for drug toxicities and vaccine responsiveness. This chapter reviews the evidence-based approach to infection screening (with emphasis on tuberculosis and viral serologies), the role of viral serologies such as varicella-zoster virus (VZV) and cytomegalovirus (CMV) in clinical decision-making, indications and options for prophylaxis against opportunistic infections including Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP), laboratory monitoring protocols for immunosuppressive drugs (including thiopurine methyltransferase testing prior to azathioprine), and practical vaccination guidance before and during immunosuppression. The focus is clinical: how to interpret tests, when to act, and how to integrate these processes into patient care.

# Screening for latent tuberculosis (IGRA/TST) and HBV/HCV/HIV serologies

Latent tuberculosis infection (LTBI)
Reactivation of latent Mycobacterium tuberculosis poses substantial risk in patients receiving many forms of immunosuppression (especially tumor necrosis factor inhibitors, high-dose corticosteroids, certain biologics and small molecules). Two principal tests are used to screen for LTBI: the tuberculin skin test (TST, Mantoux) and interferon-gamma release assays (IGRAs; e.g., QuantiFERON-TB Gold Plus, T-SPOT.TB).

TST places purified protein derivative intradermally and is read at 48–72 hours as the transverse diameter of induration. Interpretation depends on risk: thresholds commonly used are ≥5 mm (high-risk, e.g., recent contact with TB, immunosuppressed), ≥10 mm (moderate risk), and ≥15 mm (no known risk factors). TST has limitations: false-positives with prior BCG vaccination or exposure to non-tuberculous mycobacteria, and false-negatives in immunosuppressed patients due to anergy.

IGRAs measure interferon-gamma release by T cells in response to TB-specific antigens absent from BCG strains (ESAT-6, CFP-10), making them more specific in BCG-vaccinated persons and less affected by non-tuberculous mycobacteria. IGRAs require blood draw and controlled laboratory processing; indeterminate results can occur with lymphopenia or immunosuppression.

Choice of test: For most adults, an IGRA is the preferred initial test when available, particularly in BCG-vaccinated individuals or those with prior TST. In persons from low-resource settings or when IGRA is not available, TST remains acceptable. For patients with high pre-test probability or immunosuppression, consider performing both tests if initial results are discordant or if clinical suspicion remains high.

Action on positive tests: A positive TST or IGRA suggests LTBI, and further evaluation with chest radiography (posterior-anterior and lateral as indicated) is required to exclude active pulmonary TB before starting LTBI therapy. If chest imaging or symptoms suggest active disease, obtain sputum microscopy, nucleic acid amplification testing, and culture; delay or modify immunosuppression until active disease is treated or judged unlikely. If active TB is excluded, initiate LTBI treatment per local guidelines (e.g., isoniazid daily for 6–9 months with pyridoxine, or shorter rifamycin-based regimens), and coordinate timing relative to immunosuppression: where feasible, complete at least a portion of LTBI therapy (or start LTBI therapy) prior to initiating high-risk immunosuppressives such as anti-TNF agents.

HBV, HCV, and HIV serologies
Chronic viral hepatitis and HIV can flare or progress under immunosuppression, and their presence influences choice of therapy and need for antiviral prophylaxis. Baseline testing is mandatory in most practices prior to moderate or high-intensity immunosuppression.

Hepatitis B virus (HBV): Key baseline tests are hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg), total anti–hepatitis B core antibody (anti-HBc total), and hepatitis B surface antibody (anti-HBs). Interpretation:
- HBsAg positive indicates active infection (chronic if >6 months). These patients are at risk of HBV reactivation with immunosuppression and generally require antiviral prophylaxis (entecavir, tenofovir disoproxil fumarate, or tenofovir alafenamide) rather than reactive treatment only.
- Anti-HBc positive with HBsAg negative indicates resolved infection (occult infection possible). The risk of reactivation varies by immunosuppressive regimen; many such patients require either prophylactic antivirals or close monitoring (HBV DNA and ALT testing), especially if receiving B-cell–depleting agents (rituximab) or high-risk regimens.
- Anti-HBs positive with anti-HBc negative indicates immunity from vaccination.

Pre-treatment HBV DNA quantification is recommended if HBsAg positive or if anti-HBc positive and immunosuppression is planned. In general, for HBsAg positive patients starting moderate-to-high risk immunosuppression, begin antiviral prophylaxis before or at the start of immunosuppressive therapy and continue for at least 6–12 months after cessation of immunosuppression (longer for B-cell–depleting agents; some recommend 12–18 months).

Hepatitis C virus (HCV): Screen with anti-HCV antibody followed by HCV RNA if antibody positive. Chronic HCV infection can progress during immunosuppression and also poses considerations for hepatotoxicity and drug–drug interactions (direct-acting antivirals and immunosuppressants). Many centers seek to evaluate and treat HCV prior to elective immunosuppression when feasible, given high cure rates with direct-acting antivirals.

HIV: Baseline HIV antigen/antibody testing (combined p24 antigen/anti-HIV antibody assay) is essential because undiagnosed HIV confers altered infection risks, different vaccination recommendations, and drug-interaction considerations. If positive, quantify HIV RNA and CD4 count and initiate HIV care. Some forms of immunosuppression are contraindicated or require tailoring in uncontrolled HIV.

Integration into workflow: Standard pre-immunosuppression panels should include HBsAg, anti-HBc, anti-HBs, HCV antibody (and HCV RNA if positive), and HIV Ag/Ab. Document vaccination history and prior TB testing. Positive or indeterminate results should prompt consultation with infectious disease or hepatology when appropriate and tailored prophylaxis plans.

# Viral serologies (VZV, CMV) and implications for management

Varicella-zoster virus (VZV)
VZV serostatus (VZV IgG) prior to immunosuppression distinguishes those susceptible to primary varicella from those with prior immunity. Primary varicella (chickenpox) in immunocompromised hosts can be severe, with disseminated disease, pneumonia, and high mortality. VZV IgG positive patients are generally considered immune; IgM is typically not used for baseline screening.

If a patient lacks VZV IgG (seronegative), vaccination should be considered before immunosuppression, but timing and vaccine choice require attention. The older live-attenuated varicella vaccine is contraindicated during significant immunosuppression and should be administered well in advance (generally at least 4 weeks) of planned immunosuppression. For adults, varicella vaccination policy varies by region and patient age.

Regarding zoster prevention, two vaccines are relevant: the live-attenuated zoster vaccine (Zostavax) and the recombinant zoster vaccine (RZV; e.g., Shingrix). Live zoster vaccine is contraindicated in immunocompromised individuals. RZV is a non-live, adjuvanted subunit vaccine and is preferred for immunocompromised adults when available; many guidelines now recommend RZV for adults who are or will be immunocompromised, although the timing relative to initiating immunosuppression should be individualized. For example, when possible give RZV at least 2 weeks before starting moderate immunosuppression (some experts prefer 2–4 weeks) and complete the two-dose series as feasible. If immunosuppression is already established, RZV may still be given in many patients, but efficacy can be lower; consult guidance specific to the immunosuppressive agent and patient condition.

Cytomegalovirus (CMV)
CMV serostatus (anti-CMV IgG) is particularly important in transplantation (solid-organ and hematopoietic stem cell transplantation), where donor and recipient serostatus (D+/R−, D+/R+, D−/R+, D−/R−) defines risk of CMV disease and informs preemptive surveillance or universal prophylaxis strategies. In nontransplant immunosuppressed patients (e.g., those on biologics), CMV reactivation is less commonly a primary concern but can occur in profoundly immunocompromised hosts.

If the recipient is CMV IgG negative and the donor is positive (D+/R−), risk of primary CMV infection is high and prophylactic antivirals (valganciclovir) or intensive preemptive monitoring are often employed. In R+ recipients, risk is lower but reactivation can occur with intensive immunosuppression; many centers adopt preemptive strategies using regular quantitative CMV PCR to detect early viremia and initiate therapy before tissue-invasive disease.

Implications for management: For both VZV and CMV, serologies guide (1) whether vaccination is feasible and indicated prior to immunosuppression, (2) need for antiviral prophylaxis (CMV in transplant), and (3) the intensity of surveillance during immunosuppression (e.g., serial CMV PCR monitoring). When considering antiviral prophylaxis, also account for antiviral toxicities, drug–drug interactions, and renal dosing.

# Prophylaxis for opportunistic infections (Pneumocystis jirovecii prophylaxis options and indications)

General principles
Opportunistic infections (OIs) cause high morbidity and mortality in immunosuppressed patients. OI prophylaxis aims to prevent infections for which risk is predictable on the basis of the degree and type of immunosuppression. Decisions are individualized by the underlying disease, intensity and duration of immunosuppressive therapy, comorbidities, and concomitant therapies.

Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP) prophylaxis
PJP is a prototypical prophylaxis target. Indications for PJP prophylaxis vary by guideline but converge around similar principles: patients with impaired cell-mediated immunity are at highest risk. Common triggers for prophylaxis include:

- High-dose systemic corticosteroids: many guidelines recommend prophylaxis for patients receiving prednisone (or equivalent) ≥20 mg/day for ≥4 weeks. Risk is modulated by concomitant immunosuppressives.
- Hematologic malignancy, hematopoietic stem cell transplantation, or solid organ transplantation: prophylaxis is standard in many regimens.
- Certain biologic agents: for example, patients on prolonged or combination regimens including agents like rituximab, or multiple immunosuppressives, may require prophylaxis. Patients receiving calcineurin inhibitors plus mycophenolate with additional risk factors often require PJP prophylaxis.
- HIV: PJP prophylaxis when CD4 <200 cells/μL; this is a well-established, evidence-based threshold in HIV care (CD4 count threshold for starting prophylaxis is <200 cells/μL).

First-line regimen: Trimethoprim–sulfamethoxazole (TMP–SMX)
TMP–SMX is the most effective and preferred agent for PJP prophylaxis.

Dosing options:
- Single-strength tablet (TMP 80 mg/SMX 400 mg) orally once daily.
- Double-strength tablet (TMP 160 mg/SMX 800 mg) orally three times weekly (e.g., Monday-Wednesday-Friday).
- Alternative dosing regimens (e.g., double-strength once daily) are sometimes used.

TMP–SMX also provides protection against other organisms (Toxoplasma gondii) in some settings. Adverse effects to monitor include hypersensitivity (rashes, Stevens–Johnson syndrome—rare), cytopenias (neutropenia), renal dysfunction (interstitial nephritis), and hyperkalemia. In patients with sulfa allergy or intolerance, desensitization can be considered if clinically important and feasible.

Alternative regimens
When TMP–SMX is contraindicated or not tolerated, alternatives include:

- Dapsone (100 mg orally daily): requires normal glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) activity (risk of hemolysis in deficiency) and may cause methemoglobinemia.
- Atovaquone (1500 mg orally daily with fat-containing meal): well tolerated but expensive and less well studied.
- Aerosolized pentamidine (300 mg via nebulization monthly): effective for preventing pulmonary PJP but provides less extrapulmonary protection and has disadvantages including bronchospasm risk and logistical complexity.
Choice among alternatives depends on patient comorbidities (e.g., G6PD status), ability to tolerate oral agents, and cost/availability.

Duration of prophylaxis
Duration depends on the ongoing level of immunosuppression and recovery of immune function. In many non-HIV settings, prophylaxis continues while immunosuppression is at risk-producing levels and for a period after tapering; for corticosteroids, continue while prednisone ≥20 mg/day and for at least 4 weeks after tapering below threshold, with some clinicians extending longer if other risk factors exist. In transplant and hematologic settings, duration is protocol-driven (e.g., at least 6–12 months post-transplant, longer with ongoing high-risk therapy). For HIV, prophylaxis is discontinued when CD4 count rises above 200 cells/μL for at least 3 months on antiretroviral therapy.

Other opportunistic prophylaxis
- Toxoplasma gondii: TMP–SMX prophylaxis generally covers Toxoplasma in susceptible patients (e.g., HIV with CD4 <100 cells/μL and seropositive).
- Herpesvirus prophylaxis (HSV/VZV): Acyclovir/valacyclovir prophylaxis is used in selected high-risk groups (e.g., some transplant recipients, patients receiving alemtuzumab) to prevent severe herpesvirus disease.
- Fungal prophylaxis: Fluconazole or mold-active agents are employed in specific high-risk hematologic or transplant populations (e.g., neutropenia, GVHD).
- Mycobacterial prophylaxis: In certain regimens (e.g., TNF inhibitors in TB-endemic regions), isoniazid for LTBI is prophylaxis for TB reactivation.

Clinical decision points
Decisions regarding OI prophylaxis require balancing risks and benefits: assess the absolute risk of the infection without prophylaxis, drug toxicities and interactions, and patient preferences. Many centers have protocolized criteria for PJP and CMV prophylaxis; when in doubt, consult infectious disease specialists.

# Monitoring protocols for drug toxicities (CBC, LFTs, renal function, TPMT testing for azathioprine)

Rationale and general approach
Immunosuppressive agents have predictable organ-specific toxicities and hematologic effects. Baseline testing and scheduled monitoring detect adverse effects early, allow dose adjustment, and reduce morbidity. Monitoring frequency depends on the drug, patient comorbidities, and phase of therapy (induction vs maintenance).

Common baseline tests
Before initiating many immunosuppressants, obtain:
- Complete blood count (CBC) with differential (baseline cytopenias place patients at higher risk for severe myelosuppression).
- Liver function tests (LFTs): AST, ALT, alkaline phosphatase, total bilirubin.
- Renal function: serum creatinine, estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR).
- Blood glucose and lipids if starting agents that affect metabolic parameters (e.g., corticosteroids).
- Specific drug-related baseline tests as indicated (e.g., chest radiograph for TB screening, hepatitis and HIV serologies as above).

Monitoring by drug class/examples

Azathioprine and 6-mercaptopurine
Azathioprine (AZA) is a thiopurine prodrug converted to 6-mercaptopurine and active thioguanine nucleotides. Myelosuppression is the major toxicity; hepatotoxicity also occurs.

Thiopurine methyltransferase (TPMT) testing: Before starting AZA or 6-mercaptopurine, screen for TPMT enzyme activity (phenotype) or TPMT genotype (common variant alleles). TPMT deficiency is associated with high levels of active thioguanine nucleotides and severe, potentially life-threatening myelosuppression. TPMT test results guide dosing:
- Normal TPMT activity: start standard doses (e.g., azathioprine 1–2 mg/kg/day) with routine monitoring.
- Intermediate activity: begin at reduced dose (e.g., 30–70% of standard) with close CBC monitoring.
- Absent activity: avoid standard thiopurines or use dramatically reduced dosing with extreme caution; consideration of alternative agents is common.

NUDT15 genotyping: NUDT15 variants are particularly relevant in people of Asian or Hispanic ancestry and confer risk of thiopurine-induced leukopenia; many centers incorporate NUDT15 testing alongside TPMT.

Ongoing monitoring: CBC and LFTs should be checked frequently after initiation (e.g., every 1–2 weeks for the first month or two, then monthly for several months, then every 3 months if stable). Any unexplained fever, sore throat, or bruising prompts immediate CBC.

Methotrexate
Methotrexate can cause hepatotoxicity, cytopenias, and pulmonary toxicity. Baseline CBC, AST/ALT, serum creatinine are required. Folic acid supplementation reduces some toxicities. Monitoring typically includes CBC, LFTs, and creatinine every 1–2 months early in therapy, extending to every 1–3 months once stable. Evaluate for pulmonary symptoms; obtain chest imaging and consider pulmonary specialist input for suspected methotrexate pneumonitis.

Mycophenolate mofetil
Mycophenolate predominantly causes gastrointestinal side effects and bone marrow suppression. Baseline CBC and LFTs, and periodic CBC monitoring (monthly initially, then every 1–3 months), with dose reduction for cytopenias.

Calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus)
These agents are nephrotoxic and can cause hypertension, neurotoxicity, and metabolic disturbance. Baseline renal function and blood pressure should be documented. Therapeutic drug monitoring (trough levels) guides dosing and prevents toxicity. Check renal function frequently (e.g., weekly to monthly) and monitor electrolytes and blood pressure.

mTOR inhibitors (sirolimus, everolimus)
mTOR inhibitors cause hyperlipidemia, delayed wound healing, cytopenias, and proteinuria. Monitor CBC, lipid profile, LFTs, and renal function; therapeutic drug monitoring for trough levels is standard in transplant settings.

Biologic agents (e.g., anti-TNF, rituximab)
In addition to infection screening, monitor for cytopenias and infusion reactions. Rituximab (anti-CD20) can cause late-onset neutropenia and hypogammaglobulinemia; monitor CBC and immunoglobulin levels (IgG) periodically, and consider immunoglobulin replacement for recurrent infections with low IgG. Check for HBV reactivation risk in anti-CD20 recipients; prolonged antiviral prophylaxis may be required.

Corticosteroids
Systemic corticosteroids cause hyperglycemia, hypertension, osteoporosis, adrenal suppression, and increased infection risk. Monitor blood pressure, glucose, weight, and counsel on bone protection (calcium, vitamin D, bisphosphonates as indicated). Tapering strategies are important to mitigate adrenal insufficiency.

Frequency and thresholds
Monitoring frequency should be most intensive during the initial weeks to months of therapy, when adverse events are most likely to appear. Trigger values prompting dose reduction or discontinuation vary by drug; examples:
- Neutrophils <1.0 x 10^9/L or an absolute neutrophil count fall >50% from baseline often prompt holding the drug and rechecking urgently.
- Transaminases >3 times the upper limit of normal (ULN) for many drugs warrant dose reduction or discontinuation and evaluation for alternative causes (viral hepatitis, alcohol, etc.).
- Rise in serum creatinine or decline in eGFR beyond established thresholds for nephrotoxic agents triggers dose adjustments or drug substitution.

Documentation and patient education
Provide patients with written guidance about which symptoms warrant urgent evaluation (fever, signs of infection, easy bruising/bleeding, yellowing of skin, new cough or dyspnea). Ensure coordination between prescribers and primary care for monitoring labs and immunizations.

# Vaccination recommendations before and during immunosuppression

Principles of vaccination in immunosuppressed patients
Vaccination reduces the risk of vaccine-preventable infections that can be severe in immunocompromised hosts. However, live-attenuated vaccines are generally contraindicated during moderate-to-severe immunosuppression because of the risk of vaccine-associated disease. Inactivated vaccines are safe but immunogenicity may be reduced; therefore, when possible, complete vaccination series early—ideally before immunosuppression—to maximize immune response.

Timing relative to immunosuppression
- Prefer to administer vaccines at least 2 weeks before starting immunosuppression for inactivated vaccines; some experts recommend 2–4 weeks for sufficient antibody development.
- Live vaccines (e.g., measles-mumps-rubella, varicella, live zoster) should generally be given at least 4 weeks (often recommended 4 weeks, sometimes 3–4 weeks) before initiating immunosuppressive therapy.
- After stopping immunosuppression, timing of when live vaccines can be given varies by agent and immune reconstitution. For many biologics and conventional immunosuppressants, waiting at least 3 months after cessation is common, but for B-cell–depleting agents (e.g., rituximab) immune recovery may take 6–12 months; specialist guidance is recommended.

Specific vaccine recommendations (generalized; adapt to local guidelines)

Influenza
- Annual inactivated influenza vaccine is recommended for all immunosuppressed patients (including those with chronic disease or on immunosuppression). It can be administered at any time during the influenza season and, when possible, before exposure to influenza season or immunosuppression.
- Live-attenuated influenza vaccine (intranasal) is contraindicated in immunocompromised persons and household contacts should receive inactivated vaccine when possible.

Pneumococcal vaccines
- Two types: pneumococcal conjugate vaccine (PCV13/PCV15/PCV20 depending on availability) and pneumococcal polysaccharide vaccine (PPSV23). For immunocompromised adults, many guidelines recommend PCV followed by PPSV23 at a designated interval (commonly 8 weeks to 1 year depending on sequence and vaccines used). Check up-to-date local recommendations for sequencing and timing.
- Immunocompromised patients have higher risk of invasive pneumococcal disease and derive substantial benefit from vaccination.

Hepatitis B
- Vaccinate susceptible patients (anti-HBs negative) before immunosuppression when possible. Higher or additional vaccine doses may be needed to achieve seroprotection in immunosuppressed hosts; post-vaccination anti-HBs testing can guide need for revaccination or booster.

Human papillomavirus (HPV)
- Recommend HPV vaccination for eligible age groups (often up to 26 years; some guidelines extend to 45 years on shared clinical decision-making), including those who may become or are immunocompromised. Non-live vaccine and safe in immunosuppressed patients.

Varicella and zoster vaccines
- For varicella-susceptible patients (VZV IgG negative), varicella vaccination should be administered before planned immunosuppression. This is a live vaccine and is contraindicated during immunosuppression.
- For herpes zoster prevention, the recombinant zoster vaccine (RZV; non-live) is preferred for immunocompromised adults when permitted by regulatory guidance; it should ideally be given before immunosuppression if possible. Live zoster vaccine is contraindicated once immunosuppression is present.

Meningococcal and Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib)
- Vaccinate persons as indicated by underlying conditions or planned therapy (e.g., complement inhibitor therapy or asplenia). Immunocompromised patients and those on certain complement inhibitors require meningococcal vaccination prior to therapy initiation.

Other considerations
- Household contacts: Encourage up-to-date inactivated vaccinations for household members (e.g., inactivated influenza) to reduce exposure risk. Live vaccines for household contacts generally do not contraindicate contact (e.g., MMR or varicella vaccines), but specific precautions may apply in some scenarios (e.g., vaccine strain shedding for oral polio vaccine; note oral polio vaccine is rarely used in many countries).
- Post-vaccination serologic testing: For vaccines where serologic correlates are available and critical (e.g., hepatitis B), checking post-vaccine titers may be useful to confirm protection in immunocompromised patients.
- Reduced vaccine efficacy: Immunosuppression can blunt vaccine responses. When feasible, vaccinate before starting therapy. Even partial protection may reduce disease severity.

Clinical decision-making and documentation
Vaccination planning should be an explicit part of pre-immunosuppression counseling and workflow. Document vaccine history, serologic results, planned timing, and rationale for deferring live vaccines. Coordinate with primary care, infectious disease, and transplant teams to ensure appropriate immunization.

Conclusion

Preventing and managing infections in patients who require immunosuppression is a multifaceted endeavor. Adequate pre-treatment screening (for TB and viral infections such as HBV, HCV, HIV, VZV, and CMV) informs risk stratification and prophylactic plans. Targeted prophylaxis—most importantly for Pneumocystis jirovecii—should be applied when expected benefit exceeds risks, with TMP–SMX as first-line and rational alternatives available. Robust monitoring protocols (CBC, LFTs, renal function, TPMT/NUDT15 testing before thiopurine therapy) detect toxicities early and guide dose modification. Vaccination is a critical preventive measure: inactivated vaccines should be given whenever possible before immunosuppression, and live vaccines should be administered only when safe and timed appropriately. Integrating these practices into routine pre-treatment workflows and maintaining communication among specialists, primary care, and patients optimizes safety and outcomes for immunosuppressed individuals.

Introduction

Immunosuppressive therapy is central to the management of immune-mediated myopathies and other autoimmune diseases. While these agents frequently provide major clinical benefit, they carry distinct and potentially serious toxicities. Recognition of predictable adverse effects, routine monitoring to detect early complications, and strategies to prevent or mitigate harm are essential skills for clinicians caring for patients on immunosuppression. This chapter reviews the major complications of commonly used immunosuppressive agents, focusing on hematologic toxicities, hepatic and renal injury, infection risk including opportunistic pathogens, drug–drug interactions and peri-procedural management, and infusion-related reactions with strategies for prevention and response.

# Hematologic toxicities (cytopenias, hemolytic anemia) and monitoring

Hematologic toxicities are among the most common and clinically consequential adverse effects of immunosuppressive drugs. The spectrum includes bone marrow suppression causing neutropenia, anemia and thrombocytopenia; drug-induced immune cytopenias (autoimmune hemolytic anemia, immune thrombocytopenia); and hemolysis mediated by specific drug mechanisms. Recognition requires knowledge of which agents are implicated, typical time courses, pathophysiology, and the appropriate laboratory surveillance and thresholds for intervention.

Pathophysiology and offending agents
- Antimetabolites (azathioprine, 6-mercaptopurine, mycophenolate mofetil, methotrexate) commonly cause bone marrow suppression by interfering with nucleotide synthesis and DNA replication in rapidly dividing marrow progenitors. The risk correlates with dose and host factors (e.g., TPMT deficiency increases risk with azathioprine/6-MP).
- Alkylating agents (cyclophosphamide) produce direct cytotoxicity to marrow progenitors and can cause profound and sometimes prolonged cytopenias.
- Calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus) are less likely to cause frank marrow aplasia but can produce thrombotic microangiopathy or anemia via hemolysis in rare cases.
- Biologics and targeted therapies (rituximab, tocilizumab, JAK inhibitors) have characteristic hematologic profiles: rituximab can cause delayed-onset neutropenia, and some agents may unmask cytopenias by altering cytokine balance.
- Drug-induced immune hemolytic anemia (DIHA) can occur with penicillins, cephalosporins, alpha-methyldopa, and rarely with immunomodulatory agents through hapten formation or autoimmune mechanisms. Warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia (AIHA) is immune-mediated and may be triggered or exacerbated by immune dysregulation after immunosuppression changes.
- Pure red cell aplasia (PRCA) is a rare complication of some agents (e.g., azathioprine in viral infections or parvovirus B19 reactivation).

Clinical presentation and diagnosis
Cytopenias may be asymptomatic and detected on routine labs or present with infections (neutropenia), fatigue and pallor (anemia), or bleeding/bruising (thrombocytopenia). Specific diagnostic steps:
- Complete blood count (CBC) with differential; examine trends over time.
- Peripheral blood smear to look for schistocytes (microangiopathic hemolysis), reticulocytes, dysplastic changes, or blasts.
- Reticulocyte count, lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), haptoglobin, indirect bilirubin, and direct antiglobulin (Coombs) test to evaluate for hemolysis/AIHA.
- Bone marrow biopsy when marrow failure, unexplained pancytopenia, or concern for marrow infiltration/dysplasia persists.

Normal reference ranges important for monitoring
- White blood cell (WBC) count: approximately 4.0–11.0 × 10^9/L
- Absolute neutrophil count (ANC): ≥1.8 × 10^9/L (neutropenia defined as ANC <1.5 × 10^9/L; severe neutropenia <0.5 × 10^9/L)
- Hemoglobin: males 13.5–17.5 g/dL, females 12.0–15.5 g/dL
- Platelets: 150–450 × 10^9/L

Monitoring schedules and action thresholds
Monitoring frequency should be tailored to the drug, dose, and patient factors:
- Azathioprine/6-MP: CBC at baseline; then weekly for the first month, every 2 weeks for months 2–3, and then monthly for months 4–6; thereafter every 1–3 months if stable. TPMT (thiopurine methyltransferase) genotype or phenotype should be checked before starting; TPMT deficiency mandates dose reduction or alternative therapy.
- Methotrexate: baseline CBC, then every 1–2 weeks for the first month or two, then every 4–8 weeks when stable.
- Mycophenolate mofetil: baseline CBC and then monthly for 3–6 months, then every 1–3 months.
- Cyclophosphamide: CBC weekly during induction pulses and for several weeks after; consider more frequent checks in high-dose regimens.

Action thresholds:
- ANC <1.0 × 10^9/L generally prompts drug hold and evaluation for infection; ANC <0.5 × 10^9/L (severe neutropenia) requires urgent hold of myelosuppressive drugs, infectious workup, and often hospital-level management, including granulocyte colony-stimulating factor (G-CSF) in severe or febrile neutropenia.
- Platelets <50 × 10^9/L usually warrants holding the drug and hematology consultation; <20–30 × 10^9/L is a threshold for urgent intervention to prevent bleeding.
- Hemoglobin drop greater than 2 g/dL or symptomatic anemia prompts investigation for hemolysis, marrow suppression, or bleeding.

Management principles
First steps are drug cessation or dose reduction and supportive care. For marrow suppression, consider G-CSF for neutropenia, transfusions for symptomatic anemia or severe thrombocytopenia, and hematology consultation for persistent pancytopenia or atypical features. For suspected DIHA or AIHA, stop the offending agent, confirm with Coombs testing, and treat with corticosteroids and supportive red cell transfusion as needed. Identify and treat coexisting infections (e.g., parvovirus B19 causing PRCA). Rechallenge or switching agents should consider severity of reaction, availability of alternatives, and the presence of predisposing genetic factors (e.g., TPMT testing).

# Hepatotoxicity and nephrotoxicity with immunosuppressants

Hepatic and renal toxicities are common complications of immunosuppressive therapy that can range from mild, transient enzyme elevations to fulminant liver failure or irreversible kidney injury. Understanding drug-specific mechanisms, monitoring protocols, and thresholds for intervention is critical to prevent permanent organ damage.

Hepatotoxicity
Drugs commonly associated with liver injury include methotrexate, azathioprine, leflunomide, high-dose corticosteroids (fatty liver), certain antibiotics used in combination, and some biologics. Mechanisms include direct hepatocellular toxicity, cholestatic injury, immune-mediated hepatitis, and reactivation of viral hepatitis (hepatitis B, less commonly hepatitis C). Some agents (e.g., methotrexate) can cause cumulative, dose-related hepatic fibrosis and cirrhosis; others (e.g., isoniazid, sulfonamides, azathioprine) are associated with idiosyncratic or immune-mediated reactions.

Monitoring and normal ranges
- Baseline ALT and AST, alkaline phosphatase, total bilirubin, and albumin before initiating therapy.
- Normal reference ranges: ALT (approximately 7–56 U/L), AST (approximately 10–40 U/L), alkaline phosphatase 44–147 U/L, total bilirubin 0.1–1.2 mg/dL (laboratory-specific).
- For methotrexate: baseline LFTs, then every 1–2 weeks during dose escalation and every 1–3 months when stable. Consider baseline hepatic imaging or fibrosis assessment when risk factors (alcohol use, obesity, diabetes) exist.
- For azathioprine and leflunomide: baseline and periodic (e.g., monthly for first 3 months, then periodically) LFTs.

Clinical decision points
- Mild transaminase elevations (<2–3 × upper limit of normal, ULN) without symptoms may be observed with closer monitoring.
- Persistent ALT/AST elevations >3 × ULN usually require holding or reducing the drug and investigating other causes (viral hepatitis reactivation, alcohol, nonalcoholic fatty liver disease, other medications).
- Symptomatic hepatitis, jaundice, or progressive enzyme rise demands immediate cessation and hepatology consultation.

Special considerations: viral hepatitis
- Screen for hepatitis B (HBsAg, anti-HBc, anti-HBs) and hepatitis C (anti-HCV and confirmatory RNA when positive) before initiating immunosuppression. Immunosuppression (particularly B-cell depleting therapy and steroids) can reactivate resolved or occult HBV; prophylactic antiviral therapy (e.g., entecavir, tenofovir) for HBsAg-positive or anti-HBc–positive patients per hepatology guidance reduces risk.
- Monitor HBV DNA periodically in at-risk patients.

Nephrotoxicity
Nephrotoxicity may result from direct tubular injury, hemodynamic changes, crystal nephropathy, interstitial nephritis, thrombotic microangiopathy, or glomerular disease. Agents with notable renal effects:
- Calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus): cause dose-dependent vasoconstriction of afferent arterioles, reducing renal blood flow and glomerular filtration rate (GFR); chronic use causes arteriolar hyalinosis and chronic kidney disease. Toxicity is closely related to blood concentration and drug interactions.
- Cyclophosphamide: can cause hemorrhagic cystitis and, rarely, acute and chronic kidney injury.
- IV immunoglobulin (IVIG): may precipitate osmotic nephrosis or acute kidney injury in patients with underlying renal impairment, especially formulations with sucrose stabilizer.
- Drug-induced interstitial nephritis: beta-lactams, NSAIDs, proton pump inhibitors, certain biologics may cause immune-mediated tubulointerstitial nephritis.

Monitoring and thresholds
- Baseline serum creatinine and eGFR prior to initiating nephrotoxic agents; urinalysis to detect hematuria or proteinuria.
- Routine monitoring frequency depends on agent: for CNIs, check serum creatinine and drug trough levels frequently (e.g., twice weekly early, then every 1–3 months when stable); for cyclophosphamide, monitor renal function and urinalysis before each cycle.
- Normal serum creatinine: roughly 0.6–1.3 mg/dL (age- and sex-dependent); interpret relative changes from baseline and use eGFR.
- An increase in serum creatinine >30% from baseline or an absolute rise above expected for the patient should prompt dose adjustment and evaluation for other causes (volume depletion, obstruction, drug interaction).

Management
- For CNI nephrotoxicity: reduce dose or consider switching to non-nephrotoxic alternatives; correct contributing factors (volume status, blood pressure, drug interactions) and monitor levels. In suspected thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA) with hemolysis and schistocytes, stop the agent and pursue urgent hematology evaluation.
- For methotrexate-related renal dysfunction: discontinue drug if creatinine rises substantially; methotrexate is renally excreted and accumulation increases toxicity.
- For cyclophosphamide: aggressive hydration and mesna (in certain regimens) reduce hemorrhagic cystitis risk; evaluate for urinary RBCs and hematuria between doses.

# Infection risk and opportunistic infections (presentation and management)

Immunosuppressive therapies increase susceptibility to infections both by diminishing host immune defenses and by altering the balance of microbial flora. The degree and pattern of risk vary with the agent, dose, duration, combination therapy, and patient comorbidities. Clinicians must combine an understanding of typical presentations, targeted screening, prophylaxis, and early empiric therapy to reduce morbidity and mortality.

General principles and risk stratification
- Glucocorticoids at moderate-to-high doses, especially prolonged prednisone-equivalent ≥20 mg/day, impair neutrophil function, macrophage activation, and cell-mediated immunity, increasing risk for bacterial infections and opportunistic pathogens.
- Antimetabolites and alkylators suppress lymphoid and myeloid compartments, increasing risk of neutropenic infections (bacterial and fungal).
- Calcineurin inhibitors impair T-cell function and increase risk of viral and certain fungal infections.
- B-cell depleting agents (rituximab) predispose to hypogammaglobulinemia and recurrent sinopulmonary infections, hepatitis B reactivation, and rarely progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML) associated with JC virus.
- JAK inhibitors and some biologics targeting cytokines may increase susceptibility to herpes zoster, tuberculosis reactivation, and other intracellular pathogens.

Common and opportunistic infections to consider
- Typical bacterial infections: skin/soft tissue, respiratory tract, urinary tract; presentation may be blunted (e.g., minimal fever).
- Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP/PCP): risk increased with prolonged high-dose steroids and combination immunosuppression. Presentation includes progressive dyspnea, nonproductive cough, hypoxemia with diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates on chest imaging. Diagnosis by induced sputum or bronchoalveolar lavage with PCR or immunofluorescence; elevated serum beta-D-glucan supports the diagnosis. First-line therapy: trimethoprim–sulfamethoxazole (TMP–SMX) high-dose for 21 days (with adjunctive corticosteroids for moderate–severe hypoxemia). Prophylaxis: TMP–SMX (single-strength daily or three times weekly) for patients at moderate-to-high risk.
- Cytomegalovirus (CMV): disease may present with fever, cytopenias, hepatitis, colitis, pneumonitis. Diagnosis by quantitative PCR (CMV DNAemia) and tissue biopsy for organ disease. Treatment: ganciclovir or valganciclovir; consider prophylaxis in high-risk settings.
- Fungal infections: invasive candidiasis and aspergillosis in profoundly immunosuppressed patients; diagnosis via culture, galactomannan, and imaging; treat with echinocandins or mold-active azoles depending on organism.
- Nocardia: subacute pulmonary or disseminated infection with brain abscesses in patients on prolonged steroids or calcineurin inhibitors; diagnosis via culture and molecular methods; treat with TMP–SMX (often prolonged therapy).
- Herpesviridae: varicella zoster virus (VZV) reactivation (shingles) is common; prophylactic antiviral therapy (acyclovir/valacyclovir) is considered in some high-risk regimens.
- Tuberculosis: reactivation risk with TNF inhibitors and some other agents. Screen with interferon-gamma release assay (IGRA) or tuberculin skin test and treat latent TB before starting high-risk agents.

Screening and prophylactic measures
- Vaccination: administer appropriate inactivated vaccines before initiating immunosuppression when possible. Live vaccines are generally contraindicated during significant immunosuppression; any live vaccines should be given at least 4 weeks before starting therapy when feasible.
- TB screening (IGRA or TST) prior to biologics/CNI initiation for high-risk agents.
- Hepatitis B screening (HBsAg, anti-HBc) especially before B-cell depleting therapies. Offer antiviral prophylaxis for patients at risk.
- PJP prophylaxis with TMP–SMX for patients receiving prolonged high-dose corticosteroids or combination immunosuppression; alternatives (dapsone, atovaquone, pentamidine) for those intolerant to TMP–SMX.
- Consider antifungal prophylaxis in profoundly neutropenic patients or those with prior invasive fungal infection.

Recognition and early management of infection
- Maintain a high index of suspicion: immunosuppressed patients may present atypically with subtle signs (e.g., absence of fever).
- Rapid diagnostic testing: blood cultures, imaging (chest radiograph or CT), viral PCRs (CMV, HSV), antigen assays (galactomannan, beta-D-glucan), and site-specific cultures/biopsies.
- Early empiric broad-spectrum antibiotics in febrile neutropenia, following institutional guidelines; for severe sepsis, treat based on likely pathogens and local antibiograms.
- For opportunistic infections, initiate pathogen-specific therapy promptly once suspected; consider infectious disease consultation for complex cases or treatment of CMV, fungal infections, or CNS involvement.
- Adjust or temporarily halt immunosuppressive therapy in the setting of severe infection; decisions must balance infection control with the risk of disease flare.

# Drug–drug interactions and peri-procedural considerations

Understanding pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic interactions is essential for safe immunosuppressive therapy. Many immunosuppressive agents are metabolized by cytochrome P450 enzymes, transported by P-glycoprotein, or interact with metabolic pathways (e.g., TPMT) thereby producing clinically significant interactions with commonly used drugs.

Key interaction mechanisms and examples
- Cytochrome P450 (CYP3A4) inhibitors (azole antifungals, macrolides like clarithromycin, some calcium channel blockers, protease inhibitors) increase serum levels of calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus) and mTOR inhibitors (sirolimus), raising risk of nephrotoxicity, neurotoxicity, and other adverse effects. Conversely, CYP3A4 inducers (rifampin, carbamazepine, phenytoin, St. John’s wort) decrease levels and risk graft/disease activity.
- TPMT deficiency increases active thiopurine metabolites and risk of myelosuppression with azathioprine/6-MP. Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase and shifts 6-MP metabolism toward active metabolites, requiring significant azathioprine dose reductions (often to one-third to one-quarter) if coadministration is necessary; avoid unless carefully managed.
- Methotrexate interactions: NSAIDs, penicillins, sulfonamides, and trimethoprim can decrease renal clearance of methotrexate and increase toxicity. Proton pump inhibitors can modestly affect methotrexate elimination. Concurrent use of high-dose NSAIDs and methotrexate increases risk of mucositis, myelosuppression, and renal injury.
- Statins with CNIs increase statin levels and risk of myopathy/rhabdomyolysis; select statins with less interaction (e.g., pravastatin) or reduce the dose and monitor CK.
- Live vaccines with immunosuppression: live-attenuated vaccines (e.g., MMR, varicella, live zoster) are contraindicated in patients with significant immunosuppression. Inactivated vaccines may have reduced efficacy; consider timing before therapy initiation to maximize immunogenicity.

Peri-procedural management
- Vaccination timing: ideally, administer recommended vaccines at least 2–4 weeks before starting immunosuppression (longer for live vaccines, generally ≥4 weeks). For patients already on immunosuppression, give inactivated vaccines when possible; response may be suboptimal.
- Elective surgery: consider holding certain immunosuppressants perioperatively to reduce infectious risk and promote healing. There is no universal rule, but common approaches:
  - Methotrexate: many rheumatology guidelines suggest continuing low-dose methotrexate perioperatively is safe and may reduce risk of disease flare, but decisions should be individualized.
  - Azathioprine/mycophenolate: often continued for moderate procedures but may be held for major surgeries if infection risk is high.
  - Biologics: holding one dosing cycle before planned surgery (often 1–2 times the dosing interval) is common, resuming after wound healing and absence of infection; coordinate with surgical and specialty teams.
  - High-dose steroids: avoid abrupt withdrawal; use stress-dose steroids perioperatively if chronic steroid exposure >3 weeks to prevent adrenal insufficiency.
- Antibiotic prophylaxis: follow surgical prophylaxis guidelines; may need to broaden coverage for specific infections in profoundly immunosuppressed patients.
- Periprocedural antiviral prophylaxis when indicated (e.g., herpes zoster prevention) for high-risk patients undergoing certain immunosuppressive regimens.

Clinical decision points in drug interactions
- Routinely review medication lists for CYP interactions, overlapping toxicities (e.g., myelosuppression, hepatotoxicity), and cumulative immunosuppression when adding new agents.
- Monitor therapeutic drug levels when available (tacrolimus, cyclosporine, sirolimus) and adjust dosing when interacting drugs are started or stopped.
- Use drug interaction resources and pharmacy consultation for complex polypharmacy scenarios.

# Infusion-related reactions (IVIG, rituximab) and premedication strategies

IV-administered immunomodulatory therapies can cause infusion-related adverse events ranging from mild headache and flushing to severe anaphylaxis, aseptic meningitis, acute renal failure, thromboembolism, or cytokine-release syndrome. Preparation, prevention, and early recognition are key.

IVIG infusion reactions
- Mechanisms and common reactions: reactions may be due to rate-dependent cytokine release, complement activation, osmotic effects of stabilizers (sucrose-containing preparations increase risk of renal injury), and preexisting anti-IgA antibodies in IgA-deficient recipients causing anaphylaxis.
- Typical presentations: headaches (sometimes severe), flushing, fever, chills, myalgias, nausea; less commonly aseptic meningitis, acute kidney injury, hemolytic anemia (IVIG can induce hemolysis via passive transfer of anti-A/B isoagglutinins), and thrombotic events.
- Risk factors for serious complications: renal insufficiency, high infusion rate, older age, vascular risk factors, sucrose-stabilized IVIG formulations, and known IgA deficiency with anti-IgA antibodies.
- Prevention and premedication: baseline assessment of renal function (serum creatinine, eGFR) and volume status. Use low-osmolarity, non-sucrose preparations for patients at risk. Premedication commonly includes acetaminophen and an antihistamine (H1 blocker); consider low-dose corticosteroids if prior reactions occurred. Start at low infusion rates and titrate slowly while monitoring vital signs.
- Management of reactions: pause infusion for moderate reactions, provide symptomatic treatment (antipyretics, antihistamines), and consider reinitiating at lower rate if benefits outweigh risks. For severe reactions (anaphylaxis), stop the infusion, administer intramuscular or intravenous epinephrine, secure airway, give fluid resuscitation, and seek urgent critical care.

Rituximab and other monoclonal antibody infusions
- Mechanisms: infusion-related reactions often result from cytokine release after target antigen engagement (e.g., CD20 B-cell lysis with rituximab) and IgE-mediated hypersensitivity in some patients.
- Typical presentation: fever, rigors, hypotension or hypertension, bronchospasm, rash, angioedema, and in severe cases cytokine-release syndrome with multi-organ dysfunction.
- Timing: most reactions occur during the first infusion and are less common and typically milder with subsequent infusions.
- Risk factors: high circulating tumor or targeted-cell burden (in oncology), first infusion, rapid infusion rate, and history of allergic reactions.
- Premedication strategies: standard premedication for rituximab typically includes acetaminophen, an H1-antagonist (e.g., diphenhydramine), and sometimes corticosteroids (e.g., methylprednisolone 100 mg IV) particularly when treating autoimmune disease or with prior reactions. For certain agents, H2 blockers and bronchodilators may be added.
- Infusion rate protocols: start at a slow rate per product monograph and gradually increase if no reactions occur. Observe patients closely during the early period of infusion.
- Management of reactions: mild to moderate reactions warrant temporary cessation, symptomatic therapy (antihistamines, bronchodilators, corticosteroids), and consideration of resuming at a lower rate. For severe anaphylaxis or life-threatening events, stop infusion permanently, treat per advanced cardiac life support protocols, and avoid re-exposure to the same agent unless there is a compelling reason and a desensitization protocol is available.

Additional considerations
- For patients with known IgA deficiency scheduled for IVIG, test for anti-IgA antibodies; use IgA-depleted products or alternative therapies if at risk of anaphylactic reactions.
- Thromboembolic risk with IVIG: minimize risk by ensuring adequate hydration, using the lowest effective dose and infusion rate, and addressing vascular risk factors.
- Aseptic meningitis post-IVIG: presents with severe headache, neck stiffness, photophobia; manage supportively and consider alternative therapies for future dosing.

Conclusion

Managing complications of immunosuppressive therapy requires anticipatory care: baseline risk assessment, targeted screening (e.g., TPMT, hepatitis, TB), individualized monitoring for hematologic, hepatic, and renal toxicities, and vigilant surveillance for infections. Awareness of pharmacologic interactions and peri-procedural strategies prevents many adverse outcomes, and appropriate premedication and infusion protocols reduce infusion-related morbidity. When complications occur, prompt diagnosis, cessation or adjustment of the offending agent, supportive care, and specialist consultation (hematology, infectious disease, nephrology, hepatology) form the foundation of safe management while preserving disease control.

Introduction

Autoimmune inflammatory myopathies — including dermatomyositis, polymyositis, immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy, and antisynthetase syndrome — are systemic diseases in which laboratory testing plays a central role in diagnosis, monitoring, and differential diagnosis. Laboratory data must always be interpreted in clinical context: symptom timing (subacute vs chronic), pattern of weakness, extra-muscular features (skin findings, interstitial lung disease, arthritis), medication history (statins, immune checkpoint inhibitors), and comorbidities (alcoholic liver disease, chronic viral hepatitis) materially change the pre-test probability for different diagnoses and the likely source of abnormal laboratory values.

This chapter reviews interpretation of common serologic and biochemical tests encountered when evaluating suspected inflammatory myopathies. Emphasis is on pathophysiology underlying test abnormalities, strengths and limitations of individual assays, and practical clinical decision points — for example, how to distinguish muscle from liver sources of transaminase elevations, when to pursue extended myositis autoantibody panels, or how to approach complement and C1 inhibitor testing in a patient with angioedema.

# Utility and limitations of ANA testing and ANA patterns

Antinuclear antibody (ANA) testing is one of the most frequently ordered immunologic screens in patients with suspected systemic autoimmune disease. The traditional and still widely recommended screening method is indirect immunofluorescence (IIF) on HEp-2 cells. The test reports both a titer and a staining pattern (homogeneous, speckled, nucleolar, centromere, cytoplasmic, etc.), each providing clues to likely autoantibody specificities and disease associations.

Pathophysiologic basis and patterns
HEp-2 IIF detects antibodies that bind nuclear or cytoplasmic components of cultured human epithelial cells. Different autoantibodies produce characteristic patterns because they target distinct subcellular structures: anti-dsDNA and anti-histone often give homogeneous nuclear staining, anti-Sm and anti-RNP produce speckled nuclear staining, anti-centromere produces discrete speckled centromeric dots, and antibodies to some myositis-associated antigens (for example, Jo-1/anti-histidyl tRNA synthetase) may produce cytoplasmic staining. Nucleolar patterns point toward scleroderma-spectrum disease. Recognizing these patterns helps tailor reflex testing for specific antigen assays.

Sensitivity and specificity for myositis
ANA by HEp-2 is positive in a majority of connective tissue diseases but is less sensitive for some myositis-specific antibodies (MSAs). For example, classic dermatomyositis patients often are ANA-positive, but certain myositis phenotypes (e.g., anti-synthetase syndrome) may have cytoplasmic staining that is not coded as a positive "ANA" by some laboratories that report only nuclear positivity. Thus, a “negative ANA” does not exclude autoimmune myopathy, especially if the patient has high pretest probability. Some myositis-specific antibodies (anti-Mi-2, anti-TIF1-γ, anti-MDA5, anti-SRP, anti-HMGCR, anti-PL-7/PL-12) may not produce a recognizable nuclear pattern or may yield low-level cytoplasmic staining interpreted variably between labs.

Titer and clinical meaning
ANA titer relates to pre-test probability. Low-level positivity (e.g., 1:40 or 1:80) may be seen in healthy persons, particularly elderly individuals, and in a range of non-autoimmune conditions. Many labs consider titers ≥1:160 more meaningful in adults; however, interpretation depends on the laboratory’s reference population and the clinical context. High titers (e.g., ≥1:320) increase the likelihood of an autoimmune disease but are not specific for any one condition.

Limitations and pitfalls
Inter-assay variability: HEp-2 slide substrates vary across manufacturers. Automated image-analysis systems can change sensitivity and pattern recognition. Cytoplasmic staining is not uniformly reported as a positive ANA, and the label “ANA-negative” can falsely reassure clinicians when cytoplasmic MSAs are present.

Cross-reactivity and non-disease positivity: Infection, medications, and malignancy can yield positive ANAs. Patients on immune checkpoint inhibitors or with chronic viral infections may develop ANAs without classic systemic autoimmune disease.

Reflex testing and the need for targeted panels
Given the limitations above, ANA screening should be followed selectively by targeted antigen testing guided by clinical phenotype and staining pattern. For suspected myopathy, request myositis-specific and myositis-associated antibody panels even if the ANA is negative or shows cytoplasmic staining. When an ANA shows a nucleolar pattern in a myositis patient, consider scleroderma overlap testing. Ultimately, clinical judgment should determine reflex testing; a negative screening ANA does not obviate extended myositis testing if symptoms strongly suggest autoimmune myopathy.

# Interpreting CK, AST, ALT, LDH in the context of muscle vs hepatic injury

Serum enzymes — creatine kinase (CK), aspartate aminotransferase (AST), alanine aminotransferase (ALT), and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) — are frequently abnormal in inflammatory myopathies. Distinguishing muscle-derived from liver-derived elevations is essential for appropriate diagnosis.

Creatine kinase (CK)
Biology and kinetics: CK catalyzes conversion of creatine to phosphocreatine and has isoenzymes CK-MM (skeletal muscle), CK-MB (cardiac muscle), and CK-BB (brain). In inflammatory myopathies, CK-MM is the principal isoenzyme released. CK rises within 12–24 hours after muscle injury, peaks by 24–72 hours, and declines over days once injury subsides; CK half-life is roughly 36 hours. Magnitude of elevation correlates with extent of muscle fiber necrosis/inflammation in many cases, but not perfectly: dermatomyositis can have relatively modest CK despite severe weakness, while necrotizing myopathy and polymyositis often have very high CKs.

Typical reference ranges (laboratory-dependent): CK varies by sex and muscle mass. Typical adult upper limits are approximately 40–200 U/L; many labs use 170–200 U/L for men and 120–170 U/L for women. For a given patient, trend is more informative than a single value.

Clinical interpretation: CK elevations greater than 10 times the upper limit of normal (ULN) are strongly suggestive of active myocyte injury (common in inflammatory myopathies and toxic myopathies). Mild CK elevations (1–3× ULN) can be seen with statin use, hypothyroidism, chronic myopathies, and in healthy people after exercise. In suspected autoimmune myopathy, CK is useful for baseline disease activity and for monitoring response to immunotherapy. Be aware that normal CK does not exclude myositis — especially in older adults, in some dermatomyositis phenotypes, and when disease is confined to bulbar or respiratory musculature.

AST and ALT
Tissue distribution and overlap: AST is present in liver, cardiac muscle, skeletal muscle, red blood cells, kidney, and brain; ALT is more liver-predominant but also present in skeletal muscle to a lesser extent. Because of this overlap, AST and ALT can be elevated from muscle injury as well as hepatic injury.

Relative patterns and clues to source: Several practical approaches help distinguish muscle from liver source:

- Examine CK in parallel. If AST and ALT are elevated with a marked CK elevation, muscle is a likely source. For example, an AST of 150 U/L, ALT of 100 U/L, and CK of 10,000 U/L suggest muscle necrosis rather than primary hepatocellular injury.

- AST:ALT ratio: In pure muscle injury, AST often rises more than ALT because AST is more abundant in muscle. An AST:ALT ratio >1 (often substantially greater) suggests muscle contribution, whereas an isolated ALT predominance tends toward hepatic source. This is not definitive.

- Other hepatitis indicators: Synthetic liver function (albumin, INR), bilirubin (total and direct), and liver imaging help. In hepatic disease, alkaline phosphatase and gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT) may be abnormal, and there may be hyperbilirubinemia and coagulopathy if severe.

- Clinical context: Jaundice, hepatomegaly, stigmata of chronic liver disease, exposure history (hepatotoxic drugs, viral hepatitis risk) point to liver disease. Muscle pain, weakness, myalgias, and a high CK support muscle origin.

LDH
LDH is a ubiquitous enzyme with five isoenzymes that originate from different tissues (LDH-5 predominates in liver and skeletal muscle). LDH is less specific than CK for muscle injury but rises in many tissue-damaging processes including muscle, hemolysis, and hepatic injury. In the context of a markedly elevated CK, a concurrent LDH elevation generally reflects muscle injury.

Practical algorithm
When transaminases are elevated in a patient with weakness:

1. Order CK, AST, ALT, bilirubin, INR, alkaline phosphatase, GGT.
2. If CK is markedly elevated (e.g., >5–10× ULN) and transaminase elevations are modest, suspect muscle source.
3. If bilirubin, INR, or GGT/ALP are abnormal, consider hepatic contribution.
4. In ambiguous cases, fractionated CK (isoenzyme), LDH isoenzymes, or muscle imaging/biopsy can help. Viral hepatitis serologies and abdominal imaging are indicated if hepatic etiology is suspected.

Because many patients with inflammatory myopathies are on statins and other potentially hepatotoxic drugs, it is not uncommon to see simultaneous muscle and liver insults. Always reassess medication list and consider combined pathology.

# Acute phase reactants (CRP, ESR) and their meaning in inflammatory myopathies

C-reactive protein (CRP) and erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) are nonspecific markers of systemic inflammation. They provide complementary information but have different kinetics and determinants.

Biology and kinetics
CRP is a hepatically synthesized acute-phase protein whose production is driven mainly by interleukin-6 (IL-6). It rises rapidly (within 6–8 hours) after an inflammatory stimulus, peaks by 48 hours, and declines quickly with resolution. High-sensitivity CRP assays measure low-level inflammation but standard CRP assays are used for clinical rheumatologic monitoring.

ESR measures the rate at which erythrocytes sediment in a column of anticoagulated blood over one hour; it reflects plasma fibrinogen, immunoglobulin levels, and red cell properties. ESR rises more slowly than CRP and falls more slowly with resolution.

Reference ranges and magnitude
Typical reference ranges: CRP <10 mg/L (some labs use <5 mg/L). ESR is age- and sex-dependent; a common rule of thumb is: males: upper limit = age/2 (mm/hr); females: upper limit = (age + 10)/2 (mm/hr). For example, ESR upper limit in a 40-year-old woman ≈ 25 mm/hr. Marked elevations (ESR >100 mm/hr or CRP >100 mg/L) suggest severe inflammatory or infectious processes.

Interpretation in myopathies
